CAMLPR Transfusion Medicine Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Test his cells with anti-H lectin and run a panel, because a high-prevalence antibody is suspected): A positive screen with a negative autocontrol and incompatible crossmatches with every ABO group points to an alloantibody against a high-prevalence antigen, such as anti-H. Anti-H lectin on his cells and a panel to identify the specificity come before any other testing. A new ABO group does not explain a reaction with all screening cells, an elution is for a positive autocontrol, and AB units carry A and B antigens, so they do not avoid a high-prevalence antibody.
  2. The correct answer is A (Acute hemolytic reaction, because incompatible red cells are destroyed quickly and release hemoglobin into plasma): Flank pain, chills, hemoglobinuria and hypotension within minutes of starting a unit indicate acute intravascular hemolysis, usually from ABO incompatibility. Febrile reactions lack red urine and hypotension, circulatory overload shows dyspnea, and delayed reactions appear days later.
  3. The correct answer is B (Issue the pre-storage leukoreduced unit and record that CMV safety was met by leukoreduction): A component is CMV-safe when it is leukoreduced or comes from a CMV seronegative donor, and pre-storage leukoreduction meets that definition. The record must state the real method, so labelling the unit seronegative would be false. A donor who feels well says nothing about CMV status, and refusing to issue ignores a unit that meets the order.
  4. The correct answer is B (ABO incompatibility between patient plasma and donor cells, because anti-A or anti-B reacts immediately): Immediate spin is a saline, room temperature test that detects IgM reactions, so it finds ABO incompatibility from anti-A or anti-B. IgG antibodies to Kidd or Rh antigens need incubation and the antiglobulin phase. Bacterial clumping is not what this check is designed to find.
  5. The correct answer is C (Treat the screen as invalid and repeat, because check cells show the AHG step failed): IgG check cells must agglutinate in every negative antiglobulin tube. When they do not, the antiglobulin reagent was neutralized or lost, usually from poor washing or a reagent fault, so the negative result is invalid. Adding reagent, lengthening incubation or reporting the screen as negative does not fix the failure; the test is repeated after correcting the cause.
  6. The correct answer is D (Wash again until the supernatant is clear, because free serum proteins can interfere with testing): Residual plasma proteins can interfere with reactions, so cells should be washed until the supernatant is clear before the suspension is standardized. Diluting or adding protein leaves the interfering material in the tube. The wash count in a procedure is a minimum, not a reason to stop when the supernatant is still not clear.
  7. The correct answer is C (Secure the printout and notify the privacy officer, because a possible breach must be documented): Unattended identifiable results are a possible privacy breach, so the first step is to take control of the paper and report it so the privacy officer can assess and document what happened. Quietly destroying it hides the incident, and leaving it exposes more people to the information. Telling the patient is the privacy officer's decision after the facts are known.
  8. The correct answer is A (A light suspension balances antigen and antibody amounts so weak agglutination is not masked): Whole blood carries far more antigen than a standard drop of reagent antibody can bind, and antigen excess hides weak agglutination. A 3 to 5 percent suspension keeps the antigen to antibody ratio balanced so even weak reactions form visible clumps. Dilution does not remove plasma proteins, and colour is not the reason for the specified concentration.
  9. The correct answer is D (Group O red cells, because they carry no A or B antigens for recipient antibodies): Without a known group, red cells must be compatible with whatever ABO antibodies the recipient has. Group O cells have neither A nor B antigen, so recipient anti-A and anti-B cannot attack them. Group A, B or AB cells carry antigens that could be destroyed in a patient of another group.
  10. The correct answer is A (37 C for 30 to 60 minutes, because IgG binds best at body temperature): Clinically significant IgG antibodies react best at 37 C, and a saline tube without enhancement needs 30 to 60 minutes for adequate binding. Cold or room temperature conditions favour IgM antibodies instead. A temperature of 56 C would damage red cells and antibody.
  11. The correct answer is B (Use the validated tube method for this crossmatch and finish gel training before using cards): Competence is method specific, so a technologist may perform only the procedures for which competency is documented. The validated tube method keeps the urgent patient safe while gel training proceeds. Package inserts do not replace assessment, and delaying or waiving training either harms the patient or breaks the quality system.
  12. The correct answer is D (Quarantine both units and notify the medical director, because the recipient must be informed): A confirmed positive donor result means any remaining components must be removed from use immediately, and the medical director has to start the look-back so the transfused recipient is identified, informed and tested. Using the units, delaying for repeat confirmation or approaching the recipient directly all bypass the defined notification chain.
  13. The correct answer is D (Donor lymphocytes needed several days to expand and secrete enough anti-A to coat recipient cells): Passenger lymphocytes in the graft are a small donor population that must expand before they secrete enough IgG anti-A to coat recipient red cells, so hemolysis usually appears five to fifteen days after surgery. The DAT is positive and the eluate reacts with the recipient group. Recipient anti-B cannot damage group O cells.
  14. The correct answer is C (Three antigen-positive cells must react and three antigen-negative cells must not, at the same phase): The 3 and 3 rule asks for three cells that carry the antigen and react, and three cells that lack the antigen and do not react, at the same test phase. This gives a probability of 0.05 or less that the pattern arose by chance. It does not depend on homozygosity or on reaction strength.
  15. The correct answer is C (Discard the saline, refill the bottle with fresh saline and repeat the affected tests): Cloudy saline suggests microbial or particulate contamination that can interfere with reactions, so the bottle is replaced and the affected tests are repeated. Filtering or limiting its use does not make it clean, and an extra wash cannot be relied on to remove interference after the fact.
  16. The correct answer is D (Accept it back into inventory after checking the seal, indicator and time, as policy permits): The unit meets every condition in the policy: under 30 minutes out, intact seal, and an indicator in range, so it may be placed back into stock with the time documented. A blanket discard rule or a waiting period is not part of the stated policy.
  17. The correct answer is B (Ask a senior colleague for guidance, because the case exceeds the technologist's current training): Competence means recognizing the limits of one's own training and obtaining help when a task goes beyond them. A written procedure does not replace supervised practice for a complex technique. Issuing blood without investigation or hiding the difficulty could harm the patient.
  18. The correct answer is C (Cryogenic gloves, a face shield and a lab coat, because splashes cause cold burns): Liquid nitrogen boils at minus 196 C, so splashes cause severe cold burns and can damage eyes. Loose fitting insulated cryogenic gloves, a full face shield and a closed lab coat protect against splash and allow quick removal. Examination gloves and cotton do not insulate, a mask does not protect the eyes, and bare skin freezes on contact.
  19. The correct answer is D (The gel acts as a sieve that traps agglutinates but lets single cells pass through): The gel matrix in each microtube works as a filter. During centrifugation, agglutinated cells are too large to pass through and stay at or near the top, while unagglutinated cells pass down to form a button at the bottom. The antiglobulin reagent in the gel produces the agglutination, but it is the sieving action that separates the two populations.
  20. The correct answer is D (Two vials, because 1.3 rounds to one vial and the extra vial is added): Multiplying 0.8 by 50 gives 40, and dividing by 30 gives 1.3 vials. Rounding to the nearest whole number gives 1, and the extra vial in the laboratory rule makes 2. Rounding up to 2 first would give 3 and overdose the mother under this stated rule.
  21. The correct answer is C (Donor T lymphocytes engraft and attack recipient tissues, because the recipient cannot eliminate them): In transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease, viable donor T lymphocytes engraft in a recipient who cannot reject them and then attack skin, liver, gut and marrow. Hemolysis from ABO antibodies, TRALI from donor leukocyte antibodies and recipient rejection of platelets are different mechanisms with different clinical pictures.
  22. The correct answer is A (Complete orientation and supervised practice, then an assessment, because procedures change during long absences): A valid registration does not show current skill after a long break. Orientation to new procedures, supervised practice and an assessment demonstrate competence and protect patients. Reading the manual alone is not demonstrated competence, and beginning with the busiest shift raises error risk.
  23. The correct answer is A (5400 x10^9/L, calculated from 2.7 x10^11 platelets in a final 0.050 L): A 10 percent loss leaves 2.7 x10^11 platelets. Dividing by the final volume of 0.050 L gives 5.4 x10^12 per litre, which is 5400 x10^9/L. Using the starting count ignores the loss, and using the wrong volume shifts the result by a factor of ten or five.
Case CHB1

A 78-year-old resident of a long-term care home has a pretransfusion sample tested by tube method. The forward group is A Rh positive. In the reverse group, A1 cells show no agglutination and B cells show only 1+ agglutination. She has chronic lymphocytic leukemia and a low serum immunoglobulin level. A repeat sample gives the same results.

  1. The correct answer is C (Hypogammaglobulinemia from the leukemia has lowered her anti-B, so the reverse group is weaker than expected): Patients with chronic lymphocytic leukemia and low serum immunoglobulin often make too little anti-A or anti-B, so reverse reactions become weak or absent even though the forward group is clear. An acquired B antigen would change the forward group, not weaken the reverse group. A cold alloantibody would not specifically weaken the reaction of the reagent B cells.
  2. The correct answer is D (Incubate the reverse group longer at 4 C, because weak antibody reacts better in the cold): Weak or missing reverse reactions are often due to low antibody levels, and longer incubation at room temperature or 4 C strengthens them. The ABO group should not be reported until the reverse group agrees with the forward group or the discrepancy is explained. Adsorption and subgroup testing address forward group problems, which her clear A reaction does not suggest.
  3. The correct answer is C (Group O red cells, because they lack A and B antigens and suit any recipient plasma): Group O red cells have no A or B antigen, so they cannot be attacked by whatever anti-A or anti-B her plasma contains, which is why policy chooses them during an unresolved discrepancy. Group A, AB and B units depend on knowing her true ABO group. Group AB red cells carry both antigens and are not universal for red cell transfusion.
  4. The correct answer is A (Yes, fever up to 4 hours after the unit finishes can qualify, so report it): Reactions can occur during the transfusion or up to 4 hours after it ends, so a new fever with chills two hours later in a previously afebrile patient should be reported and investigated. A negative DAT and agreeing ABO groups argue against hemolysis but do not exclude a febrile non-hemolytic reaction. A discarded bag limits culture but does not remove the duty to investigate.
Case CHB2

A 71-year-old patient with a recorded group O Rh positive type begins a red cell transfusion on a surgical ward. Within 15 minutes the patient has fever, flank pain and red urine. The nurse stops the transfusion. The unit label reads group A Rh positive. The post-reaction plasma is visibly pink and the pre-transfusion plasma was clear. The DAT on the post-reaction sample is positive with mixed-field agglutination.

  1. The correct answer is B (Transfusion of ABO-incompatible red cells, because patient anti-A destroyed the donor cells and released hemoglobin): The patient's group O plasma contains anti-A, which binds the group A donor cells and activates complement, causing intravascular hemolysis within minutes, as the pink plasma, red urine and flank pain show. Bacterial contamination typically produces rigors with positive cultures rather than a labelled group mismatch, and a delayed reaction would occur days later, not during the infusion.
  2. The correct answer is B (Repeat the clerical check, ABO and DAT on both samples, because identity errors cause hemolysis): Errors in identification are the commonest cause of severe acute hemolytic reactions, so the clerical check of patient, unit and records comes first, with repeat ABO and DAT on the pre and post samples. Issuing more blood or restarting the unit would expose the patient to further harm, and a unit involved in a suspected reaction is not returned to inventory.
  3. The correct answer is A (Alert the ward to recheck that patient's unit, because the swap may involve both patients): Because the labelled unit was meant for another patient, an identification swap may have occurred at the bedside, so the second patient may have received the wrong unit or be about to. Alerting the ward promptly lets staff stop and verify identity before any harm. Group O red cells are ABO compatible, but an identification failure still needs immediate investigation, and cancelling everything or retyping the unit alone does not address the swap.
  4. The correct answer is D (Treat the reaction as unreliable and repeat tube 7 with clean supplies first): Carryover from donor cells on the shared tip could explain a lone weak reaction, so the result cannot be trusted. The tube is repeated with fresh tips and clean tubes, and only that repeat is interpreted. Reporting a result from a compromised test in either direction risks a wrong decision.
  5. The correct answer is D (One vial covers about 15 mL of fetal red cells, or 30 mL of whole blood): A standard 300 microgram vial neutralizes about 15 mL of fetal red cells, which corresponds to about 30 mL of fetal whole blood. This is why the bleed volume is divided by 30 to estimate vials. The other values overstate or understate coverage and would lead to wrong doses.
  6. The correct answer is A (The pattern is unlikely to be chance, with a probability below five percent): The rule asks for three positive cells reacting and three negative cells not reacting, which gives a probability below 0.05 that the match happened by chance. It supports the specificity statistically but says nothing about clinical significance, which depends on the phase of reaction and the antibody class. Other antibodies still need to be excluded.
  7. The correct answer is C (Collect an EDTA sample now, because screening and dose preparation need lead time): The maternal sample is drawn soon after delivery so that the fetal bleed screen and Rh typing results are ready, allowing the dose to be given within 72 hours. Cord blood tests the infant, not the mother. Waiting several days shortens the window and delays prophylaxis.
  8. The correct answer is A (The DAT is positive because complement is bound, so the report needs correction): Polyspecific reagent detects both IgG and complement, so a 1+ reaction with a negative IgG tube and a 1+ C3d tube shows complement alone is bound to the red cells. That is a positive DAT and the negative entry is wrong. Cold agglutinins and other causes of complement coating need follow up.
  9. The correct answer is B (The bag with the administration set, plus clerical check results and the reaction form): The returned bag and set allow the laboratory to repeat the clerical and serological checks on the very unit transfused. The reaction form and bedside check results show what the ward found. Resuming with another unit or sending only a vital signs sheet would skip the investigation.
  10. The correct answer is C (Treat the weld as defective, check for leaks, and repeat it using fresh tubing ends): A narrowed channel, distortion and reduced flow are signs of an incomplete or defective weld that may leak or compromise the closed system. The weld should be checked and repeated with fresh tubing ends before the product is used. Extra heat does not repair a weld, and defects matter for every component, not only red cells.
  11. The correct answer is C (Treat it as urgent and begin immediate-spin or electronic crossmatching ahead of routine work): A STAT request for active bleeding is prioritized, and a negative screen on a current specimen permits a rapid immediate-spin or electronic crossmatch. Uncrossmatched release is for situations where even that time is unavailable. Insisting on a full antiglobulin crossmatch would waste minutes the patient may not have.
  12. The correct answer is A (Report antibody detected, anti-Fya or anti-Jkb not excluded, identification incomplete, and antigen negative units pending): An interim report should say exactly what is known: an antibody is present, which specificities remain possible, and that identification is not finished. Naming one specificity or claiming both overstates the evidence. Reporting the screen as negative hides a clinically significant finding and could lead to an unsafe transfusion.
  13. The correct answer is D (Defer the donor today, because the value is below the minimum for women): The program minimum for women is 125 g/L, so 123 g/L falls below it and the donor is deferred and advised to return after her iron status improves. Small tolerances, reduced volumes or a different procedure do not change the hemoglobin requirement, which exists to protect the donor from anemia.
  14. The correct answer is B (Type E directly with the IgM reagent and use IgG removal or genotyping for Fya): IgM reagents agglutinate directly at room temperature and do not depend on the antiglobulin phase, so a positive DAT does not interfere with the E result. The Fya typing needs an antiglobulin reagent, so the patient's IgG must first be removed by a validated method, or genotyping used. Both antigens can be reliably typed.
  15. The correct answer is A (Urine from an Sda positive person or from a guinea pig, which contains soluble Sda): Sda antigen is found in a soluble form in urine, particularly from guinea pigs and from Sda positive people, and this neutralizes anti-Sda. The small mixed-field agglutinates are the classic appearance of this antibody. Hydatid cyst fluid supplies P1 substance and secretor saliva supplies Lewis and ABH substances, so neither fits.
  16. The correct answer is D (Irradiate the unit, because shared HLA types between relatives raise the risk of graft-versus-host disease): Donors who are blood relatives of the recipient are more likely to share HLA types, which can let donor lymphocytes engraft and cause transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease, so these cellular components are irradiated. Washing or freezing addresses other problems, and ABO and Rh compatibility alone is not sufficient.
  17. The correct answer is C (Retrain staff to read tubes right after centrifugation and document a competency assessment): Weak agglutination in an immediate spin tube can disperse if the tube stands before it is read, so the root cause is the technique and not the reagent or the equipment. Retraining with a documented competency check corrects the cause. Changing reagents, methods or equipment would not address a reading delay.
  18. The correct answer is D (Fourteen assessments remain, because 14 x 8 equals 112 and 112 minus 98 leaves 14): The total yearly requirement is 14 technologists times 8 assessments, which is 112. Subtracting the 98 completed leaves 14 to schedule. Using 120 as the total comes from a multiplication slip, and treating 98 as the remainder confuses completed work with work still owed.
  19. The correct answer is D (Panel lot, reactions by phase, autocontrol, rule out cells, and 3 and 3 evidence): A reviewer can only verify an identification if the summary shows which panel was used, how each cell reacted in every phase, the autocontrol, which cells ruled out other antibodies, and the evidence behind the 3 and 3 rule. A name and date alone cannot be checked, and the other phases carry information, for example about temperature range.
  20. The correct answer is C (Cryoprecipitate, because it concentrates fibrinogen into a small volume that plasma cannot match): Cryoprecipitate concentrates fibrinogen into a small volume, so a pool corrects a deficit that plasma could fix only with a very large infusion. Plasma contains fibrinogen at about its normal concentration, and cryoprecipitate-reduced plasma has had much of the fibrinogen removed. The INR of 1.3 and the normal platelet count show that other factors and platelets are not the problem.
  21. The correct answer is A (AHG bridges IgG on neighbouring cells, linking sensitized cells that sit too far apart to clump): AHG is an antibody against human globulin. It attaches to IgG molecules coating neighbouring red cells and links them into visible clumps, even though the antibody-coated cells were too far apart to agglutinate alone. It does not lyse cells, add IgG or change the surface charge.
  22. The correct answer is B (Flag it corrected and show both results, because readers need the full audit trail of changes): A corrected report must be marked as corrected and must show both the original and the amended result so the audit trail is complete. Reporting only the new result hides that a prior value was seen and possibly acted on. Naming a reader to assign blame does not belong on a patient report.
  23. The correct answer is D (Wash the wound thoroughly with soap and running water and allow gentle bleeding): Recommended first aid for a percutaneous injury is to wash the site well with soap and running water and let it bleed gently, then report the injury. Squeezing or scrubbing bruises the tissue and does not remove virus, and strong chemicals such as bleach damage skin without benefit. A glove does not filter blood, so the exposure still needs assessment.
  24. The correct answer is A (Clean the cord of maternal blood and Wharton jelly, then draw from the umbilical vein): Maternal blood and Wharton jelly on the cord can cause mixed groups and false reactions, so the cord is wiped clean and blood is drawn from the vein. Milking the cord can introduce jelly and tissue, and saline rinses dilute or spread contamination. Collecting from the placental surface gives a mixture of maternal and infant blood.
  25. The correct answer is A (The volume is inadequate for the requested tests, so a second sample is needed): Grouping, antibody screening and crossmatching of two units need more cells and plasma than half a millilitre supplies, so a further sample is needed. EDTA plasma is acceptable for crossmatching in most laboratories. One tube serves many units, and the real fault is volume, not unit count.
  26. The correct answer is C (3+, because several large agglutinates against a clear background define a strong reaction): Grading follows clump size and background: 1+ is weak small clumps, 2+ is medium clumps with a clear background, 3+ is several large clumps, and 4+ is one solid agglutinate. This tube shows several large clumps with a clear supernatant, so it is 3+. A solid single block would be 4+.
  27. The correct answer is D (Rouleaux formation, which differs from true agglutination where cells bind in irregular grapelike clusters): Rouleaux look like stacked coins with smooth cell edges and disperse when saline is added. True agglutination shows irregular grapelike clusters held together by antibody bridges. Cold antibody and fibrin give irregular clumps, not regular neat stacks.
  28. The correct answer is D (Recollect with gentle technique, because in vitro hemolysis can mimic a true hemolytic reaction): A traumatic draw and hard shaking explain in vitro hemolysis, which colours the plasma and can mask a real hemolytic reaction. Dilution weakens antibodies and spinning does not remove dissolved hemoglobin. A gently collected new sample removes the problem.
  29. The correct answer is B (Flush the eye with water or saline at the eyewash for fifteen minutes): A splash to the eye is treated by flushing with large amounts of clean water or saline for about fifteen minutes, then reporting the exposure. Rubbing can injure the cornea and drive material in, and antiseptics are irritating and unsuitable for the eye. A cold compress does nothing to remove the blood.
  30. The correct answer is D (Hold issue and notify the physician, so the earlier refusal can be reviewed now): A signed refusal remains a clear statement of the patient's wishes until it is shown not to apply. The physician and the care team decide how it applies, not the laboratory or the family alone. Splitting the dose does not respect the refusal and is not a recognized solution.
  31. The correct answer is D (Explain the reason and respect the patient's choices, because trust supports safe care): Culturally safe care means the patient feels respected, informed and able to decide, which builds trust and supports accurate information. Dismissing questions or transferring them to relatives removes the patient's voice. Skipping required questions silently can compromise safety and documentation.
  32. The correct answer is B (A possible identification or testing error that must be resolved before units are issued): ABO group does not change in a healthy adult, so a mismatch with history points to a wrong blood in tube event, a transcription error, or a previous record error. The LIS block forces investigation before blood is released. It is a safety control, not a display setting.
  33. The correct answer is C (18.75 percent, calculated as 15 wasted pools divided by the 80 pools received): Wasted pools are the 12 expired plus the 3 discarded, which is 15 of 80. Dividing 15 by 80 gives 0.1875, or 18.75 percent. Using only one cause of loss, or the transfused pools, answers a different question.
  34. The correct answer is C (Room temperature storage lets bacteria multiply, so shelf life is limited and units are screened): Platelets must be kept at 20 to 24 C, a temperature at which many bacteria grow readily, whereas red cells at 1 to 6 C restrict growth. This is why the shelf life is only 5 to 7 days and why bacterial screening or pathogen reduction is applied. Agitation and bag permeability are needed for platelet function and do not feed bacteria.
  35. The correct answer is B (Notify the transfusion medicine physician and follow the supplier's look-back process for patient notification): A transfused unit cannot be quarantined, so the response shifts to look-back: the medical director is told and the responsible physician is informed through the established process. The laboratory does not disclose directly to the patient or delay notification to run its own testing. Closing the file without notification would leave a possible exposure unmanaged.
  36. The correct answer is D (Dedicate one irradiated, CMV safe unit and issue small aliquots from it for repeat use): Assigning one unit and dividing it into small aliquots, using a sterile connecting device, limits the infant to a single donor within the unit expiry. Pooling or using a new donor each time increases exposure. Plasma is not a substitute for red cells to correct anemia.
  37. The correct answer is D (Hold the sample and ask the ward to confirm the order, because testing needs a request): The specimen is held, not rejected, until the order is confirmed and entered through the proper process, so the testing record links to a verified request. The sample itself is fine, and orders should not be entered on recollection or assumption.
  38. The correct answer is A (Place a paper barrier between coolant packs and bags, so contact cannot freeze the cells): A barrier prevents the cold surface of coolant packs from freezing red cells at the contact point, which would cause hemolysis. Direct contact, wet wrapping and unseparated stacking all raise the risk of freezing or label damage.
  39. The correct answer is C (Transfer platelets to a working agitator, tag the unit out of service, and tell the supervisor): Platelets need continuous agitation as well as 20 to 24 C, so an in-range temperature does not make a stationary tray acceptable. Units should be moved to working equipment and the failed agitator removed from use and reported. Refrigeration damages platelets, and waiting leaves the units unagitated for longer.
  40. The correct answer is B (Review actual use and propose a maximum surgical blood order schedule with the surgeons): A high ratio from one service points to ordering habits rather than a laboratory fault, so the fix is a data based agreement on how many units each procedure really needs. A maximum surgical blood order schedule reduces idle crossmatched units without removing safety. Skipping crossmatch, moving the target or cutting stock does not address the cause and may harm patients.
  41. The correct answer is C (At least 15 minutes with the eyelids held open, then seek medical assessment): Standard guidance is to flush the eye with water for at least 15 minutes with the eyelids held apart, then arrange medical assessment, because chemical contact continues to injure tissue until it is diluted out. Short flushes remove only a part of the chemical. Even disinfectants thought to be mild can cause real damage if flushed briefly.
  42. The correct answer is B (Move the platelets to a verified incubator, quarantine them pending review, and report the discrepancy): The reference thermometer is independent and shows the tray is above 24 C, so the display may be wrong and the units may have been out of range. Units should be moved to working storage, held for evaluation against supplier guidance, and the fault reported. Resetting the display or ignoring the discrepancy hides a real excursion, and automatic discard is premature before review.
  43. The correct answer is D (Read each tube promptly, because weak antiglobulin reactions can fade as antibody dissociates from the cells): Weak antiglobulin reactions can dissociate with time after centrifugation, so a delay can turn a true weak positive into an apparent negative. Tubes should be read promptly, a few at a time if needed. More spin time, extra AHG or shaking later do not recover a reaction that has faded.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (A recipient of red cells from a first-degree relative, because shared HLA haplotypes raise the risk): Blood from a blood relative carries a shared HLA haplotype, so the recipient's immune system may not recognize donor lymphocytes as foreign, allowing them to engraft and cause graft versus host disease. Immunocompetent patients with anemia, bleeding or sickle cell disease do not need irradiation, and frozen plasma does not contain viable lymphocytes.
  2. The correct answer is C (The trauma patient sample, because active bleeding makes a delay in compatible blood dangerous): Specimens are prioritised by clinical urgency, and active hemorrhage outranks scheduled work. First-come, first-served order and clearing easy tests first do not reflect patient risk, though the routine samples are still completed afterwards.
Case CHB3

A 70-year-old woman with a bleeding gastric ulcer needs two red cell units within 90 minutes. Her antibody screen is positive and the panel identifies anti-Fya. Typing of 6 stocked group-compatible units finds only 1 Fy(a) negative. The next antigen negative units must come from the supplier, with a delivery time of about 2 hours.

  1. The correct answer is D (Only one compatible unit is ready, and a second unit may take about two hours): The physician needs to know now that only one antigen negative unit exists and that the other unit depends on supplier delivery, since this affects the bleeding patient's treatment plan. Anti-Fya is clinically significant, so units that carry the antigen cannot be used. Withholding the shortage would delay decisions about the patient.
  2. The correct answer is A (Complete the antiglobulin crossmatch, issue the unit if compatible, and place the supplier order): With anti-Fya present, an antiglobulin crossmatch is needed to detect incompatibility, and the available time allows the full test. Issuing before testing or using immediate spin alone would miss a reaction at the antiglobulin phase. Placing the supplier order at once avoids a longer delay for the other unit.
  3. The correct answer is A (Tell the physician about the longer delay now, and record who was told and when): A change that lengthens the wait for blood is a delay the treating physician must hear immediately so that other measures can be considered. The call is recorded with the name of the person told and the time, which meets documentation standards. A log entry, a clerk or a later call does not give the physician timely information.
  4. The correct answer is D (A recall covers a product that may be unsafe, so prompt action is required): A recall is issued when a product may be unsafe or violates a regulation, so affected units must be found and controlled promptly. A market withdrawal covers minor issues that do not affect safety, such as a cosmetic labelling error. Neither type is optional for the hospital, and a recall covers inventory as well as units already issued.
  5. The correct answer is A (2.5 SD above the mean, a warning only, so other rules are checked before deciding): The difference is 25 minus 20, which is 5 points, and 5 divided by 2 gives 2.5 SD. One value between 2 and 3 SD is a 1-2s warning, so the remaining multirule checks are applied before deciding whether to reject the run.
  6. The correct answer is A (Reject it and request recollection, because clots make grouping and screening results unreliable): Clots trap red cells and make the cell suspension uneven, so grouping, screening and crossmatching cannot be trusted. Picking out the clots or adding anticoagulant cannot restore a clotted specimen. A fresh, well mixed sample is needed.
  7. The correct answer is D (Accept it and place it on the agitator now, since conditions stayed acceptable): The unit was within the 24 hour no-agitation allowance, the logger stayed inside 20 to 24 C, and swirling shows that the platelets are still viable, so it can be accepted and agitation resumed. Refrigeration would damage the platelets and holding it longer without agitation would waste the remaining allowance.
  8. The correct answer is B (A post-transfusion sample showing visible hemolysis plus a newly positive direct antiglobulin test result): Hemolysis with a new positive direct antiglobulin test after transfusion suggests a hemolytic reaction, which is life threatening and needs immediate action. A result that matches the historical record, a cancelled request, or a cold antibody that does not react at 37 C does not put the patient at urgent risk and can follow routine reporting.
  9. The correct answer is C (Cryoprecipitate-reduced plasma, which has less fibrinogen, factor VIII and von Willebrand factor than standard plasma): Thawing at 1 to 6 C leaves the cold-insoluble precipitate behind, and removing it leaves cryoprecipitate-reduced plasma. That supernatant is depleted of fibrinogen, factor VIII and the large von Willebrand factor multimers, while other proteins such as ADAMTS13 remain. The precipitate itself is cryoprecipitate, which is a different product.
  10. The correct answer is D (To detect a rising antibody level that signals a fetus at risk of hemolytic disease): Serial titres tracked on the same method show whether antibody concentration is rising, which predicts the risk of fetal anemia and guides when to refer for ultrasound assessment. Titration does not identify the antibody, type the fetus, or set an immune globulin dose, because those need other tests.
  11. The correct answer is D (Release the compatible units and record it, because the antibody is inactive at 37 C): The antibody does not react at 37 C and the antiglobulin crossmatch is compatible, so the policy allows release. Lewis antibodies rarely shorten red cell survival because the antigens are neutralized and shed from transfused cells. Delay, group O restriction or molecular typing would add risk or time without benefit.
  12. The correct answer is C (Check the shelf and records and resolve the count, because inventory must match): A discrepancy between a verbal report and the inventory record must be settled by physically verifying units and reviewing documentation. Accepting the verbal count risks telling the team that blood is available when it is not. Waiting until tomorrow is too late for surgery, and reserving blindly can create further inventory errors.
  13. The correct answer is D (Issue a corrected report, keeping the original visible and recording who changed it and why): A corrected report must preserve the original entry, identify the person making the change, and record the date, time and reason so the record can be audited. Overwriting or deleting hides the error trail. The care team also needs to be told directly because a unit has already been allocated.
  14. The correct answer is A (It detects viral genetic material early, which shortens the window period before antibodies appear): Nucleic acid testing finds viral RNA or DNA before antibodies are produced, so recently infected donors can be detected sooner than with antibody tests alone. It does not identify antibodies or measure hemoglobin. Bacterial contamination of platelets is addressed by culture or other bacterial detection methods.
  15. The correct answer is D (Four vials: 100 mL divided by 30 gives 3.3, which rounds to 3, plus one): The fetal bleed is 2.0 times 50, or 100 mL of whole blood. Dividing by 30 mL per vial gives 3.33, which rounds down to 3, and the policy adds one vial for a total of 4. Leaving out the extra vial, rounding up incorrectly, or dividing by 15 mL all produce wrong totals.
  16. The correct answer is A (About 15 percent, found by multiplying the two frequencies, 0.20 and 0.77, together): For independently inherited antigens the proportion negative for both is the product of the two negative frequencies, 0.20 x 0.77 = 0.154, or about 15 percent. Adding frequencies would give a value larger than either one, and subtracting or averaging has no probability basis. About 3 in 20 random donor units would therefore be expected to be negative for both antigens.
  17. The correct answer is A (Week 28, when the titre first reached 16, which meets the stated critical level): The policy sets 16 as critical, and the curve first meets the 16 line at week 28. Week 32 is higher but not the first critical value, and the earlier weeks stay below the line. The laboratory should phone the result so Doppler assessment can begin promptly.
  18. The correct answer is D (Each reagent reacts with positive cells and not negative cells, confirming reactivity and specificity): Daily testing with known antigen-positive and antigen-negative cells shows that each reagent still gives the expected reaction strength and no false reaction on that day. A reagent can deteriorate after opening through contamination or poor storage, so a pass today does not cover later days. Sterility and label accuracy are separate checks.
  19. The correct answer is C (Movement promotes gas exchange through the bag, limiting lactate buildup and a fall in pH): Stored platelets keep metabolizing, so oxygen must enter and carbon dioxide must leave through the gas-permeable bag. Without movement, lactate builds up and the pH falls, which damages platelets. Agitation does not warm the unit and does not stop bacterial growth or change surface proteins.
  20. The correct answer is A (14 March 2027, which is exactly 12 months after the collection date): The policy counts 12 months from collection, so 14 March 2026 becomes 14 March 2027. Storage at minus 25 C is colder than the stated threshold but the policy sets no longer expiry for it, and counting weeks or shorter months than the stated interval would discard usable plasma.
  21. The correct answer is C (A weak or negative DAT, because few A antigen sites on newborn cells bind maternal IgG): In ABO hemolytic disease of the newborn, A and B antigens are weakly developed on newborn red cells, so little maternal IgG binds. The DAT is therefore weak or negative even when jaundice is present. A strong DAT is more typical of Rh hemolytic disease, and IgM does not cross the placenta.
  22. The correct answer is C (Titre changes are meaningful only when measured identically, so a true rise is detected): Titres vary between methods, technologists and reagents, so a difference between two samples is reliable only when they are tested together with the same technique. A rise of two dilutions or more is then taken as significant. Matching the method does not change the antibody level, and titres from different laboratories cannot be compared directly.
  23. The correct answer is A (Record the hemolysis grade on the report and finish testing since reactions are readable): Slight hemolysis does not stop testing when reactions can be read with confidence, but the finding must be documented so later staff understand the specimen. Discarding every pink specimen would delay patients unnecessarily. Dilution does not remove free hemoglobin and changes antibody strength, so it is not an acceptable correction.
  24. The correct answer is A (Recollect a fresh sample and repeat testing, since pink free hemoglobin can mask hemolysis): Pink plasma means free hemoglobin, which can hide hemolysis caused by a complement-binding antibody and so make a true positive look negative. A new sample, drawn so that in vitro hemolysis is avoided, lets the reaction be read without that colour. Reporting a negative screen or diluting the plasma does not remove the problem, and adding cells only changes the cell to plasma ratio.
  25. The correct answer is C (Rescue anyone in danger, sound the alarm, close doors, then extinguish or evacuate): RACE stands for rescue, alarm, contain and extinguish or evacuate, in that order, so that people are protected and help is called before any attempt to fight the fire. Fighting the fire first or finishing laboratory work delays the alarm. Closing doors limits spread, but it follows rescue and the alarm.
  26. The correct answer is D (Negative, with a note that rouleaux at immediate spin was resolved by saline replacement): Rouleaux caused the immediate spin reactions, and saline replacement dispersed them. The 37 C and antiglobulin phases were negative, so no antibody is present. The screen is reported as negative with a comment, and no panel or new specimen is needed.
  27. The correct answer is B (Yes, select antigen-negative red cells, because the antibody level may now be below detection): Kidd antibodies are well known for dropping below the level a screen can detect, yet a later exposure can cause a delayed hemolytic reaction. The cumulative record keeps the history so antigen-negative units are chosen even when today's screen is negative. A negative result does not erase an earlier clinically significant finding.
  28. The correct answer is B (Investigate the steady upward trend and service the bath before readings reach the upper limit): Each reading is 0.3 C higher than the day before, a drift that will reach the 38 C limit within days even though every reading passes. Trend review catches this early so the bath can be serviced before a result is affected. Waiting for a failure, replacing a calibrated thermometer or averaging the values hides the pattern.
  29. The correct answer is A (Probable ABO HDFN, so report it and recommend clinical monitoring for bilirubin rise): A weak positive DAT in a group A infant of a group O mother with jaundice fits ABO HDFN, which is generally mild and managed by bilirubin monitoring and phototherapy. Rh disease needs an Rh negative mother with anti-D. Exchange transfusion is not indicated by a weak DAT alone.
  30. The correct answer is B (Transfusion or pregnancy within the previous three months, or a history that is unknown): Recent transfusion or pregnancy can stimulate new red cell antibodies within days, so the sample must be fresh. An unknown history is treated as recent for safety. Surgery, a known antibody alone, or blood group do not trigger the rule.
  31. The correct answer is D (Quarantine and report the excursion to the transplant program, because they decide acceptability): A reading warmer than the program limit is a deviation that must be documented and reported, not judged by the technologist. The transplant physician or program decides whether the cells remain acceptable, since some excursions are tolerated. Warming, refreezing or discarding without that decision can destroy a product that cannot be replaced.
  32. The correct answer is D (Use the temporary identifier on the band, tube and requisition so every record links): A temporary unique identifier assigned at registration is applied to the band, the tube and the requisition, so results and units can be traced to one record even before the real name is known. A room number can change and does not identify a person. Delaying the label leaves an unlabelled specimen in the department, and an informal first name is not unique.
  33. The correct answer is A (Comparison with a traceable reference thermometer, because measurement accuracy must be demonstrated, not assumed): A thermometer is only trusted when it has been compared with a reference traceable to a recognized standard and any correction is recorded. Age, a matching unit of the same model or an alarm test cannot prove the displayed value is accurate.
  34. The correct answer is C (Patient identifiers, unit number, component type, issue date and time, and the receiving person): A record of issue must let the unit be traced to one patient and one handover, so it holds patient identifiers, the unit number and component, the date and time of issue and who received it. Records limited to unit data or ward data cannot link a unit to a patient and a person.
  35. The correct answer is B (The infant is Rh positive and the DAT shows maternal antibody on cord cells): Anti-D reacts with the cord cells, so the infant is Rh positive, and the IgG DAT is positive with a negative C3d result, showing maternal IgG bound to infant cells. This pattern is consistent with HDFN caused by maternal anti-D. The infant carries the paternal Rh positive gene.
  36. The correct answer is B (Donation identification number, component name, ABO and Rh group, and expiry date): A component label must carry the unique donation identification number, the component name, the ABO and Rh group and the expiry date so the unit can be traced and checked against the patient. Donor personal details are confidential and are not printed, and recipient information belongs on the issue tag rather than on the supplier label.
  37. The correct answer is A (Discard it as biohazardous waste because blood is infectious, and record the disposition): Expired blood components remain potentially infectious and are handled as biohazardous waste even when the bag is intact. The disposition must be recorded so the inventory and traceability records stay accurate. Sink disposal, general garbage and an unrecorded return all bypass the required waste stream or the audit trail.
  38. The correct answer is B (Reject the run and hold patient results, because the 3 SD limit was exceeded): A control value beyond 3 SD breaks the 1-3s rejection rule, so patient results from the run are held until the cause is found and the controls pass. Recalculating the mean to absorb the point would hide a real problem.
  39. The correct answer is A (Ask a qualified colleague to review it, since practice stays within demonstrated competence): Provincial college standards require registrants to practise within their demonstrated knowledge, skill and judgment and to seek help when a task exceeds them. A supervisor's instruction does not transfer the technologist's own accountability. Holding the report for a medical director is not a routine requirement when a qualified peer can review the work.
  40. The correct answer is B (Washing was adequate and the reagent was active, so negative results are valid): IgG-coated check cells react only if free antiglobulin reagent was present, so their agglutination shows that the washing removed unbound globulin and that the reagent was active. This validates the negative tubes. It does not show anything about the patient's antibodies, the specimen collection, or what the reagent contains.
  41. The correct answer is A (Screening cell I, because unreactive check cells mean its negative AHG result is not valid): In the table, screening cell I was negative at the antiglobulin phase and its check cells also stayed negative, so the AHG was neutralized or not added in that tube and the negative result is invalid. Screening cell II is positive and needs no check cells. Screening cell III and the autocontrol both have valid check cell reactions at 3+.
  42. The correct answer is A (Stop use, keep others out, and report to the radiation safety officer for repair): Interlocks stop the source from exposing anyone who enters the room, so a faulty one means the device is not safe to run. The technologist should stop, restrict access and notify the radiation safety officer, who deals with the licence conditions and repair. Bypassing the sensor or changing the cycle time does not restore the protection.
  43. The correct answer is B (Discard the leaking unit and thaw a replacement, because integrity is compromised): A leak at the port means bacteria from the water bath may have entered the component, so its sterility cannot be trusted even when the plasma looks normal. The urgency of the bleeding does not justify issuing a possibly contaminated unit when a replacement can be thawed within minutes. Thawed plasma should not be refrozen.
  44. The correct answer is B (Expected engraftment of donor red cells, so follow the transplant ABO protocol before releasing blood): A group O patient engrafting group A donor marrow gradually produces group A red cells and loses anti-A, so a mixed field and absent anti-A fit the expected change. The laboratory follows its transplant protocol for component selection rather than calling an error. Because the sample was labelled correctly, wrong blood in tube and reagent failure are unlikely.
  45. The correct answer is A (The unit is group B, incompatible with the group A patient, so notify the physician): The patient is group A, but the returned unit types as group B, so the red cells were ABO incompatible with the recipient's anti-B. The mixed field forward type and mixed field DAT fit group B donor cells coated with anti-B circulating with the patient's own cells. The physician must be told immediately and the remaining units and records checked, rather than repeating tests or pursuing autoantibody work first.
  46. The correct answer is A (Obtain a new sample, because recent transfusion limits the sample to three days): When a patient has been transfused or pregnant in the previous three months, the pretransfusion sample is valid for only three days. A sample four days old has expired because new antibodies may have appeared, even though the screen and ABO groups were acceptable. A negative screen on an expired sample or a repeat ABO alone does not meet the requirement.
  47. The correct answer is D (Treat the patient as Rh negative and select D-negative units without weak D testing): Under the stated policy, recipient testing for weak D is not performed, so a patient who types D negative is managed as Rh negative and receives D-negative red cells. This avoids exposing a patient who might be partial D to D-positive blood. Extra reagents and D-positive units are not part of the policy.
  48. The correct answer is B (The sample is still acceptable, because Thursday is day 3 and expiry is 23:59): Under the stated policy Monday is day 0, so Tuesday is day 1, Wednesday day 2 and Thursday day 3, and the sample is usable until 23:59. Counting 72 hours from the draw time is a different rule that this laboratory does not use.
  49. The correct answer is A (They compare performance with peers and reveal gaps, because results guide corrective action): Proficiency testing shows how a laboratory and its staff perform against peers using unknown samples, which exposes weaknesses that can be corrected. It supplements, but does not replace, daily quality control. It is not a lifetime credential or a training course.
  50. The correct answer is D (20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation, because platelets need gas exchange to function): Platelets are stored at 20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation, which lets oxygen in and carbon dioxide out through the permeable bag and keeps pH in range. Cold storage makes platelets change shape so the liver removes them quickly after transfusion. Warming to 37 C would speed metabolism and bacterial growth, and stopping agitation lets pH fall.
  51. The correct answer is C (Repeat the six tests on new cards once the centrifuge fault is investigated and cleared): An early program end means the cards were not spun for the full time or speed, so cells were not separated and the pattern cannot be read. Scattered cells in the negative control column confirm that the run is invalid. The cards must be repeated with fresh cards after the fault is resolved, and respinning or reporting from a failed run is not acceptable.
  52. The correct answer is D (Lots can differ in potency and specificity, so reactivity must be confirmed with known controls): Reagent lots vary in potency, avidity and specificity, so each new lot is tested with known positive and negative controls before patient testing. This confirms that the lot gives the expected reactions. Verification does not change the expiry date, alter reference values or measure shipping conditions.
  53. The correct answer is B (IgG coating her cells was recovered and reacts with all cells, consistent with autoantibody): An acid eluate removes the antibody bound to red cells in vivo, so a positive IgG DAT with an eluate reacting with all panel cells shows that the coating IgG behaves as a warm autoantibody. Complement is not involved because the anti-C3d test is negative. Cold agglutinins are IgM and react best below 37 C, and an alloantibody eluate would react only with antigen-positive cells.
  54. The correct answer is D (Objective facts: times, unit number, staff involved, and actions taken, without opinion or blame): A useful incident report records verifiable facts: when it happened, which unit, who was involved and what was done. Opinions and blame distort the record and discourage reporting. Leaving details for a verbal talk or adding unrelated clinical history weakens the evidence.
  55. The correct answer is C (About 3,000 rpm, because rpm squared equals force divided by 1.118 x10^-5 times 10): Rearranging gives rpm squared equal to 1,000 divided by (1.118 x10^-5 x 10), about 8.94 million, and the square root is close to 3,000 rpm. Using the numerical force as the speed, leaving out the radius or entering the radius in the wrong units gives the other values.
  56. The correct answer is B (Invert the tube gently eight to ten times after filling, so additive mixes with blood): EDTA tubes need several gentle inversions immediately after filling so the additive dissolves and prevents small clots. Vigorous shaking can cause hemolysis, and overfilling reduces the additive to blood ratio. Waiting before mixing allows clots to start forming that mimic agglutination.
  57. The correct answer is C (Wash the cells several times in 37 C saline, because warmth releases cold antibody): Washing the cells with saline prewarmed to 37 C removes cold antibody that is bound only weakly above its thermal range, and the cells then type normally. Washing in cold saline lets the antibody keep binding. Adding reagent or diluting does not remove the antibody coating the cells.
  58. The correct answer is A (IgM binds cells in cool extremities and fixes complement, and C3b coated cells are cleared): IgM autoantibody binds red cells in cool parts of the body and fixes complement. When the cells return to the warm core the IgM leaves but C3b stays attached, and macrophages in the liver remove the coated cells. Splenic removal of IgG coated cells and direct cold damage to hemoglobin are not the mechanism of cold agglutinin disease.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (A workplace label naming the product, handling precautions and where to find the data sheet): When a hazardous product is decanted into a secondary container, the container needs a workplace label with the product identifier, safe handling information and a reference to the safety data sheet. The supplier label stays on the original container and does not travel with the spray bottle. A date or a coloured sticker alone gives later users no usable hazard information.
  2. The correct answer is D (Follow the identifier policy and address the patient respectfully, because dignity and safety coexist): Samples must carry the identifiers specified by laboratory policy so that every record matches. Staff can address the patient by the name the patient uses while labelling according to policy. Refusing to test or discussing identity publicly is neither safe nor respectful.
  3. The correct answer is C (The patient is K negative, because the controls confirm the reagent and technique worked): When the positive control reacts and the negative control does not, the reagent and technique are shown to be working. A patient tube without agglutination is then a valid negative result. An autocontrol tube is not required to accept a negative typing result, and enzyme treatment is not needed.
Case CHB4

A 58-year-old patient with a history of anti-Fya documented in the cumulative record two years ago is scheduled for cardiac surgery. The current antibody screen is negative. Four units of red cells are requested. The laboratory has two Fy(a) negative units in inventory and the blood supplier can ship more within three hours. The surgery starts in one hour.

  1. The correct answer is D (History still applies, so select Fy(a) negative red cells, because antibody levels can fall): Antibody levels can fall below detection, but memory cells remain and re-exposure to the antigen can cause a delayed hemolytic reaction. The record therefore still governs unit selection even though the screen is negative. Waiting for a positive screen would expose the patient to avoidable risk.
  2. The correct answer is A (Issue the two antigen-negative units now and order two more from the supplier): Two antigen-negative units are available now and the surgery starts soon, so those are issued and more are ordered for later in the procedure. Supplier shipment within three hours fits that need. Issuing untyped units ignores the antibody, and delaying the surgery is unnecessary.
  3. The correct answer is C (Notify the physician, test the unit and patient samples, and report the event as a deviation): Releasing a unit without the required antigen typing is a deviation from the patient's requirements even if no reaction is seen, because a delayed hemolytic reaction can appear days later. The physician must be told, the unit and a post-transfusion sample tested, and the event reported as a deviation. Altering the record to say the unit was typed would falsify it.
  4. The correct answer is B (Stop and ventilate the area, because bleach mixed with acid releases toxic chlorine gas): Hypochlorite mixed with an acid releases chlorine gas, which injures the airways, so the technologist stops, ventilates and gets help or the data sheet instructions. Pouring slowly or adding more bleach does not prevent the reaction and may increase gas release. Leaving the blood undisinfected on the floor creates a different hazard.
  5. The correct answer is D (Anti-c, because it is IgG, crosses the placenta and can cause fetal hemolytic disease): Anti-c is an IgG antibody that crosses the placenta and can cause hemolytic disease of the fetus, so titration and monitoring are indicated. Anti-P1 is usually an IgM antibody that does not cross the placenta. Titration is used for all clinically significant IgG antibodies, not only anti-D.
  6. The correct answer is B (DAT on the donor red cells, because immunoglobulin coated cells react with antiglobulin reagent): A single incompatible unit with a negative screen and negative patient DAT suggests the donor cells are coated with immunoglobulin or complement, so a DAT on the segment is the logical test. Red cells do not carry isoagglutinins for reverse grouping, and Rh phenotype alone does not explain one isolated incompatibility.
  7. The correct answer is D (It warms serum and cells to 37 C before mixing, so cold antibody cannot bind): In the prewarm technique the serum and the reagent cells are warmed separately to 37 C and only then combined, so a cold-reactive antibody has no chance to bind at lower temperatures. The device shown is a 37 C bath, and it maintains that temperature through the incubation. Chilling the tubes or heating the serum to 56 C would either promote cold binding or alter the sample.
  8. The correct answer is A (HPA-1a on glycoprotein IIIa, which the mother lacks and the fetus inherited from the father): In populations of European descent most cases of fetal and neonatal alloimmune thrombocytopenia involve anti-HPA-1a made by an HPA-1a negative mother. The antigen sits on glycoprotein IIIa and is inherited from the father. The other antigens named are real platelet polymorphisms but are much less often responsible.
  9. The correct answer is C (Unit 1, because it shows no agglutination with anti-c and so lacks the c antigen): Anti-c is directed at the c antigen, so a suitable unit must show no reaction with anti-c, and only unit 1 does. Units 2, 3 and 4 all react 3+ or 4+ with anti-c, so they carry the antigen. Absence of C, D or e does not predict absence of c, because those antigens are separate.
  10. The correct answer is D (She may carry weak D antigen, so perform weak D testing and review RhIG need): A strong rosette reaction with no fetal cells on the Kleihauer-Betke slide suggests that the mother's own cells carry D antigen, most likely weak D. Weak D testing and review of her Rh type are needed before deciding on RhIG. A massive bleed would show many fetal cells on the slide, and a D-negative infant could not cause rosettes.
  11. The correct answer is B (A Lewis antibody was neutralized, and anti-K is probably also present, needing confirmation): Reactions with cells 1 and 5 disappeared after neutralization, which fits a Lewis antibody. Cells 4 and 9 remain reactive at the antiglobulin phase and share K positivity, so a second antibody, probably anti-K, is likely and needs confirmation with selected cells. Lewis substance does not neutralize an antibody to the K antigen.
  12. The correct answer is A (Donor HLA or HNA antibodies against recipient leukocytes, because they activate neutrophils in the lung): TRALI is caused by donor antibodies, usually against HLA or HNA antigens, that activate recipient neutrophils in the lung and produce noncardiogenic edema with hypotension. Donor ABO antibodies cause hemolysis rather than isolated lung injury, and anti-IgA reactions present as anaphylaxis. Contamination would produce fever and rigors with positive cultures instead.
  13. The correct answer is B (Incubate cells with anti-D at 37 C, wash, add antiglobulin reagent and include an Rh control): Weak D testing uses the indirect antiglobulin phase: incubation with anti-D at 37 C, washing, and antiglobulin reagent, read alongside an Rh control to check for false positives. A longer spin read, plasma testing or enzyme treatment does not detect weak D reliably.
  14. The correct answer is C (Stop the transfusion and keep the vein open with saline through new tubing): The transfusion must be stopped at once and the line kept open with saline through new tubing so access remains for treatment. The bag and administration set are returned to the laboratory for investigation. Slowing, speeding up or discarding the unit delays treatment or destroys evidence.
  15. The correct answer is B (A titre of 32, because the 1 in 32 tube is the last 1+ reaction): The endpoint is the highest dilution that still gives 1+ agglutination, which is the 1 in 32 tube, and the titre is its reciprocal. The weak positive in the next tube is below the 1+ endpoint and is not used for the titre, though it adds to a score. The first negative tube and the number of tubes do not define the titre.
  16. The correct answer is D (Autoadsorption with his own treated cells, which cannot adsorb alloantibodies to antigens he lacks): Autoadsorption uses the patient's own red cells, usually pretreated to remove bound IgG, so the autoantibody is adsorbed while alloantibodies to antigens the patient lacks stay in the serum for panel testing. It is suitable only when the patient has not been recently transfused, because donor cells would adsorb alloantibodies too. Cold adsorption is for cold autoantibodies, and titration does not separate the two antibody types.
  17. The correct answer is C (Report anti-Jka as significant needing Jka negative red cells and note anti-Lea as insignificant): Kidd antibodies react at the antiglobulin phase and can cause delayed hemolytic transfusion reactions, so Jka negative red cells are required. A Lewis antibody that does not react at 37 C is generally clinically insignificant, but it is still noted so the pattern is understood later. Omitting either finding would remove useful information from the record.
  18. The correct answer is D (Keep it at 20 to 24 C, because refrigerating thawed cryoprecipitate is not permitted): Once thawed, pooled cryoprecipitate is held at room temperature, 20 to 24 C, and used within its short post-thaw expiry. Refrigerating or refreezing thawed units is not permitted, and holding at 37 C would degrade the factors.
  19. The correct answer is C (Verify the caller's identity and role by calling back the receiving hospital's main laboratory number): Disclosure for continuing care is permitted, so refusal could harm the patient. A call back to a known main number confirms that the requester is genuine. Personal email and unverified callers both risk sending sensitive information to the wrong place.
  20. The correct answer is D (Test the sample, warn the physician results may be affected, and request any earlier sample): A post-transfusion sample can show mixed fields and weak antibodies diluted by donor plasma, so results need careful interpretation. Rejecting it would delay care. The physician needs to know the limits, and an earlier pretransfusion specimen from the same admission should be sought.
  21. The correct answer is C (0.3 mL of packed cells with 9.7 mL of saline, giving 10 mL total): A 3 percent suspension of 10 mL needs 3 percent of 10 mL, which is 0.3 mL of cells. The rest of the 10 mL, 9.7 mL, is saline. Adding 10 mL of saline gives 10.3 mL and a lower concentration, and the other volumes give 30 percent and 0.3 percent.
  22. The correct answer is C (Plasma, because it supplies the missing ADAMTS13 enzyme while the harmful antibody is removed): In TTP an antibody against ADAMTS13 allows large von Willebrand multimers to form clots in small vessels. Exchange removes the antibody and plasma replaces the missing enzyme. Albumin lacks ADAMTS13, and platelet transfusion can add fuel to microvascular clotting, so it is withheld unless life threatening bleeding occurs.
  23. The correct answer is A (Send that first voided urine promptly in a clean container labelled with the time): The first urine passed after the reaction is the most useful because free hemoglobin may pass quickly. The time on the label shows how soon after the reaction it was voided. A pooled collection dilutes the finding, and waiting or discarding the supernatant loses the evidence.
  24. The correct answer is D (Select Jkb negative units and complete an antiglobulin crossmatch, because Kidd antibodies fade yet can recur): Kidd antibodies often fall below the detection limit but a new exposure can trigger a rapid anamnestic response and delayed hemolysis. Clinically significant antibodies in the history therefore still require antigen negative units and an antiglobulin crossmatch. Delaying an urgent transfusion for a panel is not necessary when safe units are available.
  25. The correct answer is D (Clumped cells are trapped in or above the gel while free cells reach the bottom): Centrifugation pushes red cells through the gel. Cells linked into clumps by antibody are too large to pass and stay trapped in or above the gel, while free cells pass to the bottom. The gel does not lyse cells or dissolve clumps, and cells do not all reach the bottom when agglutination has occurred.
  26. The correct answer is B (19 units, found by taking 120 minus the 91 transfused, 4 discarded and 6 outdated units): Start with 120 received units. Subtract the 91 transfused, the 4 discarded after return and the 6 outdated, which leaves 19 units, and these include the 9 that were returned and restocked. Taking only issued units or ignoring the outdated ones gives a wrong count.
  27. The correct answer is D (Recollect peripherally and repeat the screen, because line contamination can create false reactions): Weak reactions with every cell and the autocontrol, together with fibrin strands in a line sample, point to heparin contamination rather than a true antibody. A panel on a compromised sample wastes time and could mislead. Recollecting from a peripheral vein is the reliable way to resolve it.
  28. The correct answer is A (Label at the bedside immediately after collection, with the patient present, before leaving the room): Labelling at the bedside with the patient present links the tube to that patient before any other tube or patient can intrude. Labelling away from the bed, in advance or in batches, allows a tube to be filled for or labelled as the wrong person. Smudging is managed with printed labels, not by moving the task away from the patient.
  29. The correct answer is B (Hemoglobin and protein are both much lower than the patient's recent values): Infusion fluid adds volume without cells or protein, so several measured values fall together when a sample is diluted. Pigment, antibody and cell concentrations cannot rise from added fluid. A sudden fall across unrelated analytes with no clinical reason is the classic sign.
  30. The correct answer is B (Hold the result, recheck the entries and worksheet, and request a new specimen): A current group that differs from the historical group is a red flag for either a transcription error or a wrong blood in tube event. The result must not be finalized until the entries are rechecked and a new, properly labelled specimen confirms the group. Forcing the current result to match history would hide a possible error.
  31. The correct answer is D (Red cells washed with saline to remove nearly all residual plasma proteins): Washing red cells with saline removes most of the plasma and therefore the IgA that triggers a reaction in an IgA deficient patient with anti-IgA. Leukoreduction and irradiation target white cells, not plasma proteins, and the age of a unit does not remove its plasma. Donors who are IgA deficient are another option when washing is not available.
  32. The correct answer is C (Wear gloves, because the exclamation mark warns of irritation or other lesser health effects): The exclamation mark pictogram identifies hazards such as skin and eye irritation, skin sensitization or lower acute toxicity, so skin protection is still needed. Only the more severe acute toxicity category carries the skull and crossbones. Irritants can affect skin as well as eyes and airways, and the data sheet should be checked for the exact recommended protection.
  33. The correct answer is B (Review the twelve results, retest with acceptable reagent where needed and correct any errors): Results produced since the last acceptable control may be affected, so each is reviewed and retested with controlled reagent as required, and incorrect reports are corrected. A passing control at 0800 does not prove the reagent worked later in the day. Commenting without retesting leaves possible errors in the patient record.
  34. The correct answer is D (Homozygous expression gives the strongest antigen, so a nonreactive cell makes the antibody unlikely): Antibodies such as anti-Jka often react more strongly with cells that carry a double dose of the antigen. A nonreactive homozygous cell therefore makes the antibody unlikely and supports the rule out. A heterozygous cell can miss a weak antibody, and a negative autocontrol does not settle which antibodies are present.
  35. The correct answer is C (Factor VIII activity is lower, but other clotting factors remain adequate for most therapeutic uses): Factor VIII is one of the labile factors, so it declines somewhat when freezing is delayed to 24 hours. Factor V and the stable factors, including fibrinogen, factor IX and factor X, stay at useful levels, which is why the product is accepted for most uses. It does not become a fibrinogen concentrate, and it still corrects warfarin effect.
  36. The correct answer is C (Yes, it is usable, and the label should show Saturday at 08:00): Five days after Monday 08:00 is Saturday 08:00, so a Friday 10:00 request falls inside the expiry. The 24 hour expiry applies to plasma held as thawed fresh frozen plasma, not to the relabelled product the policy describes. Counting four days or six days misapplies the stated interval.
  37. The correct answer is D (Opening exposes the unit to bacterial contamination, so storage time is limited to reduce growth risk): Once the closed system is opened, bacteria from air, ports or hands can enter, and red cells at 1 to 6 C can still support slow bacterial growth. The shortened expiry limits how long organisms could multiply before transfusion. Potassium, oxygen affinity and light exposure are not changed by the opening itself.
  38. The correct answer is B (Wash the cord cells several times with saline, because Wharton's jelly can cause false agglutination): Wharton's jelly from the cord surface can bridge cells and mimic agglutination, so cord cells are washed several times before testing. A strong positive in a well infant with a gelatinous sample suggests this artefact. Adding reagent or warming the cells would not remove the jelly.
  39. The correct answer is A (Donor lymphocytes in the graft are making anti-A that attacks the patient red cells): Group O donor lymphocytes survive in the graft and can make anti-A against the group A recipient red cells, usually within one to two weeks. This passenger lymphocyte hemolysis gives a positive DAT with anti-A in an eluate. Recipient anti-B cannot react with group O cells because they lack B antigen.
  40. The correct answer is D (Friday at 23:59, because day 3 ends on the third calendar day after collection): Tuesday is day 0, Wednesday is day 1, Thursday is day 2 and Friday is day 3, so the specimen is valid until midnight on Friday. Counting 72 hours from the draw time gives an earlier hour than the policy allows. Starting the count a day late or counting Tuesday as day 1 shifts the expiry the wrong way.
  41. The correct answer is C (5 percent, because 24 divided by 480 equals 0.05 of the units received): The outdating rate is expired units divided by units received, multiplied by 100. Twenty-four divided by 480 is 0.05, which is 5 percent. Inverting the ratio, shifting the decimal or dividing by a rounded order quantity changes the result and misreports performance to the committee.
  42. The correct answer is B (Manage her as Rh negative and give prophylaxis, because partial D can allow anti-D formation): Discordant reagents plus a partial D result show the patient lacks part of the D antigen and may become immunized by D positive fetal cells. She is therefore treated as Rh negative for prophylaxis. Waiting for another reagent only delays protection, and a positive result with one reagent does not prove a complete D antigen.
  43. The correct answer is C (Complete an occurrence report stating the facts, because near misses reveal weaknesses before harm): Near misses show where a process can fail, and reporting them lets the laboratory fix causes before a patient is harmed. A good report describes facts and contributing factors rather than blaming a person. Silent correction loses that learning.
  44. The correct answer is D (The shortest spin that leaves a clear supernatant and the strongest reaction that resuspends cleanly): The correct spin time is the shortest one that gives a clear supernatant and the strongest reactions, since cells then pack firmly yet agglutinates still resuspend as clumps. Spinning longer than needed can make the button hard to resuspend and may shear weak reactions. A cloudy supernatant shows that cells were not fully sedimented.
  45. The correct answer is C (Disc shaped platelets scatter light, so swirling suggests viable platelets and a satisfactory pH): Swirling is produced by discoid platelets that scatter light as they move, and it correlates with a satisfactory pH and good platelet viability. Loss of swirling suggests a low pH or platelet damage. Bacteria, fibrin and free hemoglobin do not create a shimmering whorl and are recognized by colour change, clots or discoloured plasma instead.
  46. The correct answer is B (A signed physician statement that the clinical need justified release before compatibility testing): Emergency release is the physician's responsibility, so the laboratory must obtain a signed statement that the patient's need justified issue before testing finished. A technologist's verbal note does not transfer that responsibility. Consent cannot realistically be obtained from a patient in shock, and the physician statement is exactly what applies to uncrossmatched units.
  47.
