The correct answer is B (Arrange irradiation at another site or the supplier, because irradiation is required): Irradiation is what prevents transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease in an infant, and leukoreduction does not give the same protection. With two hours available the laboratory can arrange irradiation elsewhere, or obtain an already irradiated unit from the supplier. Washing does not reliably remove viable lymphocytes, and a waiver does not make the unit safe.
  48. The correct answer is D (Reactivity at 30 C or above, because a wide thermal range allows binding in vivo): Thermal amplitude, the highest temperature at which the antibody still reacts, determines whether it can bind red cells at body temperature. A high titre at 4 C is common in benign cold antibodies that do not react above about 30 C. Dispersal of clumps on warming is expected for any cold agglutinin and does not signal danger.
  49. The correct answer is A (Recentrifuge the tube, transfer clear serum to a clean tube and rerun the tests): A clot or fibrin in the sample can block the probe and cause erroneous dispensing, so the instrument aborts the test. The correct step is to remove the cause by recentrifuging and transferring clear serum to a clean tube before rerunning. Removing fibrin with a stick, overriding the alarm or diluting the serum does not give a reliable sample.
Case CHB5

A hospital transfusion service keeps red cells in a blood bank refrigerator with a continuous chart recorder and an audible alarm set at 2 C and 6 C. Policy requires red cells to be stored at 1 to 6 C. At 03:10 the alarm sounds. The recorder shows the temperature rising from 4.5 C at 02:40 to 7.8 C at 03:10. The refrigerator holds 26 red cell units, and no door opening was logged overnight. The night technologist is the only staff member present.

  1. The correct answer is B (Quarantine the units until assessed, because 7.8 C exceeds the red cell limit): A reading of 7.8 C is outside the 1 to 6 C range, so the units cannot be issued until someone with authority has assessed the excursion. Immediate discard is premature because the time out of range may be short, and continuing to issue or freezing the cells would put patients or the product at risk.
  2. The correct answer is A (Obtain medical director or supervisor review of the excursion and document the decision): Units that have been outside the storage range can be released only after an authorized person reviews the duration and peak of the excursion and records the decision. Age of the unit does not decide safety, and invented time rules are not a substitute for review. Returning the units to the faulty refrigerator would repeat the risk.
  3. The correct answer is D (Restore scheduled monthly checks with review sign-off, and add preventive maintenance of the unit): The root cause is a lapse in scheduled checks and maintenance, so the corrective action is to restore the schedule with supervisory review. The recorder and alarm worked, so a bigger recorder or tighter limits would not address the cause. Blaming one technologist ignores the system failure.
  4. The correct answer is C (A patient EDTA sample typed before conditioning, because the later ABO group may change): Recipient ABO group and antibody history before conditioning are needed to interpret later forward and reverse typing, since the recipient group can change as donor marrow engrafts. Donor typing alone does not record the recipient's original type. Samples after engraftment cannot show the baseline.
  5. The correct answer is A (Reject the specimen and request a new sample, because a discrepancy suggests mislabelling): Any difference between tube and requisition identifiers is treated as a possible wrong-blood-in-tube event, so the specimen is rejected and recollected. Laboratory staff must not alter a label they did not apply. Testing first only spends reagents and risks reporting results on the wrong patient.
  6. The correct answer is A (Keep the specimen at 37 C until separated, because cooling lets antibody adsorb onto the cells): Cold autoantibodies can adsorb onto the patient's own red cells as a specimen cools, which lowers the free antibody in the serum and gives a falsely low titre. Keeping the sample at 37 C until the clot retracts and the serum is separated avoids this loss. Chilling or refrigerating the tube before separation makes the loss worse.
  7. The correct answer is B (They allow repeat ABO, antibody and crossmatch testing if the recipient later has a reaction): The retained pair lets the laboratory repeat compatibility testing on the exact blood that was transfused if a reaction appears. They are not reagents, inventory proof or billing evidence. Their value is entirely in the investigation of adverse events.
  8. The correct answer is C (Irradiate the unit, because viable donor lymphocytes could engraft in this immunosuppressed recipient): Granulocyte concentrates contain many viable lymphocytes and the recipient is immunosuppressed, so irradiation is required to prevent graft versus host disease. A leukoreduction filter would remove the very granulocytes being transfused. The unit is stored at 20 to 24 C without agitation rather than refrigerated, and washing damages the cells and delays transfusion.
  9. The correct answer is D (Complete the antibody screen and crossmatch the issued units, and notify the physician of problems): Retrospective testing must be completed on the units already transfused so any incompatibility is found and reported. Group O red cells are ABO compatible with a group A recipient, but unexpected antibodies could still be present, so the screen and crossmatch are still required. Delaying until another order leaves a possible problem unreported.
  10. The correct answer is B (Investigate reading technique and control material, because a long run on one side signals systematic error): Ten or more consecutive results on the same side of the mean break the 10x rule and indicate a systematic shift rather than random scatter. Because nothing else changed, the reading technique, lighting or control cell handling should be checked before anything else. Staying inside 2 SD does not clear a run, and this pattern alone does not prove the control lot has deteriorated.
  11. The correct answer is D (Hold the tube at 37 C until separation, because cold antibodies bind cells when cooled): Cold agglutinins react at temperatures below body temperature, so the sample is kept near 37 C until the serum is separated from the cells. Ice or refrigeration encourages binding and autoagglutination in the tube. Room temperature of 22 C is still cool enough to let a high-titre antibody react.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Counts were reconciled but expiry checks failed, so outdated units need quarantine and process review): A matching count shows that the number of units is right but says nothing about whether stock is within its dates. Expired units must be removed from usable stock and the check that failed must be found. Expiry dates cannot be extended by inspection, and the system does not remove outdated units unless it is built to do so.
  2. The correct answer is C (Storage at 20 to 24 C favours bacterial growth, because cold storage slows most organisms): Platelets are stored at 20 to 24 C, a temperature that favours bacterial growth, whereas red cells at 1 to 6 C slow most organisms. Protein content and white cells do not explain the difference, and needle size does not decide how much skin flora enters, since skin disinfection and diversion of the first millilitres address that.
  3. The correct answer is A (Mixing risks contamination and breaks lot traceability, so each vial should be used separately): Pouring reagent between vials introduces contamination risk and makes it hard to trace which vial gave which result if a problem is found. Reagents of one lot are expected to perform the same, and they do not form a precipitate when combined. Each vial is therefore finished or discarded on its own.
Case CHB6

A 66-year-old patient with hemoglobin 68 g/L has a positive DAT (IgG) and a plasma antibody screen reacting 3+ at the antiglobulin phase with all three screening cells. The patient has not been transfused in the past 6 months. The serum was adsorbed three times with ZZAP-treated autologous cells at 37 C for 30 minutes each. After the third adsorption, the serum reacts 2+ at AHG with panel cells 4, 7 and 9 only, and all three cells are E positive. The other panel cells are E negative and nonreactive.

  1. The correct answer is A (Probable anti-E alloantibody, to be confirmed by testing E negative and E positive selected cells): A warm autoantibody is removed by repeated adsorption with ZZAP-treated autologous cells, and reactivity that remains only with E positive cells fits an alloantibody with E specificity. The next step is rule-out and testing selected E negative and E positive cells to confirm it. A pattern of exactly three E positive reactions is not typical of contaminated reagent cells.
  2. The correct answer is B (Select E negative units and crossmatch them using the adsorbed serum, because anti-E is suspected): If the residual reactivity represents anti-E and the patient lacks E, the units should lack E too. Crossmatching with the adsorbed serum, which has had the autoantibody removed, lets a true alloantibody incompatibility show instead of being hidden by the autoantibody that reacts with every unit. Ignoring the alloantibody or assuming adsorption cured it risks a hemolytic reaction.
  3. The correct answer is A (E negative autologous cells lack the antigen, so they cannot take up anti-E): Adsorption removes only antibody whose antigen is present on the adsorbing cells. E negative autologous cells cannot take up anti-E, so it stays in the serum and appears on the panel. The antigen content of the cells, not antibody strength or ZZAP treatment, determines what is removed.
  4. The correct answer is B (Report pink plasma, request a new sample from the other arm, and continue the workup): Pink plasma could mean true hemolysis or damage during a difficult draw, and the laboratory cannot tell which from this tube alone. The correct step is to report the finding, ask for a clean sample from the opposite arm and carry on with the investigation. Dismissing or diagnosing from colour alone is unsafe, and diluting the plasma hides the result.
  5. The correct answer is C (Discard the unit and record it as returned unusable, with the reason and time received): The unit came back without a temperature monitor and with a broken seal, so acceptable storage cannot be shown and policy forbids restocking. It must be discarded and recorded as returned unusable with the reason. A visual check cannot prove temperature, and the unit was not transfused.
  6. The correct answer is A (The platelet unit, because the person who collected it was not recorded at issue): The platelet unit's entry shows no recorded collector, so the record of issue does not show who took custody. The two red cell units issued together to one patient at the same time are acceptable, and the plasma entry is complete. The other entries contain every required field.
  7. The correct answer is D (Suspect a wrong blood in tube, and recollect before issuing any group specific blood): A current type that conflicts with several consistent earlier records, with both forward and reverse typing agreeing on group O, points to a wrong blood in tube collection. A fresh, properly labelled specimen is needed before any group specific blood is issued, and group O cells can cover urgent needs. Repeating reagents cannot fix a specimen from the wrong patient, and the history alone cannot assign the current group.
  8. The correct answer is B (Check both against a calibrated reference thermometer and recalibrate the one that is wrong): The 1.4 C difference exceeds the 1.0 C allowed, so one device is reading wrongly even though both readings lie inside 1 to 6 C. A calibrated reference thermometer shows which device is off so that it can be corrected. Averaging or matching one device to the other without a reference hides the true storage temperature.
  9. The correct answer is A (Unit 3, because only the antiglobulin phase reacts, suggesting a warm IgG antibody): Unit 3 is nonreactive at immediate spin and 37 C but 2+ at the antiglobulin phase, which is the pattern of an IgG antibody that sensitizes cells without direct agglutination. Unit 4 reacts at immediate spin, which indicates ABO incompatibility instead. Units 1 and 2 are compatible at every phase.
  10. The correct answer is C (Issue one unit now and release the next when needed, because ward storage is uncontrolled): Blood left outside controlled storage warms and the risk of bacterial growth increases with time. Releasing one unit at a time keeps the next unit at 1 to 6 C until it is actually needed. A ward medication refrigerator is not validated for blood and is not an acceptable holding place.
  11. The correct answer is C (Jka negative units with antiglobulin crossmatch, because an evanescent antibody can return and hemolyze): Kidd antibodies fall below detectable levels but a transfusion of antigen positive cells can trigger a rapid anamnestic rise and delayed hemolysis. Antibody history therefore requires antigen negative units and an antiglobulin crossmatch. Washing does not remove red cell antigens, and a negative screen does not remove the history.
  12. The correct answer is D (Anti-I, because adult red cells carry abundant I antigen and cord cells carry mostly i): Adult red cells carry abundant I antigen, whereas cord cells carry mostly i antigen, so anti-I reacts strongly with adult cells and weakly with cord cells. Anti-I is the typical cold autoantibody after Mycoplasma pneumoniae infection. Anti-i shows the reverse pattern, and anti-M does not explain agglutination of every adult cell.
  13. The correct answer is A (Notify the physician, issue group O red cells per emergency policy, and request a new specimen): Hemolysis in a tube at 37 C is a positive reaction and may signal a clinically significant antibody, so the physician must hear about it immediately. Group O cells under emergency release protect the bleeding patient while a clean specimen is obtained. Waiting for the new specimen or hiding the finding both put the patient at risk.
  14. The correct answer is D (Her anti-HBs antibody level, because it shows whether she has protective immunity after vaccination): A measured anti-HBs level shows whether the vaccinated worker is immune, which decides whether hepatitis B immune globulin or a booster is needed. The date of vaccination alone does not prove immunity, since some people respond poorly or lose antibody while keeping memory. Vaccine brand and blood group do not determine protection.
  15. The correct answer is B (1+, because agglutinates are mainly in the lower gel with some unagglutinated cells at the bottom): Gel reactions are graded by where the agglutinates sit in the column. Agglutinates mostly in the lower half with a few cells at the bottom are weak, which is a 1+ reaction. Stronger reactions hold cells higher in the gel, and a negative reaction shows only a button at the bottom with a clear gel.
  16. The correct answer is C (Type the stored pretransfusion sample, because it contains only the patient's own red cells): Mixed-field reactions after recent transfusion mean donor cells are still circulating. The stored pretransfusion sample has only the patient's cells, so it gives the true phenotype. Washing or dilution cannot separate the two populations, and reporting the mixed result as positive would mislabel the patient.
  17. The correct answer is A (Rh and Kidd antibodies react more strongly, while Duffy and MNS antibodies stop reacting): Enzyme treatment enhances reactions of Rh, Kidd and Lewis antibodies and destroys the antigens for Duffy and most MNS antibodies. Comparing untreated and treated panels therefore separates the antibodies in a mixture. Enzymes do not strip IgG from cells.
  18. The correct answer is B (Centrifuge and transfer the serum to a labelled tube, because prolonged clot contact can interfere): Separating serum soon after the clot forms prevents prolonged contact with cells and lets the laboratory test clean serum. Freezing a whole clotted tube lyses the cells and can compromise the sample. Anticoagulating the sample would defeat the purpose of collecting serum.
  19. The correct answer is A (A group O kidney suits all three, because it lacks A and B antigens): Group O organs express no A or B antigen, so recipients of any ABO group can receive them without an ABO barrier. The matching logic follows the same pattern as for red cells. H antigen is present on group A, B and AB tissue too, so it does not cause ABO rejection.
  20. The correct answer is A (Measure 200 mL of bleach and 1800 mL of water to reach 2000 mL): One tenth of 2000 mL is 200 mL of bleach, and the remaining 1800 mL is water. Using 2000 mL of water with 200 mL of bleach produces 2200 mL at a one in eleven strength. The other two mixtures are only one twentieth and one hundredth bleach, so they are too weak for blood spills.
  21. The correct answer is C (Test it against a panel, because the remaining antibody can be identified without anti-E): Adsorption with E-positive cells that lack other common antigens removes anti-E but leaves antibodies to antigens those cells do not carry, so the remaining serum can be tested against a full panel to identify the second specificity. Eluting from the adsorbing cells would recover anti-E, and adsorption does not remove every antibody. Pooling the adsorbed serum back would restore the interference.
  22. The correct answer is D (Reject the sample and request recollection, because layered labels suggest a possible mix-up or relabelling): A label covering another patient's label raises real doubt about whose blood is in the tube, and the laboratory cannot resolve that doubt. Peeling off the label or testing anyway keeps the questionable specimen, whereas a clean recollection removes the risk.
  23. The correct answer is A (It traces earlier donations and their recipients, because those recipients may need notification and testing): A look-back identifies previous donations from a donor who now tests positive, finds any components still in inventory and traces transfused recipients so they can be informed and tested. Retesting the donor, auditing the site and deferring the donor are separate actions that do not trace earlier units.
  24. The correct answer is A (A systematic shift from a 10x violation, so review the new lot and mean): Ten consecutive control values on the same side of the mean violate the 10x rule and indicate a systematic shift, which here began soon after the new lot was introduced. The lot, the established mean and the method should be reviewed. No single point is beyond 2 SD, so random error rules are not violated, and clustering on one side is a shift and not better precision.
  25. The correct answer is D (Enter a coded K-negative requirement with source and date, verified by a second person): A coded flag is read by the system at every order, so the requirement cannot be missed. Recording the source and date, and having a second person verify it, keeps the record auditable. Free text on a paper chart or a booking comment may never reach the technologist, and waiting for the screen ignores a known history.
  26. The correct answer is B (Remove the soaked coat, because wet fabric holds blood against skin, then wash and report): Fabric soaked with blood holds infectious material against the skin, so removing it and washing the area comes before other tasks, followed by reporting the exposure. Continuing the task or dabbing the cuff leaves the contamination in place. A colleague is already handling the floor spill, so her own exposure is the priority.
  27. The correct answer is D (Offer group O Rh negative emergency release and request an urgent sample at once): A bleeding woman of childbearing potential with no known group needs group O Rh negative red cells so she is not exposed to Rh sensitization. Waiting for a paperwork field would delay life saving treatment, and guessing a group or crossmatching without a sample is unsafe. The urgent sample lets the laboratory move to group specific units quickly.
  28. The correct answer is D (Decline, because autologous units are for the donor patient only and are not crossed over): Autologous units are collected, tested and labelled for the donor patient alone, so they are not transferred to another patient even when groups match. A compatible crossmatch addresses only red cell compatibility, and an unused autologous unit is discarded under local policy.
  29. The correct answer is A (Investigate with an eluate and panel, then consult the transfusion medicine physician about units): A positive DAT and autocontrol with universal incompatibility call for further work, such as an eluate and panel, to find a masked alloantibody. The transfusion medicine physician should guide selection. Issuing blindly or ignoring the reactions risks missing a clinically significant alloantibody.
  30. The correct answer is C (Notify the supervisor and medical director, who coordinate with the supplier and clinical areas): A fall below the minimum stock level is an alert that needs prompt escalation to those who can authorize a shortage plan. The supervisor and medical director then coordinate the supplier order and the message to wards. Delaying until the shelf is empty, or contacting wards before management, leaves decisions without proper authority.
  31. The correct answer is D (Confirmed donor unit ABO and Rh, plus a hard block on incompatible ABO selections): An electronic crossmatch relies on a validated system that compares confirmed patient and donor ABO and Rh and blocks any incompatible match. It replaces the tube test only when the patient has no antibody history and a negative screen. Extra tube testing or physician signatures are not part of the criteria.
  32. The correct answer is B (Phone the ward at once to hold the unit and prevent transfusion, then report): The immediate risk is a transfusion to the wrong patient, so the first step is to reach the ward and stop use of the unit. The incident report follows once the hazard is contained. Relying on the bedside check or relabelling in the lab without a full recheck leaves the error uncontrolled.
  33. The correct answer is D (Inspect the weld for leaks and distortion, then confirm unrestricted flow before using the product): A completed weld should be inspected for leaks and distortion, and flow should be confirmed before the product is used. A cycle complete message does not prove a good weld, since the device cannot detect every tubing defect. Pulling on bags can break a good weld and does not test sterility, and colour is not a reliable indicator.
  34. The correct answer is A (About 10 units, since two divided by 0.20 gives ten units on average): If one unit in five is negative, an average of five units must be typed to find one negative. Two negative units require about 2 divided by 0.20, which is 10. Five units yields only one negative unit on average.
  35. The correct answer is C (The antibody must be identified and antigen-negative units found and crossmatched, which adds time): A positive screen leads to a panel to identify the antibody and a search for units lacking the corresponding antigen, followed by an antiglobulin crossmatch. This takes far longer than a typing and immediate-spin sequence. The other statements describe steps that are not standard requirements.
  36. The correct answer is A (Train every technologist, document their competence and sign-off, and then set the effective date): A procedure becomes effective only once all staff who will use it have been trained and their competence recorded. Staff on leave are trained on return before they perform the test. Posting the page or collecting read signatures does not show they can perform the revised step.
  37. The correct answer is B (Keep it retrievable long term, since antibody levels can fall below detection while memory persists): Kidd antibodies are well known to fall below detectable levels while the immune memory remains, so a later transfusion of antigen positive blood can trigger a rapid delayed hemolytic reaction. The history must stay retrievable long term and visible when units are selected. Removing it or hiding it in an archive defeats its purpose.
  38. The correct answer is C (A CCI of 3.6, calculated from the increment of 7 x10^9/L, showing a poor response): The increment is 13 minus 6, or 7 x10^9/L. Multiplying by the body surface area of 1.80 m2 gives 12.6, and dividing by the dose of 3.5 x10^11 gives 3.6, which is below the usual one-hour threshold of about 7.5. Using the posttransfusion count or leaving out body surface area produces different, wrong values.
  39. The correct answer is B (Pause registration until a supervisor verifies identity and the record is corrected under procedure): Two different names and birth dates for the same donor is an identity discrepancy that must be resolved before collection, because donor history, deferrals and test results are attached to one record. A supervisor should verify identity with the donor and correct the record under procedure. Creating a duplicate record or silently overwriting the old one would break the link to deferrals and prior results.
  40. The correct answer is D (Washed platelets or IgA-deficient donor products, because anti-IgA reacts with IgA in plasma): Anti-IgA in the patient binds IgA contained in the donor plasma of the platelet unit, causing anaphylaxis. Washing removes most of the plasma, or units from IgA-deficient donors can be used. Irradiation and leukoreduction do not remove plasma proteins, and ABO matching does not address this mechanism.
  41. The correct answer is C (The panel worksheet of cells tested and antibodies excluded, because it prevents repeated work): The worksheet records which cells were tested, what reactions occurred and which antibodies were ruled out, so the next technologist continues where the first stopped. A suspected result without evidence forces repetition. Time estimates and general remarks carry no information about the actual panel.
  42. The correct answer is B (25 Gy at the central plane, and no part of the unit below 15 Gy): The accepted standard is a minimum of 25 Gy delivered to the central plane of the container, with no point in the unit receiving less than 15 Gy, so that donor lymphocytes cannot proliferate. Lower doses may fail to inactivate T cells, and much higher doses damage red cell membranes and raise potassium leakage without added benefit.
  43. The correct answer is C (Rh negative red cells, because a partial D phenotype could form anti-D after exposure): Serology cannot separate weak D from partial D, and a partial D patient lacks some D epitopes and can make anti-D. Giving Rh negative cells protects a woman of childbearing potential from sensitization that could later harm a fetus. Nothing in the stem describes an emergency that would justify overriding the policy.
  44. The correct answer is B (Quarantine it and investigate, because the pink supernatant suggests hemolysis from washing): Pink supernatant means red cells have lysed, which releases potassium and free hemoglobin, a particular risk for an infant. Interrupting and repeating the wash cycle may have damaged the cells, so the unit should be quarantined and the process investigated. Extra washing, dilution or issuing with a note does not repair lysed cells.
  45. The correct answer is D (Record a double population, because two red cell groups are present in the same microtube): The microtube holds two red cell populations, one that agglutinated and stayed high and one that did not and settled to the bottom. This double population pattern matches recent transfusion of group O cells into a group A patient. It should be recorded as a double population and not as a 4+, a negative or a defective card.
  46. The correct answer is D (Provide Jka-negative units, because the antibody may have fallen below detectable levels over time): Kidd antibodies are well known to fall below detectable levels, yet immune memory remains and a transfusion of antigen-positive cells can cause a delayed hemolytic reaction. Records of clinically significant antibodies are therefore honoured by providing antigen-negative units, with an antiglobulin crossmatch as an additional check. A negative screen today does not erase that history.
  47. The correct answer is A (Strip blood into the tubing, seal sections, and label each with the unit number): Segments must hold blood from the donor unit itself so later testing, for example repeat ABO or crossmatch, reflects that unit. The tubing is stripped to fill it, sealed to create separate sections and labelled to link each to the unit number. Segments filled with saline or patient blood or left open do not represent the unit.
  48. The correct answer is B (Disclose the relationship and step aside, because transparency protects fairness in purchasing): A conflict of interest exists whenever a personal relationship could influence a work decision, even if the bid is fair. Disclosure and withdrawal from the decision protect the integrity of the process and the person. Voting last or concealing the link leaves the conflict in place.
  49. The correct answer is B (It detects wrong blood in tube errors that a single sample cannot reveal by itself): A single mislabelled tube gives a clean and consistent group that looks correct, so only a second independent sample can expose the error. The requirement is a safety control rather than a way to gain volume or extend validity. Suppliers do not require two samples.
  50. The correct answer is C (Give the result verbally, request a read-back, and record the recipient's name, date and time): Urgent or critical results are given directly to a responsible person with read-back to confirm understanding, and the recipient, time and date are documented. A note, an unconfirmed email or an undocumented call leaves no assurance the message reached someone able to act.
  51. The correct answer is C (Hold testing until the tube carries two unique identifiers, because cord samples need traceable labels): Cord samples must be traceable to the mother and the infant, because a later mix-up can lead to wrong Rh immune globulin or wrong group decisions. Labelling after testing removes the safeguard. The nurse can complete the labelling quickly, so testing is only briefly delayed.
  52. The correct answer is D (Complete an occurrence report and inform the supervisor, because reported errors drive system improvement): Near misses reveal weak points in a process before they cause harm, so quality systems expect them to be reported. Altering a record to look complete falsifies documentation. A hallway conversation leaves no trace for the quality team to review.
  53. The correct answer is B (It lets the supplier link reports and recall components, because delay leaves unsafe units available): The supplier can only link two reports to one donation and recall remaining components if each hospital reports promptly rather than waiting for final cultures. Delay leaves potentially unsafe units available to other patients. Billing and organism confirmation are not the purpose of early reporting, and hospital cultures are still needed.
  54. The correct answer is D (Repeat the DAT on a washed fresh infant sample, because contamination limits the cord result): A contaminated cord with a cloudy wash can give a falsely strong DAT, so the result cannot be accepted as it stands. A repeat on a fresh, properly washed infant sample is needed before an eluate or any diagnosis. Calling the result negative without retesting would also be wrong.
  55. The correct answer is A (Quarantine it and notify the supplier, because pink plasma suggests hemolysis from damage or contamination): Pink or red supernatant plasma in a liquid red cell unit points to hemolysis caused by rough handling, temperature damage or bacterial growth. The unit must be quarantined and the supplier informed so the cause can be investigated. Washing or issuing the unit would expose a patient to free hemoglobin and possibly endotoxin.
  56. The correct answer is A (Hold patient results, investigate the reagent and repeat the control before any testing continues): A control that fails to give its expected reaction means the reagent cannot be trusted, so patient results from that run would be unreliable. The reagent, the cell suspension and the technique are investigated and the control repeated successfully first. Being in date does not prove the reagent performs, and picking the stronger result invents a rule.
  57. The correct answer is D (An antibody to a low-prevalence antigen on that unit but missing from screening cells): Screening cells are chosen to carry common antigens, so they miss antibodies against low-prevalence antigens such as Kpa. A donor unit that happens to carry that antigen shows the incompatibility only at crossmatch. High-prevalence antibodies would make nearly every unit incompatible, and a negative autocontrol argues against an autoantibody.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Temperature exceeded 24 C from hour 8, so quarantine the three units pending review): The probe crossed the 24 C upper limit at the 8 hour reading and kept rising, so the units spent several hours outside the 20 to 24 C range. They should be quarantined and assessed under the excursion procedure before any decision. Refrigerating platelets would harm them further, and staying above the lower limit does not make the upper limit failure acceptable.
  2. The correct answer is B (The blood supplier, because donation records link the case to co-components and donors for quarantine): The blood supplier holds the donation records that link a recipient's component to its donor and to co-components from the same donation, so it must be told promptly to quarantine other units and investigate the donor. Family physicians, ward managers and licensing colleges do not hold donation records.
  3. The correct answer is A (Continue the validated method and report the concern, because unverified methods should not report results): Accreditation standards such as ISO 15189 require verification of a method before patient reporting. A disclaimer or informal comparison does not demonstrate performance. Keeping the validated method in use protects patients while the concern goes to the quality lead for resolution.
  4. The correct answer is D (She needs a fetal bleed screen and then postpartum Rh immune globulin as indicated): An Rh negative mother with an Rh positive infant and no anti-D is at risk of sensitization from fetomaternal bleeding at delivery. A fetal bleed screen identifies the dose needed, and Rh immune globulin is then given within 72 hours. The infant's ABO group does not decide Rh immune globulin eligibility, and a negative DAT does not exclude it.
  5. The correct answer is B (The woman may donate while the man is deferred, because minimums differ by sex): The man is two grams under the male minimum, while the woman is one gram above the female minimum, so only the woman qualifies. Each donor is judged against the sex-specific limit, and measurement error does not create a tolerance band around it.
  6. The correct answer is C (Anti-E, because every E positive cell reacts and E negative cells do not): Only cells 2, 3, 7 and 8 react, and these are exactly the four cells that carry E antigen, while E negative cells stay nonreactive. The two E homozygous cells give 2+ and the two heterozygous cells give 1+, which is dosage. Anti-c, anti-K and anti-Jka are each ruled out because positive cells for those antigens (cells 4 and 6, cell 5, and cells 1, 5 and 6) do not react.
  7. The correct answer is A (Samples stay refrigerated until 7 days after transfusion, because reactions may need retesting): Retained samples let the laboratory repeat ABO, antibody and crossmatch testing if the recipient reacts after the transfusion. Discarding them the same day or after one day removes that safety net, and the 30 day period does not match the stated policy. The policy period of 7 days is the one to follow.
  8. The correct answer is B (Prestorage leukoreduced red cells, because they meet the CMV-safe requirement under the stated policy): Under the stated policy, prestorage leukoreduction removes most of the leukocytes that harbour cytomegalovirus, so the leukoreduced unit is acceptable for a seronegative pregnant patient. Irradiation targets lymphocyte proliferation and does not remove or inactivate the virus. Seropositive and fresh nonleukoreduced units remain a transmission risk.
  9. The correct answer is C (BNP rose 4.5-fold with hypertension, because findings favour circulatory overload rather than lung injury): A post to pre BNP ratio above 1.5 supports circulatory overload, and here it is 4.5 (1260 divided by 280). Rising blood pressure, tachycardia and raised jugular venous pressure point to hydrostatic edema rather than the hypotension typical of lung injury. BNP is not an index of sepsis, and a 4.5-fold rise is far beyond expected variation.
  10. The correct answer is B (Quarantine the unit and call the issuing bank, because identity must be resolved): A mismatch between the bag label and the shipping documents is an identity failure, and the unit cannot be released until the issuing bank confirms which record is true. Quarantine preserves the product while the discrepancy is documented and investigated. Relabelling or accepting on paperwork alone risks infusing the wrong product.
  11. The correct answer is C (Repeat the ABO group and perform a direct antiglobulin test on the post-transfusion sample): Pink post-transfusion plasma with clear pretransfusion plasma points to intravascular hemolysis. With a clean clerical check, the laboratory repeats the ABO group and performs a direct antiglobulin test on the post-transfusion sample. Discarding the sample, calling the finding normal or limiting testing to an immediate spin crossmatch would miss the cause.
  12. The correct answer is B (UN3373, Biological substance, Category B, shipped in leakproof triple packaging with absorbent material): Human specimens with a low likelihood of containing pathogens are shipped as UN3373 Biological substance, Category B, in triple packaging with absorbent material. Category A applies only to substances known to cause permanent disability or life threatening disease in healthy people. Dry ice would add its own separate UN number, but it is not part of this shipment.
  13. The correct answer is A (The parent unit's original expiry, because the closed system maintains sterility): When aliquots are made through a sterile connecting device, the system stays closed, so the component keeps the expiry of the parent unit. Only an open system, in which sterility cannot be assured, shortens expiry to 24 hours for red cells or 4 hours for pooled platelets.
  14. The correct answer is A (Repeat only patient four's screen from the beginning, because the failure applies to those tubes): Check cell failure in only one patient's tubes points to an error confined to those tubes, for example an incomplete wash or missing AHG. Five valid checks show that the reagent lot and the general procedure were sound. Only the affected screen needs repeating, and it cannot be released as negative.
  15. The correct answer is C (Two days, because 12 minus the 6 reserve leaves 6 units at 3 per day): Only units above the reserve may be used routinely: 12 minus 6 leaves 6 units. At 3 units per day that is 6 divided by 3, or two days. Dividing all 12 units by the daily use counts the reserve, which would leave nothing for emergencies.
  16. The correct answer is B (It shows free antibody was removed, because a nonreactive last wash supports the eluate): The last wash is the final rinse before elution, so if it reacts with no panel cell, any reactivity in the eluate came from antibody released from the red cells. A reactive last wash means residual serum antibody remains and the eluate result is not valid. It is a negative control, not a positive one, and it says nothing about temperature or antibody quantity.
  17. The correct answer is A (April 10, which is 28 days after irradiation and earlier than the original expiry): March 13 plus 28 days is April 10, since 18 days reach March 31 and 10 more follow. Because April 10 is earlier than the original expiry of April 14, it governs. Irradiation increases potassium leakage from red cells during storage, which is the reason the shelf life is shortened. Counting from collection or adding the full 42 days would give dates the policy does not allow.
  18. The correct answer is D (Tube 5, because EDTA anticoagulates the sample and preserves red cells for testing): The EDTA tube, numbered 5, is the lavender or purple cap and is the usual pretransfusion specimen when plasma is used. The red cap tube gives serum and is not the specimen named here. Heparin and citrate tubes can interfere with testing and are used for other purposes.
  19. The correct answer is A (Merge the records under controlled procedure and confirm the group on a new sample): A controlled merge keeps all earlier results and unit history attached to the one patient. A confirmatory sample under the permanent identity adds a further check against identification errors. Hand relabelling, treating the patient as new or deleting the old record would lose or confuse the history.
  20. The correct answer is C (6.7 percent, because 12 discarded units divided by 180 received equals 0.067): Wastage includes every unit discarded for any reason, so expiry (9) and the alarm loss (3) together give 12 units. Dividing 12 by 180 received units gives 6.7 percent. Counting only expiry or only the alarm loss understates the loss, and dividing by an issue count uses the wrong denominator.
  21. The correct answer is C (Several points beyond 1 SD on one side break the 4 1s rule, so investigate): Five consecutive points lie more than 1 SD above the mean, which breaks the 4 1s rule and signals a systematic shift even though no point exceeds 2 SD. Results should be held while reagent, cell suspension and technique are investigated. Staying inside 2 SD does not rule out a run, and the plotted pattern is neither a 10 point run nor a seven point trend.
  22. The correct answer is A (EDTA binds calcium, which blocks complement activation and in vitro hemolysis of cells): Complement activation needs calcium and magnesium, so EDTA stops complement from binding to cells or causing in vitro hemolysis. It does not concentrate antibodies, promote clotting or dissolve immunoglobulin. This is why plasma gives cleaner reaction readings than serum in many methods.
  23. The correct answer is D (The medial or lateral plantar surface of the heel, away from the posterior curvature): The medial or lateral plantar surface avoids the calcaneus, which lies close to the skin at the posterior curve and can be injured and cause osteomyelitis. The arch has nerves and tendons, and a bruised site gives poor flow and risk of infection. Choosing the safe zone is a basic rule of neonatal collection.
  24. The correct answer is C (Move them to a labelled quarantine area and record the action before anything else): Affected units are separated from usable stock immediately so they cannot be issued by mistake, and the action is recorded. Disposition such as return or destruction follows the instructions in the notice and the medical director's direction. Issuing them would expose patients to the risk that prompted the recall.
  25. The correct answer is C (Collect fresh EDTA and clotted tubes, because heparin interferes with molecular HLA typing): Heparin inhibits the amplification step of molecular HLA typing and is not an acceptable anticoagulant for serum-based antibody testing, so fresh tubes are needed. EDTA or ACD whole blood supports typing, and a clotted tube gives serum for antibody work. Re-clotting plasma or cooling it does not remove heparin.
  26. The correct answer is A (Passive anti-D from the injection that is slowly declining, so she is not actively immunized): Passive anti-D from the injection appears soon after the dose and then declines slowly over weeks, which matches the falling and stable titre. Active immunization would produce a rising titre rather than a decline. Detectable anti-D alone does not prove fetal risk when its origin is a documented dose.
  27. The correct answer is B (Platelets have a shelf life of five to seven days, so unused units expire quickly): Platelets are kept at room temperature with agitation and expire after about five to seven days, whereas red cells keep for up to 42 days in additive. A short shelf life makes expiry the leading wastage reason for platelets even when ordering is good. Platelets are not stored in the refrigerator, and release testing and batch ordering are not the main cause.
  28. The correct answer is C (Quarantine the two units, trace the third to the ward, and notify the medical director): Recalled units in stock are quarantined, not discarded, until the supplier gives disposition instructions. The issued unit must be traced so the ward can stop or investigate its use, and the medical director must be told. Leaving follow-up to the supplier alone, or returning units to stock, puts patients at risk.
  29. The correct answer is A (The antiglobulin reagent failed, so quarantine it and investigate before any antiglobulin testing): Every grouping reagent gave the expected positive and negative reactions, but the antiglobulin reagent did not agglutinate IgG-coated cells, so it cannot detect IgG on patient cells. It must be removed from use and investigated, for example for expiry, contamination or neutralization. Negative control reactions alone never prove a vial is working.
  30. The correct answer is A (Use distilled water, change it on schedule, and keep the level above the tube contents): Distilled or deionized water prevents the mineral film and growth that cloud the bath and foul thermometers, and a schedule for emptying and cleaning keeps it in good condition. The water level must be above the level of the contents in the tubes so the whole reaction mixture is at 37 C. Tap water deposits minerals, and waiting for visible dirt delays needed maintenance.
  31. The correct answer is D (Decline and refer the caller to the care team, because release needs patient consent): Provincial health privacy law such as PHIPA lets a custodian release identifiable results only to the patient, an authorized agent or the care team. A telephone caller who cannot be verified has no authority, and a birth date or a call back number does not prove consent. A blood group is still personal health information once it is linked to a named patient.
  32. The correct answer is C (Explain results cannot be shared without consent and direct the caller to the care team): A blood group and antibody history are personal health information, and unverified callers have no entitlement to them. A birth date is easy to guess and does not prove consent. Directing the caller to the care team lets the patient decide who is told.
  33. The correct answer is D (A near miss, because the error was caught before blood reached the patient): The error was detected before any blood was issued, so it is a near miss: an event that could have caused harm but did not. Near misses are recorded and reviewed because the same process failure could lead to a true transfusion error. Reporting does not depend on harm having occurred, and the event is not classed as a reaction.
  34. The correct answer is B (Test it in a separate environment with documented cases, then approve go-live after review): A change that affects result interpretation must be tested with documented test cases before it reaches the live system, and approved through change control. The vendor's own testing does not replace local validation, and testing after installation exposes patients to unvalidated software. Patches that change behaviour need validation like any other change.
  35. The correct answer is A (The reagent is diluted to one third strength, which can weaken or miss reactions): The final volume would be about 0.3 mL, so the 0.1 mL of reagent is diluted to about one third of its strength. Diluted antihuman globulin may fail to agglutinate cells carrying small amounts of IgG or complement, so weak reactions can be missed. Residual saline does not strengthen the reagent or lyse the cells.
  36. The correct answer is A (Continue under the alias, issue group O red cells until typing is confirmed, and merge formally): Records keep the alias that matches the wristband and tube until registration performs a formal merge, so the specimen identity is not changed informally. Emergency group O red cells are issued while the type is confirmed. Relabelling or double testing creates identification risk.
  37. The correct answer is B (Lids contain aerosols and spills if a tube breaks during the spin): Sealed buckets trap the aerosols and droplets released if a tube cracks at speed, which protects staff from infectious material. They do not control temperature, balance the load or change the force on the cells. Capped tubes can still break or leak under centrifugal force.
  38. The correct answer is B (Do not incubate, because the 34.6 C well falls below the 35 C minimum): Every well must read within 35 to 39 C, because gel card reactions depend on a stable 37 C incubation. The 34.6 C well is under the minimum, so cards must not be incubated until the cause is found and the temperature is verified again. An average, or adjusting the setting without investigation, would hide a real fault.
  39. The correct answer is D (Platelet refractoriness is possible, so the cause is investigated, including possible antibodies to HLA antigens): Two consecutive poor one hour increments suggest refractoriness, which may be immune, for example HLA antibodies, or related to non immune factors. Investigation and HLA matched or crossmatched platelets may then be considered. A single low value could be a timing issue, but repeated poor results require evaluation, and halving the dose would not improve the response.
  40. The correct answer is B (An irradiated label showing the irradiation date, placed clear of the donation number): Modifications such as irradiation must be shown on the component by a label with the date, and the original donation number must stay readable so the unit can be traced to its donor and collection. Replacing or covering the supplier label, or assigning a new donation number, breaks that traceability, and a note on a tag is not a label on the unit itself.
  41. The correct answer is C (Bacteria and food carry A-like sugars, so people lacking A antigen make anti-A after birth): Anti-A and anti-B form in early infancy in response to A-like and B-like sugars on bacteria and in food, in people who lack the matching antigen. Transfusion or pregnancy is not required, and maternal IgG fades within months, so it cannot explain an adult's antibody. B and A antigens are different sugars and do not stimulate each other's antibody.
  42. The correct answer is B (Unbound globulins neutralize the antiglobulin reagent, so a true weak D reaction may be missed): Without washing, free serum globulins neutralize the antiglobulin reagent and a weak D reaction can be missed, giving a false negative. Adding check cells to negative tubes would reveal this failure by showing no agglutination. The shortcut does not cause hemolysis or shorten the incubation.
  43. The correct answer is D (Retrieve the record from the archive, since the ten year period has not yet expired): A transfusion in March 2020 is about six years old, so under a ten year policy the record must still be retained and retrievable. The laboratory remains responsible for it even if the patient moved. Destroying or reconstructing it would breach the policy and defeat the audit.
  44. The correct answer is D (Group O D negative cells compatible with maternal plasma, reconstituted with group AB plasma): Cells for exchange must lack the antigen that maternal antibody targets and be compatible with maternal plasma, so group O D negative is selected, and group AB plasma avoids ABO issues in the reconstituted unit. D positive cells would be attacked by maternal anti-D still circulating in the infant.
  45. The correct answer is B (1 to 10 C, because units may warm slightly in transit but must not freeze): Red cell units may be transported between 1 and 10 C because brief warming during transport is tolerated, provided the units are not allowed to freeze, which would cause hemolysis. The storage refrigerator range of 1 to 6 C is narrower than the transport range. Room temperature and frozen conditions would both damage red cell viability.
  46. The correct answer is C (Patient 1, because the specimen from 8 March passed its day three limit): With the draw date as day zero, the specimen drawn on 8 March reaches day three on 11 March and expired at midnight, so it cannot be used on 12 March. The specimen from 9 March reaches day three on 12 March and is still acceptable. The patient without recent exposure follows the seven day limit, so that specimen is also valid.
  47. The correct answer is D (Complete the report as the procedure requires, and explain that reporting is not optional): Reporting duties are set by the hospital procedure and the program, not by one physician's opinion, and mild reactions still contribute to rates and trends. A negative direct antiglobulin test supports a febrile reaction but does not remove the duty to report. Sign-off and admission criteria are not conditions in the procedure.
  48. The correct answer is B (The unique donation number on the unit label, recorded at issue and again at transfusion): The donation number is printed on every unit label and is captured at issue and at transfusion, so it links a donor record at the supplier to a patient record at the hospital. Patient numbers are not known to the supplier, and a blood group or a collection time is shared by many different units.
  49. The correct answer is B (As a privacy breach, because access requires a direct role in the patient's care): Health privacy legislation such as PHIPA limits access to personal health information to those who need it to provide or support care. Curiosity or friendship is not a care purpose. Viewing alone, without copying, is still unauthorized access that can be traced in the audit log.
  50. The correct answer is D (Notify the physician and medical director at once, and document the result and all notifications): An incompatibility on a unit already transfused may signal a hemolytic reaction or delayed problem, and the clinical team needs to know immediately so the patient can be monitored. The notification and result must be documented. Repeating the test first delays communication, and a worksheet entry alone leaves the physician uninformed.
  51. The correct answer is B (More than one alloantibody, so selected cells should be used to separate each specificity): When no one antigen accounts for every reactive cell and every nonreactive cell, the 3 and 3 rule cannot be met for a single specificity, which suggests two or more alloantibodies. Selected cells that carry one suspected antigen but lack the other are then used to separate them. A negative autocontrol makes an autoantibody unlikely, and dosage cannot explain reactions on cells lacking the antigen.
  52. The correct answer is D (Quarantine remaining components from the donation, phone the supplier now, then send the written report): A suspected transfusion-transmitted infection with a death is a serious event, so the procedure requires immediate telephone contact followed by a written report, and other components from the donation are quarantined. Waiting for an autopsy or a causation decision delays protection of other recipients.
  53. The correct answer is C (Suspect bacterial contamination, quarantine the unit and alert the supervisor so it can be cultured): Cells that are darker than their segment, together with gas bubbles, are classic visual signs of bacterial growth. The unit must be isolated and the supervisor informed so cultures and a supplier report can be completed. Discarding it at once would lose evidence needed to protect other recipients of components from the same donation.
  54. The correct answer is C (Antibody levels can fall below detection, but re-exposure can trigger a rapid antibody rebound and hemolysis): Kidd antibodies commonly decline to undetectable levels, but memory remains. A Jka-positive transfusion can cause a quick anamnestic response and delayed hemolysis. The negative screen is a valid result but does not erase the clinical history.
  55. The correct answer is B (Anti-Fya is favoured, because proteolytic enzymes such as ficin destroy Duffy antigens on red cells): Duffy antigens Fya and Fyb are destroyed by ficin and papain, so anti-Fya reactions vanish with treated cells. Kidd antigens are enhanced by enzymes, so anti-Jka would react as well or more strongly. A cold autoantibody would not be expected to produce this specific pattern.
  56. The correct answer is B (Reactivity is lost by 30 C, so the amplitude is low and significance is unlikely): The score falls to 0 at 30 C, so the antibody does not react at or near body temperature and its thermal amplitude is below 30 C. Antibodies that react only below roughly 30 C are generally not clinically significant for transfusion. The strong reaction at 4 C reflects the usual cold optimum and does not by itself signal a risk in vivo.
  57. The correct answer is A (Quarantine it and notify the supplier, because the donor's antibody may be present in it): Other components made from the same donation may carry the same implicated antibodies, so they are quarantined and the supplier is told so that the donor can be investigated. Irradiation does not neutralize antibodies, and washing platelets is not a validated way to make a suspect unit safe. Leaving the unit available risks a second patient being harmed.
  58. The correct answer is B (To remove run-to-run variation so a true rise of two dilutions can be judged): Testing both samples in one run removes day-to-day technical variation, so a difference of two or more dilutions can be accepted as a real rise. Here 16 to 64 is a two-dilution increase, which is significant, and averaging or rechecking specificity does not address variation.
  59. The correct answer is B (75 mL/h, because 300 mL divided by 4 hours gives the hourly rate): Dividing the 300 mL volume by the 4 hours allowed gives 75 mL/h, the slowest rate that still finishes within the time limit for a patient at risk of overload. The faster rates come from dividing by shorter times, and 60 mL/h would take 5 hours, which exceeds the time a unit may remain out of controlled storage.
  60. The correct answer is D (Label it autologous only and store it apart, because only she may receive it): Autologous units are collected for the patient alone, so they are labelled for autologous use with her identifiers and kept apart from general inventory to prevent them being given to anyone else. ABO and Rh typing is still done, and treating the unit as an ordinary donation risks misdirected use.
  61. The correct answer is B (Secure them upright and keep them from heat, because they hold gas under pressure): The gas cylinder pictogram identifies gases under pressure, including refrigerated liquefied gases, so containers are secured upright to prevent falling and kept away from heat. Laying cylinders flat does not reduce pressure, and sealing them in an airtight cabinet traps leaking gas. Warming a cylinder raises the pressure and the risk of rupture.
  62. The correct answer is C (The ratio exceeded the policy limit from month 5 onward, so ordering needs review): The month 4 value of 1.9 is still under the limit of 2.0, and month 5 at 2.4 is the first value above it, with the rise continuing through month 8. A rising ratio after a new program suggests over ordering, which calls for review with the surgical teams. Suspending crossmatching would endanger patients and is not a quality response.
  63. The correct answer is D (Deterioration of the control or card lot, so test fresh control and a new lot): With temperature, cells and technique checked, the remaining likely sources are the control material and the card lot, so each is replaced and tested. A control does not contain patient plasma, so patient interference is not relevant. Repeating with the same materials cannot show whether they are the cause.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Remove the vial from use, because reduced reactivity could miss weak antigens and misgroup patients): A typing reagent that gives a much weaker reaction than expected may fail to detect a weak antigen, so patients could be grouped incorrectly. The vial must be withdrawn and patients tested with a reagent that passes control. A passing negative control does not prove strength, and adding more reagent or accepting a borderline repeat does not restore validated performance.
  2. The correct answer is C (Patient 2, because the forward and reverse groups agree and anti-D shows no agglutination): Patient 2 has no reaction with anti-A, anti-B or anti-D and both reverse cells are 4+, so the forward and reverse groups are both consistent with group O Rh negative. Patient 1 is D positive. Patient 3 is group A. Patient 4 has no reaction with A1 cells, so the reverse group is missing an expected anti-A and needs investigation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Pink-red plasma, because free hemoglobin can mask or mimic hemolysis in a reaction): Free hemoglobin in a hemolyzed sample colours the plasma pink to red and can hide a true hemolytic reaction or imitate one in the test tube. Lipemia and icterus may affect reading in automated methods, but they do not mimic the hemolysis endpoint. A normal sample shows clear pale yellow plasma.
  4. The correct answer is C (The band number ties all samples and units to one record until identity is confirmed): The trauma number is the single identifier shared by the wristband, requests, samples and issued units. Keeping it until identity is formally confirmed prevents records from splitting or mixing. A name guessed from belongings is not verified, and the band never shows a blood group or changes with each unit.
  5. The correct answer is C (Test the single small sample for all needed pretransfusion tests, because extra draws worsen the anemia): A preterm infant has a very small blood volume, and repeated draws deepen the anemia, so one minimum-volume sample should serve all required tests. Waiting or drawing more blood harms the infant without improving testing. Microsamples are validated for serology.
  6. The correct answer is B (Scrub for the full contact time, let it dry, and do not repalpate the site): Skin disinfection needs the full contact time stated in the procedure, and the site must dry so the antiseptic can kill bacteria that could contaminate the unit. Touching the site again after the scrub reintroduces bacteria from the collector's glove. Shortened scrubs, a single quick wipe or a saline rinse do not give the same reduction in skin flora.
  7. The correct answer is B (Stop ABO testing with that reagent, investigate the cause and review the overnight groupings): A group A control should react strongly with anti-A, so no reaction means the reagent or instrument failed the quality check. Testing must stop until the cause is found, and groupings reported since the last passing control must be reviewed because they may be wrong. Repeating once and moving on, or relying on the reverse grouping, does not meet quality requirements.
  8. The correct answer is C (A cold IgM antibody, usually insignificant because it fails to react at 37 C): Reactions that appear only at immediate spin and are lost at 37 C and the antiglobulin phase are characteristic of cold IgM antibodies. These are usually not clinically significant unless they react at 37 C. Rh antibodies are IgG and react at the antiglobulin phase.
  9. The correct answer is B (Issue the A1 unit after the antiglobulin crossmatch, because cold-reactive anti-A1 is not clinically significant): Anti-A1 that reacts only below 37 C and does not appear at the antiglobulin phase is not clinically significant, so a compatible antiglobulin crossmatch makes the A1 unit acceptable under the stated policy. It does not destroy A1 cells in vivo. Neither a warning label nor adsorption is needed.
  10. The correct answer is A (Residual serum proteins neutralized the AHG, so repeat the testing using the full washing procedure): Unwashed serum globulins neutralize antiglobulin reagent, so the check cells fail to react and the negative result is invalid. The test must be repeated with the full washing procedure. Reporting the tubes as negative would risk missing a clinically significant antibody.
  11. The correct answer is D (Quantify the fetal bleed by Kleihauer-Betke or flow cytometry, because the dose must be calculated): A positive rosette screen means the fetal bleed is larger than one standard dose may cover, and the controls show the test is valid. Quantitation then gives the volume needed to calculate additional vials. Issuing one vial without a count risks under-dosing.
  12. The correct answer is A (13.0, calculated as the count rise times body surface area divided by platelet dose): The count rise is 38 minus 12, which is 26. Multiplying by 1.6 gives 41.6, and dividing by 3.2 gives a CCI of 13.0. Leaving out body surface area gives 8.1, using the post count instead of the rise gives 19.0, and dividing by the surface area gives 5.1.
  13. The correct answer is C (Newborns have not made anti-A or anti-B, so reverse grouping is not expected to work): Anti-A and anti-B production begins a few months after birth, so a neonate's reverse group is normally blank or reflects maternal IgG only. Neonatal ABO typing therefore relies on the forward group, and the reverse group is not used to confirm it. The result does not indicate group AB, because the forward group shows A antigen without B.
  14. The correct answer is A (Carryover of antibody or cells that could cause false positives or mask real reactions): A used tip carries traces of the previous sample, and antibody or cells transferred this way can create a false reaction or dilute a real one. Single use tips are a basic contamination control in serology. Temperature, volume and static charge are not the reason tips are changed between samples.
  15. The correct answer is C (Between 30 and 37 C, because higher heat damages clotting factors and cooler heat slows thawing): Plasma is thawed at 30 to 37 C. Higher temperatures denature heat-labile coagulation factors such as factor VIII, and cooler temperatures only prolong the wait for a patient who needs the unit. Thawing at 1 to 6 C or room temperature is not an approved method.
  16. The correct answer is A (Four vials, because 85 mL divided by 30 mL gives 2.83, rounds to 3, plus one): The estimated fetal bleed is 1.7 x 50 = 85 mL, and dividing by 30 mL per vial gives 2.83. Rounding to 3 and adding the policy extra vial gives four vials. Dividing by 15 mL confuses the red cell volume covered with the whole blood volume covered.
  17. The correct answer is B (The unit donation number and recipient identifiers, so the segment links to the transfusion): A retained segment is useful only if it can be matched to one donor unit and one recipient. The donation number ties it to the unit, and the recipient identifiers tie it to the patient. Ward location, nurse initials or the issue date alone cannot prove which unit the segment came from.
  18. The correct answer is C (Antibodies can fall below detection, so history guides antigen negative and special product selection): Antibody levels can drop below what the screen can detect, and a later exposure can trigger a rapid anamnestic response, so the historical anti-Jka still requires Jka negative red cells. The record also carries special requirements such as irradiation. Current ABO and Rh typing is still repeated on every specimen, and consent is not part of this record check.
  19. The correct answer is C (Non immune consumption, such as fever or sepsis, because the early response was adequate): A good one hour increment shows the platelets entered the circulation, so the later fall points to rapid consumption or clearance from fever, sepsis, bleeding or similar causes. Immune refractoriness usually gives a poor increment at one hour as well. A body surface area error would affect both values and would not explain the difference.
  20. The correct answer is A (Press stop, let the rotor stop fully, then check the load balance): An unbalanced load can throw a tube or damage the rotor, so the run should be stopped with the controls and the rotor allowed to come to rest before the lid is opened. Touching the unit or opening the lid while the rotor spins risks injury. Once it is stopped, the load can be rebalanced and the equipment problem reported.
  21. The correct answer is A (Replace part of the volume with plasma, because albumin exchange has removed clotting factors): Repeated albumin exchanges remove plasma proteins, including fibrinogen and clotting factors, and albumin replaces none of them, so the INR climbs and fibrinogen falls. Adding plasma or cryoprecipitate for part of the replacement restores factors. Saline also lacks factors, and platelets are not the problem shown by these tests.
  22. The correct answer is D (Notify the physician immediately about the incompatibility, then investigate with a panel and DAT): An incompatible retrospective crossmatch suggests a possible clinically significant antibody in a patient already exposed to the unit. The physician must know right away so the patient can be monitored. Investigation proceeds in parallel and should never delay that call.
  23. The correct answer is A (Biohazardous infectious material that can cause disease in people, so contents need safe handling): The trefoil of crescents inside a circle is the Canadian WHMIS pictogram for biohazardous infectious material, which suits containers holding used blood and reagent red cells. The flame, corrosion and flame over circle symbols each describe a chemical hazard and have different shapes. Matching the shape to the hazard class lets staff choose suitable handling and disposal.
  24. The correct answer is D (Report possible bacterial contamination, notify the supplier, and request culture of the returned bag): Rigors, a large temperature rise and hypotension with platelets, which are stored at room temperature, raise concern for bacterial contamination, and normal hemolysis and DAT results make ABO incompatibility unlikely. Culture of the bag and notification of the supplier protect other recipients of the same donation. A febrile nonhemolytic label is a diagnosis of exclusion and should not close the file.
  25. The correct answer is C (Group A red cells crossmatched per policy, since the patient's group is confirmed): Once the ABO group is confirmed and policy allows, type-specific red cells conserve group O stock and are fully compatible. Group AB or B red cells would be attacked by the patient's anti-B, so only group A or group O can be selected.
  26. The correct answer is C (Peer assessors compare records and practice with the standard and issue findings needing written responses): Assessment is a review of the laboratory's records, practice and staff against the standard, followed by findings that the laboratory must answer with corrective actions. Assessors do not take over testing, pre-approve each procedure or write hospital policy.
  27. The correct answer is A (Report each monospecific result with its strength, as IgG coating alone narrows the possible causes): The pattern of IgG alone, IgG with complement, or complement alone points toward different causes such as warm autoantibody, drug effect or cold autoantibody. Giving each monospecific result with its strength lets the physician interpret the positive. Withholding or merging results removes information that guides the work-up, and an eluate is a further test, not a condition for releasing a DAT.
  28. The correct answer is A (About 10 units, because 3 divided by the combined frequency 0.31 is nearly 10): Independent frequencies multiply, so 0.91 x 0.34 is about 0.31, meaning roughly 31 percent of random units lack both antigens. Three units divided by 0.31 gives 9.7, which rounds up to 10 units to screen. Dividing by one frequency alone, or subtracting the frequencies, ignores that both antigens must be absent in the same unit.
  29. The correct answer is D (In an insulated container at room temperature without cold packs, because cold damages platelet function): Platelets are stored at 20 to 24 C and must not be chilled, because cold causes shape change and rapid clearance after transfusion. A short trip is covered by an insulated container at room temperature. Warming above room temperature promotes bacterial growth and is not used.
  30. The correct answer is C (Corrosive material that burns skin and eyes and attacks metals, needing splash protection): The symbol of liquid dripping onto a hand and a metal bar is the corrosion pictogram, used for products that damage skin, eyes or metals. Concentrated bleach is handled with gloves, eye protection and compatible containers for that reason. The flame, flame over circle and skull pictograms describe fire, oxidizing and acute toxicity hazards instead.
  31. The correct answer is A (Acceptable, because platelets belong at 20 to 24 C with constant agitation): Platelet concentrates are stored at 20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation to keep them oxygenated and prevent clumping. Refrigeration damages platelets, and removing agitation shortens their survival. A reading of 22 C with the agitator running meets requirements.
  32. The correct answer is C (Pink plasma can hide or mimic a true hemolytic reaction in the test tube): Hemolysis of red cells in a test tube is itself a positive reaction for some complement-binding antibodies, so pink plasma can conceal that finding or imitate it. Free hemoglobin does not change the ABO group, does not carry blood group antigens and does not neutralize antibodies. Rh typing is read by agglutination and is exposed to the same masking problem.
  33. The correct answer is A (Hold the unit, tell the oncologist the CMV-safe need is unmet, and request another unit): Irradiation prevents graft versus host disease but does not remove or inactivate cytomegalovirus, and antigen typing has no bearing on CMV risk. Without leukoreduction or a seronegative donor the unit does not meet the order, so the physician must be told and a suitable unit requested. Bedside filtration is not a validated way to create a CMV-safe component.
  34. The correct answer is C (Stop and clarify the identity discrepancy with the nurse first, because wrong patient errors are dangerous): A name conflict between a request and a requisition is a warning sign of possible wrong patient identification. The technologist must resolve it before any testing starts. Processing either name or both without clarification risks transfusing the wrong blood.
  35. The correct answer is C (An IgG autoanti-P that binds cells in the cold and activates complement at 37 C): The Donath-Landsteiner antibody is a biphasic IgG autoanti-P that attaches to red cells at low temperature and causes complement-mediated lysis when the blood warms to 37 C. It is the cause of paroxysmal cold hemoglobinuria, and it differs from IgM agglutinins that act without a biphasic step.
  36. The correct answer is D (Perform a manual serologic crossmatch, because the validated electronic logic is unavailable): Without the validated software the laboratory falls back on a manual serologic crossmatch, because the electronic logic cannot be relied on. A spreadsheet is not a validated system, delaying all issue can harm patients, and a paper group record alone does not show compatibility.
  37. The correct answer is B (Saline replacement after the first spin, because rouleaux disperses in saline while true agglutination persists): Saline replacement removes the serum after the first spin and adds saline, which disperses rouleaux while true antibody agglutination remains. Dextran infusion is a classic cause of rouleaux. Warming and antiglobulin reagent do not reliably disperse rouleaux, and a heavier cell suspension does not overcome protein stacking.
  38. The correct answer is A (Stop the collection, remove the needle, and lay her flat with legs raised): These signs point to a vasovagal reaction, so the first priority is donor safety: stop the collection, remove the needle and lay the donor flat with her legs raised to restore blood flow to the brain. The unit can be dealt with afterwards according to procedure. Sitting or walking the donor risks a fall, and a drink helps only once she has recovered.
  39. The correct answer is D (Adjust the eluate to pH 7 and retest, because negative check cells invalidate the run): Check cells that stay negative show the antiglobulin test did not work, here because an eluate left near pH 5 is too acidic for the reagent cells and the antiglobulin reaction. Neutralizing to about pH 7 and repeating gives a valid result. Reporting a negative eluate from an invalid run could hide a real antibody, and a different elution method would not fix the pH problem.
  40. The correct answer is B (Residual fibrin traps cells at the top of the gel, because clotting was incomplete): Fibrin from incompletely clotted samples can trap cells at the top of the gel and look like agglutination. The control column showing the same pattern points to a non-antibody cause. A true antibody would not react identically in the control, and the card spin and cell concentration would not produce this trapping.
  41. The correct answer is A (A pediatric EDTA tube, because plasma or red cells from anticoagulated blood are suitable for testing): Pretransfusion testing can use plasma and red cells from EDTA-anticoagulated blood, which fits a small pediatric container. Fluoride tubes serve glucose testing, heparin tubes are not preferred for serology and culture bottles are for microbiology. Using the correct small tube also conserves the infant's blood.
  42. The correct answer is C (The reciprocal of the highest dilution with 1+ agglutination, so a titre of 32): The titre is the reciprocal of the highest dilution that still shows 1+ agglutination, so a last positive tube at 1 in 32 gives a titre of 32. The first negative tube is not used, and the count of positive tubes depends on where the series started. Writing the dilution rather than its reciprocal is not the accepted way to report a titre.
  43.
