The correct answer is B (Negative antibody screen and no history of significant antibodies, because incompatibility is then unlikely): Electronic crossmatch needs a validated system, two ABO determinations, a negative current antibody screen and no history of clinically significant antibodies. These conditions make ABO or antibody incompatibility unlikely enough to skip the serologic crossmatch. One ABO typing, a hemoglobin level or a physician signature does not meet the requirements.
  44. The correct answer is C (A CCI of 13.0, calculated from the one-hour increment of 26 x10^9/L, showing adequate response): The increment is 34 minus 8, or 26 x10^9/L. Multiplying by the body surface area of 1.90 m2 gives 49.4, and dividing by the dose of 3.8 x10^11 gives 13.0. A value above 7.5 at one hour is considered an adequate response. Using the posttransfusion count or leaving out surface area produces different values.
  45. The correct answer is C (Notify the physician directly and document who was told, when, and by what method): When a corrected report changes the clinical meaning, the person who acted on the original must be told directly, and the notification is recorded with name, time and method. Waiting for a system flag may miss a transfusion that is about to begin. Removing the original breaks the audit trail.
  46. The correct answer is D (Decline the tube and request a new collection, because identity cannot be confirmed after the draw): A specimen must be labelled at the bedside at collection, in the presence of the patient. Labelling afterwards cannot recreate that identity check, and matching a historical type does not prove who the blood came from, so a new specimen is required.
  47. The correct answer is A (An EDTA sample labelled at the draw, because it supports typing and antibody screening): EDTA whole blood is the usual tube for ABO/Rh typing and antibody screening because it prevents clotting and keeps cells and plasma together. Citrate dilutes the sample, and heparin and fluoride tubes are not accepted for serology in most laboratories. The tube must be labelled at the time of collection with two identifiers.
  48. The correct answer is C (Mild allergic reaction, caused by recipient IgE reacting to proteins in donor plasma): Urticaria and itch without fever, hypotension or respiratory compromise indicate a mild allergic reaction to donor plasma proteins. Anaphylaxis involves airway, circulatory or multi-system signs, and febrile reactions are defined by temperature rise rather than hives. Lung injury presents with hypoxemia and bilateral infiltrates, not isolated skin findings.
  49. The correct answer is C (Request HLA-selected or crossmatch-compatible platelets, because HLA antibodies are the most likely cause): A high panel reactive antibody level with failed increments despite fresh ABO-identical units points to HLA alloimmunization, so donors selected by HLA type or by crossmatch are needed. Pooled units still expose the recipient to many HLA types, and irradiation does not remove HLA antigens. Giving more random platelets mostly feeds the antibody.
  50. The correct answer is D (Fya, Fyb, M and N antigens are destroyed, so antibodies to them stop reacting): Ficin and papain cleave certain surface glycoproteins, destroying the Duffy antigens Fya and Fyb and the M and N antigens. Rh, Kidd and Lewis reactions are enhanced, Kell antigens are unaffected, and ABO antigens remain detectable.
  51. The correct answer is A (Investigate it as a nonconformance and document corrective action, because unsatisfactory results require formal review): An unsatisfactory proficiency result is treated like any other nonconformance: the cause is investigated, such as panel interpretation or technique, and corrective action is documented. A graded result cannot be replaced, and proficiency samples are meant to be tested like patient specimens.
  52. The correct answer is A (It lets the supplier and hospital trace a unit from donor to recipient in a recall): Traceability exists so that if a donor is later found infected or a component is recalled, every recipient can be found, and if a recipient develops a transfusion infection the donor can be traced. It is not a billing tool and cannot replace repeat grouping or bedside checks, which must be performed each time.
  53. The correct answer is D (Raise the concern through the laboratory's quality reporting process, because patient safety outweighs collegial loyalty): Registrants share a duty to protect patients, which includes reporting unsafe practice through the quality system rather than waiting for harm. Quiet correction of results hides the underlying training gap and leaves the competency problem in place. Discussion among peers alone does not create a record that management can act on.
  54. The correct answer is A (Test a separately drawn sample and compare its group and Rh with the first result): An independent determination must come from a separate collection so that a wrong-blood-in-tube error would be exposed. Retesting the same tube, copying the wristband or having another person read the same tube only repeats the original source. A newly drawn sample that agrees with the earlier result confirms the group before group-specific red cells are issued.
  55. The correct answer is B (Policies follow evidence on risk, because fair access and recipient safety are both goals): Eligibility criteria are updated as evidence about transfusion-transmitted risk improves, so more people can donate safely and unnecessary exclusions are removed. They are not driven by supply pressure or local preference, and regulators review risk data when approving changes.
  56. The correct answer is A (Test them like patient samples with routine methods, without referral to another laboratory): Proficiency samples are meant to measure how the laboratory performs in everyday work, so they are tested by the usual staff with routine methods. Referring them out, repeating until the best result appears, or comparing answers with another laboratory defeats the purpose and is not accepted by accreditors.
  57. The correct answer is D (Three vials, because 50 mL calculates to 1.67 vials, rounds to two, plus one): A 1.0 percent fetal cell count with a maternal blood volume of 5000 mL means 50 mL of fetal whole blood. Dividing by 30 mL per vial gives 1.67, which rounds to two vials, and the added vial makes three. A single standard vial covers up to 30 mL of fetal whole blood, so one vial would leave this bleed undertreated.
  58. The correct answer is A (Group A, Rh positive, because forward and reverse reactions agree and anti-D is positive): Anti-A and anti-D agglutinate the patient red cells while the plasma agglutinates B cells but not A1 cells, so forward and reverse grouping agree for group A, Rh positive. A discrepancy exists only when the two sets of reactions disagree, which is not the case here. Reporting group B or Rh negative would contradict the observed red cell reactions.
  59. The correct answer is B (Stop and resolve the mismatch under the nonconformance procedure, and document it before releasing anything): Every item from one donation must carry the same donation identification number, so a mismatch is a nonconformance that has to be investigated and documented before anything is released. Correcting a number by hand, relabelling later or letting two of three numbers carry the unit all risk linking a unit or its test results to the wrong donor. Resolving it while the donor is still present allows the correct tube to be collected if needed.
  60. The correct answer is D (The test is invalid; repeat it, because antihuman globulin was neutralized or not working): IgG-coated check cells should agglutinate when antihuman globulin is added to any negative tube. A failure means the reagent was neutralized by residual unwashed serum, or the washing or reagent failed, so the earlier negative cannot be trusted. The whole test is repeated after the cause is corrected.
  61. The correct answer is B (Irradiate a fresh unit near issue or wash this one, because irradiation increases potassium leakage): Irradiation damages the red cell membrane and increases potassium leakage into the supernatant during storage, which matters in neonates receiving large volumes. Irradiating close to the time of issue or washing the unit limits the potassium load. A second dose adds more damage, and freezing does not repair the membrane.
  62. The correct answer is D (25, which is the sum of the points from all five tubes): The titration score adds the points assigned to every tube: 10 plus 8 plus 5 plus 2 plus 0 equals 25. Leaving out the weak reaction gives 23, which understates the antibody strength. The titre of 16 is a different number, taken from a single dilution, and a negative tube adds no points.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Discard the card and record the defect, because drying or bubbles can disturb the gel reaction): Dried gel or an air bubble can change how cells move during centrifugation and give a misleading pattern. A defective card should be removed from use and the defect documented for the manufacturer and quality records. Adding saline, using only part of a card or using it for control work are not acceptable repairs.
  2. The correct answer is A (Units that are C positive, c negative, E negative, e positive and K negative): Prophylactic matching gives the patient donor red cells with the same C, c, E, e and K antigens, so no foreign antigen can stimulate an antibody. Using units with antigens the patient lacks creates a risk for each mismatch, and D-negative status alone does not protect against anti-c or anti-E.
  3. The correct answer is C (Issue and disposition records linking each donation number to the patient or the discard): Issue and disposition records tie each donation number to the patient who received the component or to the reason it was discarded. That link is what makes look back possible. Temperature logs, reagent lot logs and competency files document other quality controls and cannot show which patient received a given unit.
  4. The correct answer is A (Anti-P1 is confirmed, because soluble P1 substance binds the antibody and removes its reactivity): Soluble P1 substance neutralizes anti-P1 by occupying the antibody binding sites, so the reactions are lost. Neutralization is specific to the antibody and its matching soluble antigen, so it does not prove a warm autoantibody or a different cold antibody.
  5. The correct answer is A (Order units by expiry date so the soonest expiring unit is issued first, not by receipt): Rotation by expiry date issues the product closest to outdating so it is not wasted. Arrival order does not always match expiry, which is why these new units belong ahead of older ones with later dates. Placing units for convenience or sorting by group alone ignores the outdating risk.
  6. The correct answer is D (The antibody specificity, its clinical significance, and the need for antigen negative red cells): Anti-K is an IgG antibody that can cause hemolytic reactions, so the record must carry the specificity and the need for K negative red cells. A name alone does not tell a later laboratory what to do. Titre and panel strength do not decide which red cells are suitable.
  7. The correct answer is A (Three vials, because 70 divided by 30 gives 2.3, rounded to 2, plus one): Fetal cells of 1.4 percent times 50 gives 70, and 70 divided by 30 is 2.33 vials. Rounding to the nearest whole vial gives 2, and the stated rule adds one more vial for a total of 3. Stopping at the rounded 2 vials leaves out the extra vial, and using 100 instead of 50 in the multiplication overstates the bleed.
  8. The correct answer is A (Perform an eluate and antibody identification, because IgG coating transfused cells suggests an alloantibody): A DAT positive for IgG with negative C3d, together with a new positive antibody screen and a falling hemoglobin, points to an alloantibody coating donor red cells, as in a delayed hemolytic reaction. Eluting the antibody and identifying it, with antigen typing of the transfused units, is the next step. Cold antibodies would more likely give a C3d-positive pattern, and the ABO group is unchanged.
  9. The correct answer is B (The label was applied away from the bedside, so the tube cannot be traced): The label was created from the requisition rather than from the patient, so it only proves the paperwork matches itself. Nobody can show that the blood in the tube came from the person named. Printed labels are acceptable when applied at the bedside, and clerks may handle requisitions.
  10. The correct answer is D (CSA Z902, because it is the Canadian standard for blood and blood components): CSA Z902 is the Canadian standard that addresses blood and blood components and is used by transfusion services. AABB standards are a general reference rather than Canadian law, ISO 15189 is a general laboratory quality standard, and a licence authorizes operation without listing technical steps.
  11. The correct answer is D (Reject the tube and request a correctly labelled sample, because identification must match exactly): Any discrepancy between the tube label and the requisition makes the specimen unacceptable, because identification errors are the leading cause of wrong blood in tube events. Correcting a label after the fact or testing first defeats the purpose of the check. Urgency is handled by a fast recollection or emergency release, not by relaxing identification.
  12. The correct answer is C (Telephone the ward at once with the corrected result and issue a corrected report): An incorrect group on the chart could lead to an unsafe order, so the responsible nurse needs the correction immediately. The original report is kept and marked as corrected so the history stays traceable. Waiting for a report cycle leaves the wrong result in use.
  13. The correct answer is C (Low ionic strength increases antibody uptake onto cells, so shorter incubation is possible): At low ionic strength, fewer ions shield the opposite charges on antibody and antigen, so antibody is taken up onto the cells faster. This is why LISS incubation can be about 10 to 15 minutes instead of 30 to 60. Removing water is how PEG works, and LISS does not replace antibody.
  14. The correct answer is B (Identity is verified only at the bedside during collection, and that verification cannot be repeated later): Positive patient identification happens at the bedside, and a tube whose label is wrong or missing has lost the only link to the patient that matters. Memory of the collector cannot substitute. The rule is about identity assurance, not about system locks or countersignatures.
  15. The correct answer is B (Arrange washed red cells or IgA-deficient donor components, because both avoid IgA exposure): Anti-IgA reacts with the small amount of IgA in residual plasma, so the patient needs washed red cells or components from IgA-deficient donors arranged through the supplier. Leukoreduction and irradiation do not remove plasma IgA, and ABO group has no bearing on IgA content. The transfusion is not urgent, so time exists to arrange suitable components.
  16. The correct answer is D (Reactions disappear while testing stays at 37 C, because cold antibodies bind only at lower temperatures): Prewarming keeps the reaction at physiological temperature, so a cold antibody cannot bind and the reactivity disappears. That shows the antibody has no activity at 37 C and is not clinically significant. Reactions that persist at 37 C would point to a different, warm reacting antibody.
  17. The correct answer is A (Donor group A is established and anti-A is gone, so group A cells qualify): At day 90 the forward typing shows only anti-A reactivity and the A1 cells no longer react, so recipient anti-A has disappeared and the donor group A cell line has taken over. This meets the stated policy, so group A cells may be issued. At day 30 the mixed field showed two cell populations and anti-A was still present.
  18. The correct answer is C (A signed form stating the clinical urgency and acknowledging the risk of uncrossmatched transfusion): Emergency release depends on the ordering physician accepting responsibility in writing for the risk of omitting pretransfusion testing. The form may be signed at issue or shortly afterward per policy. A crossmatch cannot exist yet, and family consent is not the release requirement.
  19. The correct answer is A (Transfusion can trigger new antibodies, so only a current specimen shows his present status): Exposure to foreign red cells within three months can stimulate a primary or secondary antibody response that was not present on the earlier screen. Only a specimen collected close to the planned transfusion reflects the antibodies that exist now. The ABO group of the patient does not change after transfusion of compatible cells.
  20. The correct answer is A (Tuesday of the following week, because day 7 falls seven days after day 0): With the collection day counted as day 0, day 7 falls exactly one week later, which is the following Tuesday, expiring at 23:59. Subtracting a day, starting the count a day late, or shortening the shelf life to four days does not match the stated rule.
  21. The correct answer is D (Patient confidentiality under provincial privacy law, because identifiable details must not be discussed publicly): Provincial health privacy laws protect identifiable personal health information, and discussing a named patient where others can hear is a breach. The other principles concern quality testing, stock levels and consent for treatment, which do not govern this conversation.
  22. The correct answer is B (Tell the supervisor, correct both records with audit entries, then notify both care teams): A misfiled result affects two patients, so both records are corrected with audit trail entries after the supervisor is told, and both care teams are informed so no decision rests on a wrong or missing result. Repeating the crossmatch is not needed when the testing was sound, and waiting for an audit leaves both charts wrong.
  23. The correct answer is A (Known reactive and nonreactive controls tested with daily reagents, giving the expected reactions): Quality control must challenge the system with materials whose results are known in advance, both reactive and nonreactive, so that false positives and false negatives are both detected. Duplicate patient results only show repeatability, and a saline wash tests nothing about reagent performance. A positive control without a negative control cannot detect false positive reactions.
  24. The correct answer is C (Investigate, retrain and reassess before independent work resumes, because competence must be shown again): A failed assessment calls for finding the reason, giving focused training and reassessing before the person works independently on that task. An eighty percent score is not acceptable when errors in grouping can kill a patient. Permanent removal is excessive, and retesting two samples alone does not demonstrate overall competence.
  25. The correct answer is C (It keeps platelets suspended and allows oxygen in and carbon dioxide out, protecting pH): Gentle agitation prevents platelets from settling into clumps and lets gases cross the gas permeable bag, so oxygen enters and carbon dioxide leaves, which stops pH from falling. It does not warm the unit, since the incubator holds 20 to 24 C, and it does not remove proteins or dissolve fibrin.
  26. The correct answer is B (Before the injection, because RhIG gives passive anti-D that would confuse a sample drawn afterwards): The antibody screen is drawn before Rh immune globulin so that any anti-D found is known to be immune rather than passive. Drawing after the dose would produce a weakly reactive anti-D that cannot be separated from sensitization. Waiting until delivery would miss the antenatal prophylaxis window.
  27. The correct answer is A (Do not report a final group until resolved, because expected anti-B is absent): Group A red cells should be accompanied by anti-B in the plasma, so the lack of reaction with B cells is an ABO discrepancy. Weak or absent antibodies are common in elderly patients, and the technologist resolves the problem with enhanced testing and review before releasing a group. Reporting group A, AB or O now would rest on results that do not agree.
  28. The correct answer is D (Stop testing, review the two reported results and repeat them after the cause is found): A failing negative control suggests contamination or a faulty reagent that may have caused false positive reactions. Patient work stops, and any results produced since the last acceptable control are reviewed and repeated if needed. A passing repeat does not remove the earlier reports from doubt.
  29. The correct answer is C (20 days from today, the original expiry, because it comes sooner than 28 days): The policy takes whichever expiry comes first. The original expiry of 20 days is earlier than 28 days after irradiation, so the original date stays on the label. Irradiation does not extend a unit beyond its original expiry, and the 28 day figure is a cap, not a period that is added or subtracted.
  30. The correct answer is D (Turbid plasma hides supernatant hemolysis, so a lysed reaction could be overlooked during reading): Lipemia makes the plasma cloudy, which makes it hard to see the pink colour of hemolysis in the supernatant. Fat does not lyse reagent cells or coat red cells. Turbidity is common after lipid infusions and does not by itself mean infection.
  31. The correct answer is B (Close and replace it with an empty one, because overfilling raises needlestick risk): Sharps containers must be closed and replaced at the fill line because overfull containers expose workers to needlestick injury. Pushing contents down or reaching in with a hand is a common cause of injuries. Transferring loose sharps between containers also creates exposure.
  32. The correct answer is A (Quarantine remaining components from the donation and notify the supplier for a donor investigation): Transfusion-related acute lung injury is linked to donor antibodies, so the supplier must investigate the donor and other components from the same donation must be quarantined. Normal hemolysis and DAT results rule out a hemolytic reaction but do not exclude lung injury. Calling it allergic or hemolytic would misdirect the report.
  33. The correct answer is C (Label each person's tubes at the chair before the next draw, because mix-ups ruin baselines): Labelling each person's tubes in front of that person before the next draw keeps the identity link intact and is the main protection against mixed-up baseline samples. Sharing the same group does not distinguish the tubes, and pre-labelling or relying on draw order invites errors. Baseline patient samples cannot be recollected after conditioning has begun.
  34. The correct answer is A (Gamma irradiation of the cellular component, because it stops donor lymphocytes from dividing): Irradiation, at least 25 Gy to the central plane, prevents donor T lymphocytes from proliferating and causing TA-GVHD. Leukoreduction lowers but does not eliminate lymphocytes, and washing or short storage does not inactivate them. Irradiation is therefore specified for intrauterine and neonatal red cells.
  35. The correct answer is A (It establishes accountability and the chain of custody from the laboratory to the clinical area): The signature establishes who took custody of the units and when, so any later question about handling or storage can be traced. It does not show training, replace the bedside identification check, or support billing.
  36. The correct answer is B (It compares results on unknown samples with peer laboratories, giving an independent accuracy check): Internal controls have known expected results, so they cannot show how the laboratory performs against other laboratories on unknown specimens. Proficiency testing gives that outside comparison. It supplements, and does not replace, internal quality control, and it is not a reagent lot check or a training tool.
  37. The correct answer is A (IgG antibodies such as anti-Jka react at 37 C and at the antiglobulin phase): Anti-Jka is usually an IgG antibody that reacts best at 37 C and with antiglobulin reagent, so an immediate spin alone would miss an incompatible unit. The antiglobulin crossmatch is also a final check that the antigen negative typing was correct. ABO confirmation is still needed, and Kidd antibodies are not cold reactive.
  38. The correct answer is C (Group O red cells, because they lack the A and B antigens patient antibodies target): Group A plasma contains anti-B, so group B red cells would be attacked in the patient's circulation. Group O red cells carry neither A nor B antigen, leaving the patient's anti-B with no target. Matching exactly is preferred but is not required when identical stock is unavailable.
  39. The correct answer is A (Heat at 56 C releases ABO antibodies well, because the antigen antibody bond weakens): Heat elution at 56 C is classically used when ABO hemolytic disease is suspected because warming loosens the bond between ABO antibody and red cell antigen. Acid elution is more often chosen for Rh and other IgG specificities. Heat does not remove IgM selectively, and it does not destroy the cells being tested.
  40. The correct answer is D (Repeat the screen after verifying the block is at 37 C, as excess heat weakens binding): Temperatures well above 37 C can weaken or release IgG antibody binding, so a negative screen from an overheated block cannot be trusted, particularly in a patient with a history of a Kidd antibody that may have fallen to a low level. The test must be repeated once the block is verified at 37 C. A negative result obtained under a failed condition should not be reported, and the history does not replace testing.
  41. The correct answer is A (Clear the lipid by high speed centrifugation, because turbid plasma hides weak reactions): Turbid lipemic plasma hides weak agglutination in the reverse group. High speed centrifugation separates the lipid layer so the plasma can be reread, and a recollected sample is the fallback. Saline dilution weakens the antibody reactions, forward grouping alone cannot establish the ABO group, and heating at 56 C does not clear the lipid.
  42. The correct answer is D (Inform the physician of the anti-D risk and arrange antibody screening at later follow-up intervals): Rh-positive red cells given to an Rh-negative recipient carry a high risk of anti-D formation, which matters for future pregnancies. The physician and a transfusion medicine specialist decide on any treatment. Antibody screens at set intervals detect an antibody.
  43. The correct answer is D (Positive for D, C and e, negative for c and E, which is the R1R1 pattern): The DCe haplotype carries D, C and e, so a homozygous DCe/DCe donor expresses D, C and e and lacks c and E. The other patterns correspond to the DcE, Dce and DCE haplotypes, which would produce different typing results. Because the same haplotype comes from both parents, no c or E antigen can appear.
  44. The correct answer is B (Handle every specimen as potentially infectious, because infectious status is often unknown at collection): Routine practices treat all blood and body fluids as potentially infectious, because hepatitis B, hepatitis C and HIV are often carried without symptoms or a known diagnosis. Limiting protection to known infections or to visible blood leaves staff exposed to the unrecognized cases. Requisition flags are incomplete and cannot replace consistent precautions.
  45. The correct answer is D (A and B antigens are weak on newborn cells and found in many tissues): ABO antigens are weakly expressed on fetal red cells and are present on many other tissues, so maternal IgG anti-A or anti-B is partly absorbed away from the red cells. This is why ABO disease is usually milder than Rh disease. The antibodies do cross the placenta, because the IgG component is present.
  46. The correct answer is B (Insignificant, so M negative units are unnecessary, because it fails to react at 37 C): Clinical significance depends on reactivity at 37 C or the antiglobulin phase. An IgM anti-M limited to immediate spin and room temperature does not shorten transfused red cell survival, so antigen negative units are not needed. A negative autocontrol confirms it is an alloantibody.
  47. The correct answer is A (The blood supplier, to quarantine other components, and the provincial hemovigilance program): A contaminated component can affect other recipients, so the supplier must be told to quarantine other components from the same donation and investigate the collection. The provincial hemovigilance program also needs the report to track serious events. An internal infection control review alone does not protect other patients.
  48. The correct answer is B (In the platelet incubator at 20 to 24 C with continuous agitation, preserving platelet function): Platelets are stored at 20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation so they keep metabolizing and stay functional. Refrigeration makes platelets change shape and clear rapidly from the circulation after transfusion. Freezing or warming above room temperature damages the cells and does not extend usable life.
  49. The correct answer is C (One standard vial is sufficient, because the screen excluded a hemorrhage larger than 30 mL): The rosette screen is sensitive to fetal bleeds of roughly 10 mL of whole blood or more, so a negative result with a valid control excludes a bleed larger than the 30 mL of whole blood that one standard vial covers. A single standard dose is therefore sufficient without further counting. Withholding the dose fails because the mother is still at risk of immunization from smaller bleeds.
  50. The correct answer is D (24 hours from the time of washing, kept at 1 to 6 C): Washing in an open system breaks the closed sterile pathway, and the washed cells have lost their preservative solution, so the expiry drops to 24 hours at 1 to 6 C. Keeping the original date or a multi day expiry would risk bacterial growth in a component that is no longer protected.
  51. The correct answer is C (Repeat the full skin preparation before inserting the needle, because touching the area reintroduces bacteria): Once the site is cleaned, touching it can transfer skin bacteria onto the puncture area, so the preparation must be repeated before the needle goes in. Gloves, a single wipe or gauze do not restore a clean surface or the full contact time.
  52. The correct answer is B (It links this specimen, its results and handling steps to one record, because patient numbers repeat): An accession number makes each received specimen traceable through receipt, testing, storage and disposal, even when the same patient sends many samples over the years. It does not replace patient identifiers, record the collector, or number crossmatches.
  53. The correct answer is B (Hold the unit for review, because an unchanged indicator suggests irradiation did not occur): The indicator label gives a visible check that the bag actually received radiation, so an unchanged label means irradiation cannot be confirmed even if the record says otherwise. The unit is held until the irradiator log and indicator are reconciled, and another dose would give unnecessary extra exposure without showing that the earlier dose was missed.
  54. The correct answer is A (Neutralization, consistent with a Lewis antibody, because the saline control stayed strongly reactive): The saline control has the same serum dilution as the test, so a 3+ control against a 0 test shows the loss of reactivity was due to the soluble Lewis substance and not to dilution. Neutralization supports a Lewis specificity. Without the paired control, dilution alone could have produced a weaker reaction.
  55. The correct answer is B (Five units, because five times 0.60 x10^11 equals the target dose of 3.0 x10^11): The dose needed is 3.0 x10^11, and each unit supplies at least 0.60 x10^11, so 3.0 divided by 0.60 is 5. Four units would give only 2.4 x10^11, which is under the target. Adding a sixth unit exposes the patient to another donor without being needed to reach the stated minimum.
  56. The correct answer is D (Group B remains, because a liver does not change red cell type; issue B or O): A solid organ transplant does not change the recipient's red cell production, so the blood group stays group B. Group B or group O red cells are both compatible with a group B recipient. Group O cells lack A and B antigens and are safe, so insisting on B only is unnecessary.
  57. The correct answer is B (Three positive cells react and three negative cells do not, at the same phase): The 3 and 3 rule asks for at least three antigen positive cells that react and three antigen negative cells that do not, tested at the same phase, which gives a probability under 0.05 that the pattern is a chance finding. It is a statement about cells and phase, not about the number of antibodies or screening cells.
  58. The correct answer is B (60 mL of fetal whole blood, calculated from 1.2 percent fetal cells): The fetal cell percentage is 18 divided by 1500, which equals 1.2 percent. Multiplying by 50 mL gives 60 mL of fetal whole blood. Using 30 mL or 10 mL as the multiplier confuses the vial coverage or an arbitrary factor with the maternal blood volume factor.
  59. The correct answer is A (Unit 3 may carry a low prevalence antigen, because only that unit reacts with serum): When one unit reacts with serum that is otherwise unreactive with screening and other donor cells, the donor unit may carry a low prevalence antigen the screen cells lack. An autoantibody would react with all cells and the autocontrol. The other three units remain compatible and can be issued.
  60. The correct answer is A (Add a balance tube of equal volume opposite the unpaired tube, avoiding vibration): Tubes must be loaded in opposite pairs of matching mass, so the unpaired tube needs a balance tube of equal volume placed directly across from it. Adjacent placement or empty gaps leave the load off centre. Slow speed reduces the force but does not remove the imbalance.
  61. The correct answer is D (Flag a name alert, process one sample at a time, and verify name and birth date): Similar names are a recognised risk for specimen mix-ups, so a name alert is raised and each sample is handled separately with full identifier checks. Batching, relying on arrival order or checking partial identifiers leaves the confusion unresolved.
  62. The correct answer is B (Protect the donor from injury and call for help, because the jerking is usually brief): Brief jerking after fainting is common and usually self-limited, so the priority is to prevent injury to the donor, keep the airway safe and summon help. Restraining limbs or putting objects in the mouth can cause harm, and delaying assessment leaves a possible emergency unchecked.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Excess abnormal globulins reduce the repulsion between red cells so that they stack into rouleaux): Excess abnormal globulins from myeloma reduce the repulsion between red cells so they stack into rouleaux. This produces false weak agglutination with every reverse cell. True agglutination by antibody would need a specific antigen match, and complement does not cause stacking.
  2. The correct answer is A (Collect a new sample, because the Tuesday transfusion may have caused a new antibody): Monday is day 0 and Thursday is day 3, so age alone would pass, but the transfusion on Tuesday could have caused a new antibody that the Monday sample cannot show. A new specimen is needed. Where the patient was transfused does not change the immune risk.
  3. The correct answer is D (Corrosive, meaning it can burn skin and eyes and damage some metals): Two test tubes pouring onto a hand and a metal bar is the corrosion pictogram, which warns that the product can cause skin burns, eye damage or metal corrosion. Concentrated bleach fits this hazard, so gloves and eye protection are needed. The flame, skull and flame over circle symbols have different shapes and mean different hazards.
  4. The correct answer is B (Issue Fya-negative units tested by antiglobulin crossmatch, because immediate spin cannot detect an IgG antibody): A past clinically significant antibody must be respected even when the current screen is negative, since levels can fall below detection. Antigen-negative units tested by antiglobulin crossmatch protect against an anamnestic response. Immediate spin crossmatch will not detect an IgG antibody.
  5. The correct answer is B (Defer her until three months after the tattoo, because infection may not yet be detectable): Under the stated policy the deferral runs three months from the tattoo date, so she may return in about six more weeks. Infection acquired from non-sterile equipment can fall inside the window period of donor tests, and a healed-looking site does not show that blood-borne agents were not transmitted.
  6. The correct answer is A (Rh positive, because weak D is detected at the antiglobulin phase with a valid negative control): The anti-D tube reacts 2+ at the antiglobulin phase while the Rh control and saline control do not, and check cells are reactive in every tube, so the test is valid. That pattern identifies weak D, and donors with weak D are labelled Rh positive. Absence of reactivity at immediate spin is expected for a weak antigen.
  7. The correct answer is D (Handle it gently, because frozen plastic is brittle and can crack or leak): Plastic blood bags become brittle at freezer temperatures and can crack if dropped, bent or struck, which leaks plasma and contaminates the area. Frozen units are handled gently, often inside a protective overwrap, and thawed in a controlled device. Bending, dropping or warming by hand risks damage and uneven thawing.
  8. The correct answer is B (Notify the transfusion physician, document the override decision, then issue D-positive cells as directed): Giving D-positive cells to a D-negative woman of childbearing potential is a risk decision that belongs to the transfusion medicine physician, weighed against life-threatening bleeding. The technologist informs that physician, records the decision and issues as directed. Deleting the rule, refusing blood or waiting all fail to manage the risk safely.
  9. The correct answer is A (448 mL: subtract the empty bag weight, then divide by density; within range): The net weight of blood and anticoagulant is 570 minus 98, which is 472 g. Dividing by the density of 1.053 g/mL gives about 448 mL, which falls inside the acceptable range of 405 to 495 mL. Treating grams as millilitres or skipping the subtraction gives a volume that is wrong by tens of millilitres.
  10. The correct answer is B (The cells lack D antigen but can still carry C, c, E or e antigens): Rh negative means that the D antigen is not detected on the red cells, and D is the most immunogenic Rh antigen. Rh negative cells can still carry C, c, E or e from the same gene complex. Absence of the lowercase c or e antigens does not define Rh status, and the Rh system includes several antigens beyond D.
  11. The correct answer is C (Leave the room, secure the area, and call the radiation safety officer immediately): A source that is stuck in the exposed position is an emergency, so the first priority is to get away from it, keep others out, and notify the radiation safety officer who holds the emergency procedure. Opening the chamber or cycling the power could increase the exposure. Platelet viability is a lower priority than staff safety, and waiting in the room adds dose.
  12. The correct answer is B (It shows the equipment gives correct, reproducible results with real samples in routine use): Performance qualification confirms that the equipment produces accurate, reproducible results with real samples under routine conditions. Delivery and installation against specification belong to installation qualification, and running through the operating limits belongs to operational qualification. Reading the manual is training, not qualification.
  13. The correct answer is B (Review the student's work fully and sign only after confirming each step meets procedure): A signature attests that the signer has verified the work, so the review must cover every step and not only the result. Supervised student work can be accepted once a registered technologist has checked it against the procedure. Returning the unit wastes a compatible component and does not solve the review requirement.
  14. The correct answer is C (3360 mL, found by multiplying the 5600 mL blood volume by 0.60): Blood volume is 70 mL/kg x 80 kg = 5600 mL. Plasma makes up the share left after removing the hematocrit, 1 minus 0.40 = 0.60, so plasma volume is 5600 x 0.60 = 3360 mL. Multiplying by the hematocrit itself gives the red cell volume of 2240 mL, which is not plasma.
  15. The correct answer is B (A written comment stays visible to later staff, so decisions do not depend on memory): The record is the only reliable way to carry reasoning from one shift to the next, because spoken explanations are forgotten or not heard. A comment does not replace review and does not prove accuracy. Good systems also keep an audit trail of edits, so a comment is not meant to allow silent change.
  16. The correct answer is A (Prestorage leukoreduced red cells, because fewer donor leukocytes release cytokines during storage): Febrile nonhemolytic reactions are driven mainly by cytokines and by recipient antibodies to donor leukocytes. Removing leukocytes before storage prevents cytokine build-up and is the preferred modification. Irradiation prevents lymphocyte proliferation rather than cytokine release, fresh units still hold white cells, and ABO or Rh group does not affect this type of reaction.
  17. The correct answer is B (Compare the keyed group and Rh with the worksheet reactions and the specimen label): A transcription error occurs between the observed reaction and the entered result, so the check must compare the entry with the original worksheet and the specimen identity. Equipment calibration and reagent lots do not detect a typed mistake. Checking only the name would leave the group itself unverified.
  18. The correct answer is C (Hold unit 2, test a donor segment by DAT, and issue other compatible units): Only unit 2 reacted at the antiglobulin phase while the screen is negative, so the donor cells may be DAT positive or carry a low-incidence antigen. The unit is held and a donor segment DAT is done, while units 1, 3 and 4 are compatible and can be issued. Weak reactions are not safe to ignore, and one reactive unit does not invalidate the patient sample.
  19. The correct answer is B (As H, because hemolysis is a positive reaction that needs investigation): Hemolysis of the reagent cells during testing is read as a positive reaction and recorded as H, because an antibody has bound and activated complement. It is not a negative, and since the plasma was clear beforehand it is not a poor quality specimen. It needs investigation for the responsible antibody.
  20. The correct answer is C (About 178 patient tests, because 9.0 mL usable volume allows 180 tests and two are controls): Usable volume is 10 mL minus 1.0 mL, which is 9.0 mL, or 9,000 microlitres. Dividing by 50 microlitres per test gives 180 tests, and two are used by the controls, leaving 178 for patients. Forgetting the dead volume overstates the supply, and mixing millilitres with microlitres understates it badly.
  21. The correct answer is A (Two patient identifiers, collection date and time, and the collector's identification such as initials): Transfusion specimens need two identifiers plus the date, time and the person who collected the sample so the draw can be traced. Ward location and the testing technologist do not identify the patient or the collector. Details added later by the laboratory defeat the purpose, because only the collector knows who was drawn.
  22. The correct answer is A (Recentrifuging the tubes and reading for agglutination, because the check cells must be spun to react): After check cells are added to a negative tube, the tube must be recentrifuged and read for agglutination. Without a second spin and reading, there is no evidence that the AHG was active. Washing again or adding more AHG would remove or dilute the check cells and does not meet the validity requirement.
  23. The correct answer is B (Citrate first, then EDTA, because EDTA carried over can distort coagulation results): Standard order of draw places blood culture, then citrate, then serum, then heparin, then EDTA, then fluoride. EDTA contains potassium and chelates calcium, so carryover into a citrate tube can distort coagulation results. Urgency of the group and screen does not change the sequence.
  24. The correct answer is A (Rh positive platelets carry a risk of immunization, so Rh immune globulin may be considered): Platelets do not carry the D antigen, but a small number of contaminating red cells can immunize an Rh negative recipient. For a woman of childbearing potential the risk is weighed against bleeding, and Rh immune globulin may be offered after the transfusion. The urgent need means the request is not simply cancelled, and the immune globulin is not a prerequisite.
  25. The correct answer is C (Keep using the old passing lot, quarantine the new lot, and contact the supplier): The control must react as expected before a lot is used for patients, so the failing lot is held and the supplier is told. The old lot passes and is still in date, so testing can continue with it. Mixing lots or splitting use by urgency would put an unverified reagent into patient testing.
  26. The correct answer is D (Cells homozygous for the key antigens, because they carry a double dose of antigen): Homozygous cells carry a double dose of antigen, so weak antibodies react more strongly and are less likely to be missed. Heterozygous cells have half the antigen and can give weak or negative reactions. Antigen negative cells cannot detect antibody to those antigens, and enzyme treatment destroys some of these antigens instead of removing dosage.
  27. The correct answer is C (Pack the units in a validated cooler holding 1 to 10 C, because this meets limits): Red cells are shipped and transported in a validated container that keeps them between 1 and 10 C. Uncovered transport has no protection, direct contact with frozen packs can freeze and damage cells, and warming accelerates deterioration.
  28. The correct answer is C (Yes, because a homozygous Jka cell carries enough antigen to react with anti-Jka): Kidd antibodies show dosage, so reactions are stronger with cells that are homozygous for the antigen. The Jka positive, Jkb negative cell is homozygous, so a nonreactive result rules out anti-Jka. A heterozygous nonreactive cell could hide a weak antibody and would not be accepted.
  29. The correct answer is B (Thaw in a controlled water bath or dry thawer at 30 to 37 C): Plasma is thawed in a controlled water bath or dry thawer at 30 to 37 C, which thaws it within a reasonable time without denaturing labile factors such as factor VIII. Thawing on the bench or in a refrigerator is slow and uneven, and heating to 56 C would damage proteins.
  30. The correct answer is C (Weakly bound IgG may dissociate from cells, causing a falsely weak or negative result): In low ionic strength medium, antibody uptake is fast, and prolonged incubation allows weakly bound IgG to dissociate from the cell surface. This can reduce a true reaction to a weak or negative result. The validated incubation time should be followed.
  31. The correct answer is A (Accept it, because the central dose exceeds 25 Gy and the minimum exceeds 15 Gy): The standard requires at least 25 Gy to the central plane and no part of the unit below 15 Gy. Here the central dose is 27 Gy and the lowest point is 17 Gy, so both conditions are met. The 25 Gy figure is a minimum at the centre, not a maximum or an everywhere requirement, so rejection or reirradiation is not needed.
  32. The correct answer is D (Nonimmune consumption and sequestration, because a good early increment with rapid loss argues against antibodies): Antibody-mediated destruction is rapid, so immune refractoriness typically gives a poor increment at one hour. A good one-hour value followed by a quick fall, together with fever, sepsis and splenomegaly, points to nonimmune factors such as consumption and splenic sequestration. The negative HLA screen and the identical ABO group further weaken the immune explanations.
  33. The correct answer is B (52.5 mL, because 3.5 kg multiplied by 15 mL/kg gives the prescribed total volume): Dose in mL equals weight in kilograms multiplied by the dose per kilogram, so 3.5 x 15 equals 52.5 mL. Dividing instead of multiplying gives 4.3, and the other values come from misplaced decimals or a wrong dose. The answer must make clinical sense for a small neonate.
  34. The correct answer is D (Strike the error with one line, add the correct time, initials, date and a reason): Records must keep the original entry legible, so a single line is drawn through the error and the correction is added with initials, date and reason. Erasing or covering an entry hides the original, and leaving a wrong time in place makes the record unreliable.
  35. The correct answer is C (Wrong blood in tube from mislabelling, so that patient is recollected): A group that differs from a reliable record, with no transfusion to explain it, points first to a sample from the wrong person, especially after batch labelling away from the bed. ABO group does not change with illness, and a different reagent lot should still give the same group. Recollection settles the question.
  36. The correct answer is C (Hold the unit and investigate for identification error, because donor ABO group does not change): A donor's ABO group is a stable inherited trait, so a change from the historical record points to a mislabelled specimen, an incorrect donor record or a mix-up of units. The unit stays on hold until the source of the error is found, and neither group should be assigned or the record altered on the strength of the newest result alone.
  37. The correct answer is B (Hold at room temperature, 20 to 24 C, and do not refrigerate it): Thawed cryoprecipitate is kept at 20 to 24 C because cold storage can precipitate and lose factor VIII and fibrinogen activity. It is not refrigerated and not refrozen once thawed, and it should be issued promptly because its expiry is only a matter of hours.
  38. The correct answer is B (Requirements for quality and competence in medical laboratories, covering management and technical processes): ISO 15189 sets requirements for the quality and competence of medical laboratories, including management system and technical requirements. It does not contain donor selection rules, component storage specifications or building design rules.
  39. The correct answer is D (Continuous recording shows excursions between manual checks, so affected units can be identified and managed): A recorder with an alarm captures every temperature change, including brief excursions that occur between manual readings and overnight. This lets staff decide which units may still be used and which must be quarantined. Manual readings alone cannot show how long a unit was out of range, and thermometer accuracy and supplier rules are not the reason for the requirement.
  40. The correct answer is D (It diverts the first millilitres, which may carry skin bacteria, away from the donated unit): The first 30 to 40 mL of blood carries the highest chance of skin bacteria and a skin plug, so it is diverted into the pouch and kept out of the unit. This lowers the risk of bacterial contamination of the component. Hemoglobin screening is done before donation, and the pouch is not meant to vent air or collect anticoagulant.
  41. The correct answer is A (1 to 10 C, because red cells may warm slightly in transit while remaining acceptable): During transport red cells are permitted to range from 1 to 10 C, which is wider than the 1 to 6 C storage range. The validated cooler is designed and tested to hold units within that range for the maximum trip time. Room temperature belongs to platelet handling and frozen transport applies to frozen plasma.
  42. The correct answer is B (The 1-3s rule is violated, which suggests random error and requires investigation before reporting): A single control value more than 3 standard deviations from the mean breaks the 1-3s rule, which is mainly sensitive to random error. The 2-2s, 4-1s and 10x rules each need two, four or ten consecutive points, and the neighbouring points here do not meet those patterns. Patient results are held until the cause is found and the control passes.
  43. The correct answer is B (Just inside the limits, at 2 and 5 C, so staff can act in time): The alarm must sound while units are still within the acceptable range, so it is set just inside the limits, at about 2 and 5 C, which gives staff time to respond. Setting it at the limits gives no warning before damage, and setting it outside the limits means units would already be out of range. Very narrow points only create nuisance alarms.
  44. The correct answer is D (Red cells and plasma near a one to one ratio, with platelets added regularly): Balanced protocols replace red cells and plasma near a one to one ratio to limit dilutional coagulopathy while platelets are added regularly. Waiting for laboratory results delays factor replacement. A four to one ratio underdoses plasma during ongoing hemorrhage.
  45. The correct answer is D (No alloantibody was detected after adsorption, so only the warm autoantibody is reported): When autoadsorption removes the warm autoantibody and the adsorbed serum reacts with no panel cell, any alloantibody that was present would have remained and shown reactions, so none is detected. Nonreactivity is the expected sign of successful adsorption, not a failure. Two adsorptions can be enough, because the number needed depends on the strength of the autoantibody.
  46. The correct answer is C (Patient's full name and second identifier, location, product needed, number of units and urgency): An urgent request still needs two patient identifiers, the product, the quantity, the location and the level of urgency. Room numbers and diagnoses change and do not identify a patient. Supplier preferences do not affect compatibility or priority.
  47. The correct answer is B (Enter the antibody, date and an antigen-negative flag, then have a second person verify it): A record entry must be findable and reliable, so the antibody is entered in the structured field with its date and an antigen-negative requirement flag, and a second person verifies it. A paper note or an entry delayed until the next specimen can be missed. Clinical staff do not control laboratory antibody records.
  48. The correct answer is B (The cryoprotectant is exhaled and excreted, so the odour is an expected effect): Dimethyl sulfoxide is cleared through the lungs and skin, producing a garlic or creamed corn odour for hours after infusion. This is expected and is not harm in itself. Bacterial contamination or hemolysis would show fever, rigors or red urine, and anaphylaxis presents with airway and skin changes, not odour.
  49. The correct answer is D (Reject the lot for routine use, because one A1 sample reacted below the required 3+ strength): The acceptance criterion requires every A1 sample to react at 3+ or stronger, and one sample reacted 2+, so the lot fails verification as written. Passing most samples does not meet a criterion set for all of them. Repeating a single failed result to replace it hides a possible lot weakness, and group O samples should be negative, not weakly positive.
  50. The correct answer is A (Quarantine unused components, trace issued ones, then notify the responsible physician per procedure): A recall requires immediate control of components still in the laboratory, followed by tracing any units already issued using the donation number in the issue records. The responsible physician is then told so the recipient can be assessed. Waiting delays protection, and discarding by group would waste unrelated units.
  51. The correct answer is C (Yersinia enterocolitica, because it grows at 4 C and thrives on iron from stored cells): Yersinia enterocolitica grows at refrigerator temperature and uses iron released from stored red cells, so it is the classic cause of severe, rapid reactions to old red cell units. Skin flora such as staphylococci are the usual platelet contaminants, Bacillus is also a platelet problem, and Treponema does not survive prolonged cold storage.
  52. The correct answer is C (14:50, because typing runs alongside the 45 minute screen and the crossmatch follows it): Typing and the screen start together, so the longest step is the 45 minute screen, which ends at 14:45. The crossmatch needs a negative screen first and adds 5 minutes, giving 14:50. Adding the typing time in series would overstate the wait, and skipping the screen is not acceptable.
  53. The correct answer is D (The ordering physician or delegated clinician who can explain risks, benefits and alternatives): Consent is a conversation between the patient and the clinician responsible for the order. The laboratory prepares compatible components but does not counsel patients. Suppliers and porters have no role in discussing the treatment decision.
  54. The correct answer is B (Timer and tachometer speed verification, because spin parameters determine reproducible sedimentation of cells): Centrifuge performance is verified by checking the timer against a reference clock and the rotor speed with a tachometer, since the force applied to cells depends on speed and time. Rack inspection, temperature and reagent lot review are useful but do not show that the delivered spin conditions are correct.
  55. The correct answer is D (Recipient anti-IgA reacts with IgA in the donor plasma, triggering complement and mast cell activation): An IgA-deficient recipient who has made anti-IgA reacts immediately to IgA present in normal donor plasma, forming immune complexes that activate complement and mast cells and cause anaphylaxis. Donor antibodies, HLA antibodies and endotoxin produce different clinical pictures.
  56. The correct answer is A (Compare every identifier on the tube with the requisition and resolve differences before testing): Every identifier on the tube must agree with the requisition, and any difference is resolved before testing starts. Checking the surname alone misses wrong-patient errors between people with similar names. A history file helps only after identity is confirmed, and cap colour describes the additive, not the patient.
  57. The correct answer is B (Defer her until symptoms resolve, because an active respiratory illness fails the good health rule): Donors must be well on the day of collection, and an ongoing respiratory infection is a reason to defer even when vital signs are normal. Reduced volumes or a plasma-only collection do not remove the need for good health, so she can return once the symptoms are gone.
  58. The correct answer is A (Annotate the group as transitional after transplant and apply the transplant transfusion rules): A mixed field in a transplant recipient is expected as donor and recipient red cells coexist. The group should be annotated as transitional, and transplant-specific selection rules applied until the type stabilizes. Calling it final, rejecting the sample or ignoring the change gives a misleading record.
  59. The correct answer is B (Suspect a sample mix-up, continue group O red cells, and collect a new verified sample): Two different ABO groups under one identifier means at least one tube came from the wrong patient. Until a new properly identified sample resolves it, group O red cells remain the safest course. Trusting either result, or calling it a subgroup, could lead to an ABO incompatible transfusion.
  60. The correct answer is A (Seven more plasma units, bringing the plasma total to twelve to match the red cell units): A one to one ratio with 12 red cell units means 12 plasma units in total. With 5 already issued, 12 minus 5 leaves 7 still to be released. Six more would only reach 11, and twelve more would overshoot the target.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Handling error outside the temperature limits, a preventable cause that needs documented corrective action): A unit left unrefrigerated beyond the permitted time is lost because of a handling failure, which is a preventable and reportable cause. Tracking it separately from true outdating or order changes lets the committee target training and equipment fixes. Mixing it with outdated or cancelled categories hides the real source of the loss.
  2. The correct answer is C (To confirm the recorder is accurate, because a faulty recorder could hide temperature drift): A recorder is a measuring device that can drift or fail, so an independent thermometer is read and compared with it to verify its accuracy. The liquid bath only damps the reading and does not measure each unit. Both devices are kept because accreditation expects a continuous record and an independent check.
  3. The correct answer is A (Contamination with Wharton's jelly, because it causes nonspecific clumping that is removed by washing): Wharton's jelly is a gelatinous substance in cord tissue that causes spontaneous clumping, and thorough washing of the cells removes it. A reaction in the saline control points to nonspecific clumping rather than antigen antibody reactions. Maternal IgG anti-D coats cells but does not clump them in saline.
  4. The correct answer is A (Quarantine the units and assess for hemolysis, because the minimum fell below the accepted range): A transport minimum of 0.4 C is below the accepted range of 1 to 10 C, so the units may have partly frozen and need to be quarantined and checked for hemolysis before any decision on release. Averaging the readings hides an excursion, because each reading must stay within the limits. Discarding without assessment is premature, and a low maximum does not excuse a low minimum.
  5. The correct answer is C (Remove unbound serum globulins that would neutralize antihuman globulin and cause false negatives): Free immunoglobulin left in the tube binds and neutralizes antihuman globulin, so antibody on the red cells would go undetected and a false negative would result. Several complete saline washes remove this unbound protein before the reagent is added. Washing does not lyse cells, warm them or change their concentration.
  6. The correct answer is C (Decline to release donor identity, because donor information is confidential and not shared with recipients): Donor and recipient identities are kept confidential under provincial privacy law and blood service policy, so the technologist does not release any donor information to a recipient's care team or family. Anonymous thanks can be passed through the blood service's own program instead. A unit number, a physician's request or partial details such as initials do not create a legal right to disclosure.
  7. The correct answer is B (Probable in vitro hemolysis from collection, so a fresh specimen should be requested): Narrow catheters and forceful aspiration commonly lyse red cells during collection, and a clear earlier specimen from the same patient with no transfusion supports in vitro damage. Recollecting by venipuncture gives a specimen that reflects the patient's circulating plasma. True intravascular hemolysis would need clinical signs and a transfusion or disease context.
  8. The correct answer is D (The highest serial dilution of recipient plasma that still shows 1+ agglutination with A1 cells): A titre is the reciprocal of the highest dilution of plasma that still gives the chosen endpoint reaction, here 1+, so 1 in 64 means the plasma reacts through the sixth doubling dilution. Transplant programs follow it to decide whether more plasma exchange is needed. It does not measure percent removal or antigen numbers.
  9. The correct answer is A (A cold antibody was blocked from binding, and a possible IgG alloantibody remains to identify): Warming the serum and cells before mixing prevents a cold antibody from binding, which explains the loss of the immediate spin reactions. The antiglobulin phase reactivity that remains with only some cells points to an IgG antibody that reacts at 37 C and needs to be identified. A technical failure would not leave a clean, cell-specific pattern of reactions.
  10. The correct answer is B (The unit lacks the C, E and K antigens that the patient also lacks): Prophylactic phenotype matching protects a patient from forming antibodies by avoiding exposure to antigens the patient lacks, so the unit is negative for C, E and K when the patient is negative. This is different from ABO and Rh compatibility or a crossmatch. Selecting donors who carry the antigens would expose the patient to them.
  11. The correct answer is A (Refrigerated at 1 to 6 C, labelled with patient identifiers and the collection date): Retained samples are kept in the same cold range as red cells so that cells and plasma remain suitable for repeat testing. Freezing would hemolyze the cells, room temperature allows deterioration and bacterial growth, and 37 C speeds loss of antibody and cell integrity. Clear labelling with identifiers and date also lets staff find and discard tubes on time.
  12. The correct answer is C (State the training gap and request supervised training, because competence must be verified first): Professionals are accountable for work they personally perform, so they must state limits and arrange training and assessment. A supervisor's assurance does not transfer accountability. Refusing every related task is excessive, and putting someone else's signature on work is dishonest.
Case CHB7

A 72-year-old woman with anemia needs two red cell units. Her group is A positive. The antibody screen is reactive at the antiglobulin phase only. On a 10-cell panel, cells 2, 4, 7 and 9 react at the antiglobulin phase. Cell 4 is E positive and e negative and reacts 3+; the other three reactive cells are E positive and e positive and react 2+. The six nonreactive cells are all E negative. Anti-c, anti-K, anti-Fya, anti-Jka and anti-D were ruled out on homozygous nonreactive cells. The autocontrol is negative. Four E-negative units were crossmatched; three were compatible and one reacted 2+ at the antiglobulin phase.

  1. The correct answer is A (Dosage, because cells with a double dose of the antigen bind more antibody): Antibodies in several systems, including anti-E, show dosage, so cells that are homozygous for the antigen carry twice as many antigen sites and react more strongly. The cell lacking e is homozygous for E. Enzyme treatment and complement are not part of the panel method described, and the negative autocontrol excludes autoantibody.
  2. The correct answer is C (About 14 units, because 70 percent of 20 units are expected to be E negative): The number of E-negative units is the stated frequency times the number screened: 0.70 x 20 = 14. The figure of 6 comes from using the E-positive frequency of 30 percent, and half of 20 gives 10 only if the frequency were 50 percent. Each E-negative unit still requires a compatible antiglobulin crossmatch before issue.
  3. The correct answer is B (Withhold the unit and investigate a second alloantibody to an antigen on that unit): An incompatible antiglobulin crossmatch with an E-negative unit after anti-E is identified suggests a second alloantibody that was masked or not detected, such as an antibody to a low-prevalence antigen. The unit is withheld and the serum retested against that donor's phenotype. Issuing it or dismissing the reaction risks a hemolytic reaction.
  4. The correct answer is D (Flush the eye at the eyewash station for about 15 minutes, then report and seek assessment): Mucous membrane exposures are flushed immediately and continuously at an eyewash station, because dilution within seconds reduces the dose of any pathogen. Reporting and medical assessment follow straight afterwards. Wiping the eye or delaying irrigation to make calls removes the most useful minutes.
  5. The correct answer is B (Report pink plasma as a visual finding and compare it with the first specimen): A change from straw coloured to pink plasma after a reaction is an important sign of red cell destruction and belongs on the report with the comparison to the earlier specimen. A negative DAT does not remove the significance of visible hemolysis. Omitting colour would hide a key clue from the physician.
  6. The correct answer is B (Anti-Fya, because every reactive cell carries Fya and every nonreactive cell lacks it): Anti-Fya fits all eight cells: the four reactive cells all carry Fya, while the four nonreactive cells all lack it, and the two Fy(a+b0) cells react more strongly because of dosage. Anti-K and anti-D are excluded because they are absent from reactive cells, and anti-Jka is excluded by the nonreactive Jka-positive cells.
  7. The correct answer is C (Use a qualified interpreter to confirm identifiers, because identification should not depend on informal translation): A qualified interpreter reduces the risk of misunderstanding and misidentification and respects the patient's right to understand care. A family member may mistranslate or answer for the patient. Using only a wristband ignores the requirement to confirm identity, and delaying care for language is inequitable.
  8. The correct answer is A (They remove or shield sharp points, lowering needlestick injuries and bloodborne pathogen exposure for staff): Engineered safety devices are designed to prevent needlestick and other percutaneous injuries by eliminating or covering the sharp. They do not shorten collection time or replace gloves, and sterility of the specimen is a separate matter from staff protection.
  9. The correct answer is D (Anti-Jka, so select Jka-negative red cells and crossmatch at the antiglobulin phase): Only cells that carry Jka reacted, and the strongest reactions occurred with cells lacking Jkb, which fits dosage for a Kidd antibody. Cells carrying D, C, E, c, e, K, Fya, Fyb and Jkb without Jka were nonreactive, ruling those out. Jka-negative units are therefore needed.
  10. The correct answer is B (Quarantine the unit and notify the supplier, because these changes suggest bacterial growth): Loss of swirling, brownish discolouration and gas bubbles suggest bacterial growth, so the unit must be quarantined and the supplier told. Remixing, filtering or issuing with a warning does not make a contaminated unit safe, and leukoreduction does not remove bacteria.
  11. The correct answer is B (The lifelong flag for irradiated components, entered with the reason and the date): Policy states the need is lifelong, so the matching entry is the lifelong flag with its reason and date. A field that expires would allow nonirradiated product to be issued after 30 days. A free text comment does not trigger the automated checks that protect the patient.
  12. The correct answer is C (Platelets travel at 20 to 24 C without ice, because cold damages them and shortens survival): Platelets are stored and transported at room temperature, 20 to 24 C, because chilling changes their shape and causes rapid removal after transfusion. Ice packs belong to red cell transport, and warming to body temperature promotes bacterial growth and activation.
  13. The correct answer is D (Offer group A D-positive units instead, because they match the patient's own type): The patient is group A D-positive and can safely receive group A D-positive red cells, so the scarce group O D-negative units are not needed. Advising the surgeon preserves emergency stock without harming the patient. Using the scarce units, cancelling surgery or splitting stock all waste a resource or delay care unnecessarily.
  14. The correct answer is D (Issue group O Rh-positive red cells, accepting the small alloimmunization risk in male patients): Rh-negative red cells are scarce, and a male patient has no pregnancy risk from anti-D. Group O Rh-positive units are therefore accepted for unknown males. Waiting for typing risks death, and group A units could be incompatible with an unknown ABO group.
  15. The correct answer is B (A new sample collected now, because the earlier sample is outside the three-day window): A sample used for crossmatching in a patient pregnant within three months must be collected within three days of the planned transfusion because new alloantibodies can form quickly. Day 4 is outside that limit, so a new sample is needed. A negative earlier screen does not extend the validity period.
  16. The correct answer is C (Record the death date and unit trace, then report the recipient as deceased to the supplier): A look-back report needs an outcome for every traced recipient, and a death date with the unit trace shows the unit was followed to its end result. Leaving the recipient out or calling them lost to follow-up misstates what was found. Family contact is decided by the physician and provincial rules, so it is not needed to close the trace.
  17. The correct answer is A (Release the units under emergency policy and obtain the signed acknowledgment, because delay harms patients): The written policy already authorizes emergency issue with a signed physician acknowledgment, so the technologist follows it and informs the director afterward. Holding units for a crossmatch or for approval puts a bleeding patient at risk. Verbal-only requests leave no record of who accepted the risk.
  18. The correct answer is C (It does not represent her exposure, so the dose needs a documented estimate): A dosimeter only records radiation it was exposed to while worn in the work area, so a badge left in the staff room cannot show her real exposure. The period needs a documented dose estimate, and she should be reminded to wear it correctly. A zero reading from a badge that was not worn proves nothing about shielding or safety.
  19. The correct answer is C (Unit number, component type, reason for discard, date, and the technologist's identity for traceability): A complete discard record carries the unit identifier, component type, the reason, the date and who performed the action, so the discard can be traced and counted by category. A leaking bag is a product defect, which matters for supplier feedback. Incomplete or indirect records prevent meaningful wastage analysis and break traceability.
Case CHB8

A 29-year-old woman, gravida 3 para 2, is group B Rh negative. Her antibody screen at 12 weeks is positive. The panel identifies anti-D only, titre 4 by the indirect antiglobulin method. She received Rh immune globulin after her first delivery but not after a miscarriage at 10 weeks in her second pregnancy. The laboratory critical titre is 16.

  1. The correct answer is D (Sensitization from an Rh positive fetus during the unprotected miscarriage, because fetal cells entered her circulation): Fetal Rh positive cells can enter the maternal circulation during a miscarriage, and without immune globulin the mother may make active anti-D. Passive anti-D from an old dose would have disappeared long before. Infection does not produce specific anti-D, and the history includes no transfusion.
  2. The correct answer is B (Test stored and new samples together using the same method and cells): A change of two or more dilutions is meaningful only when old and new samples are run side by side with the same technique, cell lot and reader. Using different cells or testing only the new sample adds variation that makes the comparison unreliable.
  3. The correct answer is D (Reserve irradiated, CMV safe, D negative group O red cells crossmatched against maternal plasma): Intrauterine red cells must lack the antigen the maternal antibody targets, be compatible with maternal plasma and group O so they cannot meet fetal ABO issues, and be irradiated and CMV safe. D positive cells would be destroyed, and old unirradiated units carry higher risk to the fetus.
  4. The correct answer is C (The reads are invalid, so repeat the tests with thorough washing and fresh antiglobulin): Check cells should agglutinate in every negative antiglobulin tube, and failure to do so shows that the antiglobulin reagent was neutralized, often by poor washing. The negative results therefore cannot be accepted. The tests are repeated with proper technique, not reported as positive without evidence.
  5. The correct answer is D (Retype the donor segment and recheck patient identity, because an ABO discrepancy is the likely cause): A strong spin reaction with a negative screen and matching grouping results points to a possible ABO error, such as a mislabelled unit, a wrong segment or a patient sample problem. Retyping the segment and confirming identity must happen before any decision. Issuing the unit or warming the tubes could hide an ABO incompatible unit that would cause an acute hemolytic reaction.
  6. The correct answer is B (Two vials, from a 20 mL bleed rounding to one plus one extra): The bleed is 0.4 x 50 = 20 mL, and 20 divided by 30 is 0.67, which rounds to one vial. The policy then adds one vial, giving two in total. A single vial ignores the added vial, and rounding 0.67 up to two misapplies the nearest whole number rule.
  7. The correct answer is D (A platelet incubator at 20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation): Platelets are stored at 20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation so that gas exchange continues and pH stays acceptable. Refrigeration changes platelet shape and causes rapid clearance after transfusion, and freezing destroys function. Resting them on a shelf is acceptable only briefly, for example during transport.
  8. The correct answer is A (Remove gloves first, then gown and eyewear, then wash hands, because gloves are most contaminated): Gloves are the most contaminated item, so they come off first, followed by the gown and eyewear, and the hands are cleaned last. Removing the gown while still gloved, or wearing gloves out of the work area, spreads organisms to clothing, doors and the face. Washing gloved hands does not make the other items safe to touch.
  9. The correct answer is D (1.5, because crossmatched units are divided by the units actually transfused): The crossmatch to transfusion ratio is units crossmatched divided by units transfused, so 360 divided by 240 gives 1.5. Inverting the fraction gives 0.67, which is the transfusion probability rather than the ratio. The 120 unused units and the 66.7 percent figure describe related ideas but are not the indicator the committee tracks.
  10. The correct answer is B (Validation of the system logic, because mismatches must trigger the expected blocks before use): Validation proves that the computer logic blocks incompatible entries by testing deliberate mismatches before the system goes live. Proficiency testing, maintenance and reagent lot checks are separate quality activities that do not show whether the software logic works.
  11.
