The correct answer is B (Group O red cells, because they lack A and B antigens for either plasma): In a major ABO incompatible graft the recipient's anti-A persists until donor derived red cells replace the patient's own, so group A cells would still be attacked. Group O red cells carry neither antigen and are safe with both plasmas. Group B and AB cells carry antigens that the recipient's anti-B or anti-A can attack.
  12. The correct answer is A (Labelling the tube away from the patient instead of at the bedside straight after collection): Specimen tubes must be labelled at the bedside immediately after collection, in the presence of the patient, because carrying unlabelled tubes away allows tubes to be mixed up. Checking the wristband before the draw is correct and is not the error. Printed labels with two identifiers are acceptable, and physician countersignature is not part of the labelling step.
  13. The correct answer is B (Pick E negative units that are antiglobulin crossmatch compatible, then extend rule out): Anti-E has been found but the other antibodies were not excluded, so units should lack E and be compatible at the antiglobulin crossmatch while rule out is completed with selected cells and the patient is typed for the unexcluded antigens. Releasing units on the basis of one antibody risks a hemolytic reaction. Delay is not needed when compatible units can be chosen.
  14. The correct answer is D (It alerts staff to a possible specimen identification error before blood is selected): A historical comparison, sometimes called a delta check, spots results that disagree with a patient's earlier record. Because ABO group does not change in an ordinary patient, a mismatch usually means the specimen is from another person or was mislabelled. The conflict is investigated and resolved before any blood is selected, and the old record is not simply overwritten.
  15. The correct answer is A (40, because tube 1 is 1 in 5 and each following tube doubles the dilution): Tube 1 holds 0.2 mL in 1.0 mL, a 1 in 5 dilution. Doubling gives 1 in 10, 1 in 20 and 1 in 40 in tubes 2 to 4, so tube 4 is 1 in 40 and the titre is 40. Assuming the opening tube was 1 in 2 would give 16, which ignores the actual volumes used.
  16. The correct answer is B (Activate it one-handed right after withdrawal, then discard the whole assembly, because this limits exposure): The shield is engaged immediately after withdrawal, while the needle is still pointing away from the collector, and the assembly is discarded intact. Recapping, carrying an exposed needle or separating the needle by hand all add steps during which a sharps injury can occur.
  17. The correct answer is C (The 2-2s rule, indicating systematic error, so patient results are held pending further investigation): Two consecutive results beyond 2 SD on the same side violate the 2-2s rejection rule, which points to a systematic shift such as reagent or instrument change. No value exceeded 3 SD and the two points do not span 4 SD, so the other rules do not apply.
  18. The correct answer is D (State the available inventory, offer the one unit, and give the supplier's estimated arrival time): The physician can make a good decision only with accurate information about what is available and when more will arrive. Withholding the single unit delays treatment without any benefit. A false promise erodes trust and may lead to unsafe planning.
  19. The correct answer is B (Place platelets in validated 20 to 24 C containers and follow the downtime plan): Platelets need storage at 20 to 24 C, so a warm room and a stopped agitator threaten them, and validated containers hold the range while the downtime plan guides the next steps. Refrigerating platelets damages them. Leaving them in a failing incubator or issuing stock to wards without need removes control of their condition.
  20. The correct answer is D (Recall the issued units and alert the supervisor, because one sample is from another patient): Two different groups for one patient within an hour almost certainly mean one tube belongs to someone else. Group A units could be incompatible if the second sample is correct, so they must be recalled and the supervisor told while both samples are investigated. Retyping or discarding a result does not make the units safe.
  21. The correct answer is C (Anti-Jka, because reactions follow Jka with dosage and it can cause delayed hemolytic reactions): Cells 1, 2, 4 and 7 react and every one carries Jka, while cells 3, 5, 6 and 8 lack Jka and do not react. Homozygous Jka cells react 2+ and heterozygous cells react 1+, showing dosage. Anti-Jka is a classic cause of delayed hemolytic reactions, because it falls below detection and returns after re-exposure.
  22. The correct answer is C (Anti-D reacts with cell I only, while anti-K reacts with cells II and III): Antigen-positive cells must react with the matching antiserum and antigen-negative cells must stay nonreactive, which matches the package insert. Cell I carries D but lacks K, so only anti-D should react with it, and cells II and III should react only with anti-K. Reactions on the wrong cells, or no reactions at all, show that the cells or the antisera are faulty.
  23. The correct answer is A (One part bleach to nine parts water, because ten counts the final total): A one in ten dilution means one part of bleach in ten parts of final solution, so nine parts of water are added to one of bleach. Adding ten parts of water gives a one in eleven mixture that is slightly weaker than intended. Stronger or equal part mixtures waste bleach, release more fumes and corrode surfaces without a better kill.
  24. The correct answer is B (Anti-Jka, because only the two Jka-positive cells reacted and the Jka-negative cell stayed nonreactive): The only antigen present on both reactive cells and absent from the nonreactive cell is Jka. Anti-D cannot explain the reaction of the D-negative cell, anti-K cannot explain the reaction of the K-negative cell, and anti-Fya cannot explain the reaction of the Fya-negative cell. An antibody too weak to detect on day 0 was boosted after transfusion, matching a delayed reaction.
  25. The correct answer is C (A warm IgG autoantibody is present in addition, because the autocontrol and DAT are positive): A positive DAT and autocontrol that persist after the cold component has been removed by prewarming point to an IgG warm autoantibody coating the patient's cells. The prewarm technique removes cold reactivity only, so warm autoantibody reactions at the antiglobulin phase remain. An antibody to a high-prevalence antigen would not usually give a positive autocontrol and DAT.
  26. The correct answer is C (Both reagent cell types agglutinate, because group O plasma contains both anti-A and anti-B): Group O people make both anti-A and anti-B, so their plasma agglutinates both reagent A1 and B cells. A lack of reactions would suggest a missing antibody, and a single reaction would suggest group A or group B rather than group O.
  27. The correct answer is A (8, because the titre is the reciprocal of the highest dilution showing a 1+ reaction): The titre is the reciprocal of the highest dilution that still gives a 1+ reaction, which here is 1 in 8, so the titre is 8. The final negative tube at 1 in 16 lies beyond the endpoint and is not used. Counting positive tubes or using the strongest tube does not describe antibody concentration.
  28. The correct answer is C (Premature infants lack maternal antibody, so donor cell CMV can cause severe disease): Very low birth weight infants of CMV negative mothers have no passive immunity and can develop serious infection from CMV carried in donor leukocytes. Leukoreduced or CMV seronegative units reduce that risk. They do not remove every virus, and CMV does not cause hemolysis.
  29. The correct answer is A (Quarantine the units and document the excursion, then hold them for physician review): The policy makes continued use depend on a documented review and physician approval, so the units are separated and the event recorded while that review happens. Staying under 10 C does not authorize release on its own. Immediate discard is not required by the policy, and issuing the units first would bypass the review.
  30. The correct answer is D (Short incubation explains the weak control; repair the timer, then repeat control and patient tests): The gel reaction needs the full manufacturer incubation time to develop, so a timer that ends incubation at 6 minutes of a 10 minute setting can weaken reactions while temperature and centrifuge remain acceptable. The timer must be repaired and verified, and affected tests repeated. Changing temperature or speed would not address the measured fault.
  31. The correct answer is C (Excess heat damages red cell membranes, causing hemolysis and free hemoglobin in the recipient circulation): Red cells are damaged by heat above about 41 C, so overheated blood contains free hemoglobin and damaged cells that can harm the recipient. This is why warmers need an independent over-temperature alarm and regular checks. Warming does not sterilize a unit, change its citrate content or activate complement.
  32. The correct answer is D (Heterozygous cells express less antigen, so a weak reaction shows the reagent detects low antigen levels): A heterozygous control shows dosage: it carries one copy of the gene and reacts more weakly than homozygous cells. If the reagent detects it, the reagent can be trusted to detect weakly expressed antigen on patient or donor cells. Choosing a strongly reactive homozygous control could hide a weak reagent.
  33. The correct answer is C (Overtreatment damaged the antigens, so the cells are unsuitable and a new batch is needed): Kidd antigens are enhanced by enzymes, so a known anti-Jka should react strongly. Loss of reactivity after a treatment three times longer than validated shows that the digestion was excessive and damaged antigens. The batch must be discarded and prepared again with the correct time.
  34. The correct answer is B (Anti-Fya reactive at the antiglobulin phase, because Duffy antibodies can cause hemolytic transfusion reactions): IgG antibodies that react at the antiglobulin phase after 37 C incubation, such as anti-Fya, can shorten red cell survival and are clinically significant. Anti-P1, anti-M and anti-Leb that react only at room temperature or below are usually IgM and not expected to cause reactions in vivo. Reactivity at 37 C is what changes that picture.
  35. The correct answer is C (Add a balance tube holding matching volume of saline directly opposite the odd tube): Tubes must be balanced by mass across the rotor axis, so an odd tube needs a counterweight of similar volume placed opposite it. An unbalanced rotor vibrates, can crack tubes and gives uneven cell packing. Shortening the run or moving the tube beside another does not restore balance.
  36. The correct answer is D (Report an unresolved ABO discrepancy, obtain a new specimen, and give group O red cells meanwhile): The forward group does not match the previous record and the reverse reactions are missing, so ABO cannot be interpreted. The result is reported as an unresolved discrepancy and a new specimen is requested. Until it is resolved, group O red cells are the safe choice. Choosing a group from either record ignores the conflict.
  37. The correct answer is A (Hydatid cyst fluid or pigeon egg white, which contain soluble P1 substance): Hydatid cyst fluid and pigeon egg white contain soluble P1 substance, so adding either to the serum binds anti-P1 and removes its reactivity. Secretor saliva contains ABH and Lewis substances, not P1, and pooled plasma does not carry enough P1 to neutralize the antibody. Lewis substance neutralizes Lewis antibodies, not anti-P1.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (A delayed serologic reaction, because the new antibody appeared without evidence of red cell destruction): A new alloantibody found after transfusion without a falling hemoglobin, rising bilirubin or positive DAT is a delayed serologic transfusion reaction. A delayed hemolytic reaction needs evidence of red cell destruction as well. The timing of days rules out an acute reaction, and passive transfer would not make the antibody appear as a primary finding in the patient.
  2. The correct answer is D (Trace both units through ward and chart records and report them as unresolved until found): The 201 transfused, 11 restocked and 6 discarded units account for 218 of the 220 issued, leaving 2 with no outcome. Those must be traced through ward and chart records and shown as unresolved until found. Guessing a final outcome would put unverified data into the report.
  3. The correct answer is D (Notify the transfusion physician and request rare antigen-negative units from the blood supplier): The physician must weigh the risk of anemia against the risk of transfusing incompatible cells, and the laboratory supports that decision by requesting rare antigen-negative units from the blood supplier while identification continues. Group O Rh-negative blood is not antigen-negative for most high-prevalence antigens. Acting without informing the physician or waiting silently both delay care.
  4. The correct answer is B (Infant plasma has no reliable own isoagglutinins at this age, so reverse grouping is not valid): Newborns do not make their own anti-A and anti-B until several months old, so the reverse group would be blank or reflect passively acquired maternal antibody and cannot confirm the forward group. The omission is accepted practice, not a size problem. Maternal IgG does cross the placenta, which is why reverse grouping is unreliable.
  5. The correct answer is C (DAT positive with IgG coating and no complement, strengths reported, and the team notified promptly): The polyspecific and anti-IgG reactions are both 2+ while anti-C3d is negative, so the cells carry IgG and no detectable complement. The negative saline control and the 3+ check cells show the test is valid. A valid positive DAT in a jaundiced neonate should be reported with strengths and communicated to the team without waiting for an eluate.
  6. The correct answer is A (Anti-E, because every reactive cell carries E and the nonreactive cells lack it): Only the E positive cells, cells 2, 3 and 6, reacted, and every E negative cell was nonreactive, so the pattern matches anti-E. Cell 3 appears homozygous for E (E+ e0) and gave the strongest reaction. Anti-c is excluded because the c positive, E negative cells 4, 5, 7 and 8 were nonreactive, and anti-D is excluded by the nonreactive D positive cells 1 and 7.
  7. The correct answer is B (Notify the physician, request a new specimen, and provide group O red cells until resolved): The current forward and reverse results agree with each other, but they differ from the earlier record, and ABO group does not change. This suggests a wrong blood in tube event or a past record error, so the patient receives group O red cells until a new specimen settles the question. Group AB red cells are not a universal donor group.
  8. The correct answer is D (Removing donor white cells reduces the cytokines that accumulate during storage and provoke fever): Donor white cells release cytokines during storage, particularly in platelet units, and removing them before storage lowers the amount of pyrogen the recipient receives. Preventing engraftment requires irradiation, while leukoreduction neither sterilizes the unit nor removes plasma proteins.
  9. The correct answer is A (An eluate with antibody screen, because anti-A in the eluate points to donor lymphocytes): An eluate that contains anti-A, with no new alloantibody in the plasma, supports hemolysis from donor lymphocytes, because the antibody comes from the graft and matches the recipient group. A delayed reaction would show an alloantibody against an antigen on the transfused units. Cold agglutinin and warm autoantibody titres do not identify the source of the antibody.
  10. The correct answer is A (Investigate the two failing units and the filter lot, and hold the lot pending review): Two of ten units exceed 5 x10^6, which is a failure of the leukoreduction process and suggests a problem with a filter lot, a procedure step or handling. The service must investigate and hold related product until the cause is known. Accepting failures, ignoring the lot or repeating tests until they pass would hide the problem.
  11. The correct answer is C (A cold antibody reacting only below 37 C, so crossmatch-compatible units may be issued): Reactions that appear only in cold or refrigerated samples and disappear at 37 C and the antiglobulin phase point to a cold agglutinin of low clinical significance. Keeping the sample warm prevented the artifact on the repeat. Warm autoantibodies and alloantibodies would react at 37 C or the antiglobulin phase, and no reaction was in progress.
  12. The correct answer is D (Titre is above critical, so refer for fetal assessment with middle cerebral artery Doppler): A titre of 32 exceeds the stated critical level of 16 and rose by two dilutions from 8, so the pregnancy needs fetal assessment, typically with Doppler of the middle cerebral artery. Rh immune globulin prevents new sensitization but does not reverse existing immunization. The numbers show a clear rise.
  13. The correct answer is A (The supplier completes required donor testing before release, so hospital repeat testing is unnecessary): Donor infectious disease testing is performed by the blood supplier under national standards and results must be satisfactory before components are released. Hospital laboratories perform compatibility testing and verify labels but do not repeat donor screening. Bedside testing is not part of the process.
  14. The correct answer is B (Irradiation does not make the units radioactive, because the dose only inactivates donor lymphocytes): Gamma or X ray exposure passes through the unit and inactivates lymphocytes, but it does not induce radioactivity in the blood or plastic. The unit is therefore handled like any other component after treatment. Dosimeters and bag shielding are not the reason, because no radiation is retained to measure or absorb.
  15. The correct answer is C (Complete and document in-house validation of outlet temperature at expected flow rates, then train users): Equipment must be validated in the laboratory's own conditions before use, showing that outlet temperature stays in the safe range at the flow rates the clinical areas will use. A supplier certificate supports but does not replace this work. Users must also be trained, and validation after clinical use begins would expose patients to an untested device.
  16. The correct answer is D (Review the transfusion history and report group A with a comment on mixed field reactions): The transfused group O cells do not react with anti-A, so the patient's own group A cells produce agglutinates among unagglutinated donor cells, which is a mixed field. Checking the transfusion history explains the pattern, and the report keeps the native group A with a comment so later readers are not misled. Group O or AB would misstate the patient's own type.
  17. The correct answer is B (Drug modified cell membranes take up plasma proteins non-specifically, so no immune destruction follows): Some cephalosporins alter the red cell membrane so that plasma proteins, including IgG and complement, stick to it non-specifically. This gives a positive DAT without immune destruction, so hemoglobin and bilirubin stay normal. A drug dependent or induced antibody would be expected to cause hemolysis or a reactive screen.
  18. The correct answer is B (Remove it from use and report it, because turbidity signals possible contamination or deterioration): Turbidity or particles in a reagent point to bacterial contamination or protein breakdown, and the vial should be quarantined and reported even if controls passed. A passing control shows the reagent worked at that moment, not that it is safe to continue. Filtering or diluting a reagent changes its validated strength and is not permitted.
  19. The correct answer is A (Quarantine the units and contact the issuing laboratory, because they did not stay frozen): Frozen plasma must stay at or below minus 18 C until it is deliberately thawed, so soft units with condensation show that the frozen state was lost and their storage history cannot be trusted. The units are quarantined and the issuing laboratory is contacted so the deviation can be assessed and recorded. Refreezing or relabelling them would hide the loss of temperature control and could reduce labile factor activity.
  20. The correct answer is C (Select ABO-compatible, phenotype-matched units, using adsorbed serum to exclude any hidden alloantibodies before issue): Autoantibody reacts with nearly all donor cells, so crossmatch alone cannot show whether a unit is safe, and hidden alloantibodies can cause delayed or acute hemolysis. Adsorbed serum is tested to exclude alloantibodies, and ABO-compatible, phenotype-matched units reduce the chance of new alloimmunization. Transfusion is not withheld when the need is urgent and the physician accepts the risk.
  21. The correct answer is C (A physician signed statement of urgent need, which may be completed after release): Emergency release without full testing is a physician decision, so the laboratory needs a signed acknowledgement of urgent need and of the risk accepted. Treatment must not be delayed, so the signature can follow the release. An administrator, a crossmatch printout or a nurse repeat order is not what the policy asks for.
  22. The correct answer is A (Switch it off and keep the lid closed to let aerosols settle): After a breakage the rotor should be switched off and the lid kept closed for a set time, often about 30 minutes, so that aerosols settle before anyone opens the unit. Opening it early or wiping the window exposes the worker to infectious aerosols. Cleanup with disinfectant and gloves follows after the wait.
  23. The correct answer is A (Select E negative units with an antiglobulin crossmatch, because anti-E reacts at the antiglobulin phase): Anti-E is an IgG antibody reactive at the antiglobulin phase and is clinically significant, so E negative units and an antiglobulin crossmatch are needed. Anti-M reacts only at immediate spin and does not require avoidance. Immediate spin crossmatching alone would miss anti-E.
  24. The correct answer is D (Donor 2, because anti-A reacts, the lectin does not and A1 cells react in reverse grouping): Donor 2 is typed as group A by anti-A, but the A1 lectin does not agglutinate the cells, which marks an A2 subgroup. The 2+ reaction with A1 reverse cells shows that the donor serum contains anti-A1. Donor 1 is an A1 type, donor 3 is group A1B because anti-A, anti-B and the lectin all react, and donor 4 is group O because anti-A and anti-B are both negative.
  25. The correct answer is B (The run fails, because one point received less than the 15 Gy minimum): The requirement has two parts: at least 25 Gy to the central plane and no part of the unit below 15 Gy. The 14 Gy point violates the second part, so the central value and the average cannot rescue the run. The 31 Gy reading is not a problem because the stated policy sets no upper limit that this value breaks.
  26. The correct answer is B (Report all results with transfusion dates, and flag a possible delayed hemolytic reaction for review): A new alloantibody in the serum, the same specificity in the eluate and a positive DAT weeks after transfusion fit a delayed serologic or hemolytic reaction. The laboratory should present all results with the transfusion dates and flag the possibility for the medical director and physician. It should not call the diagnosis proven, since clinical review decides that, and it should not dismiss the pattern.
  27. The correct answer is D (Wash the cells extra times until the supernatant is clear, because jelly dilutes away with washing): Wharton jelly is removed by additional saline washes, and the supernatant should be clear before antiglobulin reagent is added. Extra reagent or warmth does not dissolve jelly, and reporting a false positive could trigger unnecessary investigation. The DAT must be repeated on clean cells.
  28. The correct answer is C (No hemolysis or incompatibility is shown, because all results agree, fitting a febrile nonhemolytic reaction): Matching clerical, ABO, plasma colour, DAT and antibody screen results show no evidence of hemolysis or serologic incompatibility, so the fever and chills fit a febrile nonhemolytic reaction, which is diagnosed after other causes are excluded. A negative DAT does not exclude bacterial contamination, so culture is still considered when the picture is more severe. Nothing in the table suggests an antibody-mediated delayed reaction.
  29. The correct answer is A (Culture the bag and the patient's blood, because bacterial contamination can cause fever and shock): With a correct clerical check, no free hemoglobin, a negative DAT and matching groups, hemolysis is unlikely. Fever with hypotension raises the possibility of bacterial contamination, which needs culture of both the component and the patient and notification of the supplier. Closing the case as febrile non-hemolytic is wrong because hypotension is not part of that reaction.
  30. The correct answer is C (Culture the retained bag and the patient's blood, because matching organisms confirm the source): Culturing both the retained bag and the patient's blood is needed because matching organisms from the two sources confirm the unit as the cause. An ABO error would produce hemolysis rather than isolated septic features, and discarding the bag destroys the evidence. Free hemoglobin may be measured but does not address sepsis.
  31. The correct answer is C (Read back the request, including patient identifiers and units, and confirm the pickup arrangements): A short read back catches errors in identifiers or quantities at almost no time cost. Verbal orders are accepted in emergencies when they are confirmed and documented later. Waiting for written orders delays products the patient needs now.
  32. The correct answer is D (Document the incidents and use the complaint process, because respect supports safe communication): Harassment harms wellbeing and safety because staff who fear mockery avoid asking for help. Recording specific incidents and using the formal complaint process allows management to act. Avoidance, retaliation and informal settings do not protect the employee or fix the culture.
  33. The correct answer is C (Disclose the skipped step in the investigation, because honesty supports learning and safety): A skipped required step is a deviation that must be reported even when it did not cause the event, because the same lapse could cause harm another time. Selective reporting undermines the investigation. Honest disclosure is a professional obligation and is treated as a learning opportunity.
  34. The correct answer is A (All positive samples react and negatives stay negative, with strengths matching the old lot): A new lot is accepted when it detects every known positive and gives no false positives, with reaction strengths comparable to the lot in use. Much stronger reactions can signal a change in reagent behaviour that needs review, missing two positives is a failure, and negatives are required to show specificity.
  35. The correct answer is C (Discard and document it, because storage conditions during the extra time cannot be verified): Once the unit has been out longer than the validated limit, there is no proof it stayed within a safe temperature range, even if it feels cold. The unit should be discarded and the event documented under the quality system. A seal and a cold surface are not accepted evidence of storage temperature, and waiting in a refrigerator cannot undo the excursion.
  36. The correct answer is D (About 3000 rpm, which gives close to 1000 x g at 10 cm): Solving 1000 = 1.118 x 10^-5 x 10 x rpm squared gives rpm squared of about 8.9 million, so the speed is about 3000 rpm. Force depends on the square of the speed, so it does not equal rpm and does not rise in direct proportion. At 2000 rpm the force would be only about 447 x g, and at 4000 rpm about 1789 x g.
  37. The correct answer is A (Gram stain and culture of the platelet unit and the patient's blood to detect bacteria): Rigors, high fever and hypotension during a platelet transfusion strongly suggest bacterial contamination, since platelets are stored at room temperature and support bacterial growth. Gram stain and culture of the unit and the patient's blood confirm it and allow the supplier to be notified. The other tests address immune or allergic causes that do not fit this presentation.
  38. The correct answer is C (Do not collect, and refer the deferral to the supervisor for registry investigation): A permanent deferral in the registry must be honoured until the owner of the record has investigated it, because it may protect recipients from an infectious donation. The collector has no authority to override or delete it, and collecting then quarantining still exposes the donor to an unnecessary procedure. Referring it to a supervisor lets the error be corrected properly if it is one.
  39. The correct answer is C (Cooling the blood before serum separation, because cold agglutinins bind red cells at lower temperatures): Cold agglutinins bind red cells strongly below body temperature, so blood should be kept warm until serum or plasma has been separated. Cooling the tube lets the antibody coat the patient's cells and clump them in the tube. Warming, anticoagulant and vein choice do not create this pattern.
  40. The correct answer is B (Special requirements such as irradiation or CMV-safe status, because these change component selection): Components must be modified or selected for patients who need irradiated or CMV-safe products, so the request must state those needs. Occupation, home address and shift patterns do not change which component is safe to issue. A telephone extension helps communication but not product selection.
Case CHB9

An outpatient clinic sends a 4 mL EDTA tube and a requisition to the blood bank for an urgent crossmatch of two red cell units. The tube label shows the patient's surname, given name and health card number written in pen. No collection date, time or collector initials appear. The requisition lists the same name and number. The patient received a transfusion two weeks ago.

  1. The correct answer is C (Date, time and collector identification are missing, so the draw cannot be traced): Transfusion labels need the collection date, time and the collector's identification so the draw can be traced and the specimen age checked. Handwritten labels are acceptable when complete. Four millilitres is ample, and a health card number is a valid second identifier.
  2. The correct answer is A (Notify the clinic at once, record the reason, and ask for a newly collected labelled sample): A rejected specimen is reported to the sender straight away with the reason documented, and a replacement is requested. The laboratory cannot supply collection details it did not witness. Partial testing or verbal verification still rests on a tube that cannot be traced.
  3. The correct answer is B (Collect a further sample, because the transfusion history limits the specimen to three days): A patient transfused within three months falls under the short validity limit, and day five is beyond day three, so a new sample is required. A negative screen on an earlier day does not show that no antibody has formed since. Two weeks since transfusion is inside the three month window, not outside it.
  4. The correct answer is C (Stop the draw and lay the donor flat, because brain blood flow must improve): These signs indicate a vasovagal reaction caused by a drop in blood pressure and pulse. Removing the needle and lowering the donor flat restores blood flow to the brain, whereas continuing the draw, sitting upright or standing can lead to fainting and injury.
  5. The correct answer is D (Patient identifiers, results, test date, and the identity of the person who performed the test): Accreditation standards require every result to be traceable to the patient, the date and the person who did the test. Lot numbers, ward details or a final group alone cannot show who performed or verified the work. Keeping identifiers, results, date and technologist identity lets an auditor reconstruct what was done.
  6. The correct answer is B (It is clinically significant, so antigen negative units are needed, as delayed hemolysis can occur): Anti-Jka is an IgG antibody that is usually clinically significant and a classic cause of delayed hemolytic reactions, because the titre can fall below detection between exposures. The physician must know that antigen negative units have to be selected and compatibility confirmed at the antiglobulin phase. Treating it as minor or filing it without notice leaves the patient exposed.
  7. The correct answer is B (Direct observation of actual procedures being performed, because a written test cannot show technical skill): Competence is shown by watching the technologist perform the real procedures, which a written test cannot do. Orientation sign off and exam scores show knowledge and attendance, not hands-on performance. A physician review of reports is a useful check but does not replace direct assessment by qualified laboratory staff.
  8. The correct answer is C (Three vials, since the calculated two vials are increased by one for measurement error): The calculation is 1.2 x 50 = 60 mL of fetal whole blood, and 60 divided by 30 mL per vial gives 2 vials. The stated policy adds one vial to allow for counting error, giving 3. A single vial covers only about 30 mL of fetal whole blood, far less than the 2 percent suggested.
  9. The correct answer is A (Request a fresh specimen collected carefully and mixed well, because clots invalidate the grouping results): A clotted or poorly mixed EDTA specimen gives unreliable cell and plasma reactions, and the partial pattern is not interpretable. The technologist should obtain a properly collected, well mixed specimen rather than guess a group from incomplete data. Filtering, saline dilution and reporting from the forward group alone all risk a wrong ABO group being issued.
  10. The correct answer is A (Icteric plasma is acceptable, because bilirubin does not cause or block agglutination): Bilirubin colours the plasma but does not cause or prevent agglutination of red cells, so grouping remains valid. Only the reading can be harder, so tubes can be compared against a white background. Saline replacement or heating adds nothing, and unneeded recollection stresses a neonate.
  11. The correct answer is B (4 units, because 900 mL divided by 250 mL is 3.6, which rounds to 4): The dose is 60 kg multiplied by 15 mL/kg, which equals 900 mL. Dividing by 250 mL gives 3.6 units, which rounds to 4. Rounding down to 3 contradicts the stated rule, and the other answers come from incorrect operations.
  12. The correct answer is B (1 to 6 C, with expiry up to 42 days from collection): Red cells in additive solution are stored at 1 to 6 C for up to 42 days from collection. Room temperature with agitation applies to platelets, and freezer storage applies to plasma. The 2 to 8 C range belongs to reagents and not to blood components.
  13. The correct answer is C (The unit was exposed to radiation, but periodic dosimetry still verifies the delivered dose): The label colour change shows that the bag was exposed to ionizing radiation, which helps prevent an unirradiated unit from being issued. It does not measure the exact dose to every part of the bag, so the dose must still be confirmed by routine dosimetry and timer checks. Irradiation does not sterilize the unit or extend expiry, and no indicator proves that every lymphocyte was inactivated.
  14. The correct answer is D (Peak temperature, time outside 1 to 6 C, units affected, actions taken and staff involved): The log shows the readings at 8 hours (6.8 C) and 10 hours (7.4 C) above 6 C, while the 12 hour reading of 5.2 C is back within range. Disposition depends on how high the temperature rose, how long units were outside range, which units were affected, what was done and who was involved, so these are recorded. Service dates, alarm silencing details or donor sources do not describe the excursion itself.
  15. The correct answer is A (Yes, because A2B red cells lack A1 antigen and some A2B people make anti-A1): A2B red cells carry A2 and B antigens but lack A1 antigen, and some people with this type form anti-A1, which weakly agglutinates the A1 reagent cells. Anti-B is absent because the patient has B antigen, so B cells stay negative. A true anti-A would also react with A2 cells, which would not be expected in a group AB patient.
  16. The correct answer is C (Very early infection before antibodies develop, so the unit is discarded and the donor referred): A negative antibody screen with a reactive nucleic acid test fits the window period of early infection, when viral genes are present but antibodies are not yet made. Such a donation is not released, and the donor is deferred and referred through the supplier. Resolved infection and vaccination do not give this pattern.
  17. The correct answer is B (Quarantine the units, check the incubator and agitator, and review exposure time against policy): From hour 15 the readings are above 24 C, so the stored platelets have left their validated range and must be held while the cause and the exposure time are reviewed. Cold storage damages platelets, and assuming the probe is wrong without checking it risks issuing an unsafe unit.
  18. The correct answer is D (Remove the vial from use and document it, because turbidity can signal contamination or lost reactivity): A cloudy reagent with particles may be contaminated or may have lost reactivity, even when it is in date and yesterday's quality control was acceptable. The vial should be removed from use and the finding documented, and a replacement used. Warming or filtering does not make a suspect reagent safe, and urgent workload does not change that.
  19. The correct answer is A (Quarantine the vial and open a new one, since weak reactions suggest deterioration): A drop from 4+ to 1+ after storage outside the required temperature points to deterioration of that one vial. It should be removed, and a fresh vial must pass quality control before use. Changing spin time hides the problem, and vials stored correctly that gave expected results do not need to be discarded.
  20. The correct answer is D (Dosage: cells homozygous for the antigen carry more antigen sites than heterozygous cells): Cells homozygous for the antigen carry a double dose, so the same antibody reacts more strongly with them than with heterozygous cells. Here Jk(a+b0) cells give 3+ and Jk(a+b+) cells give 1+, and cells lacking Jka do not react. This is dosage, typical of Kidd antibodies, and not prozone or a second antibody.
  21. The correct answer is A (10 to 15 mL/kg, because this usually raises coagulation factor levels adequately): A typical plasma dose is 10 to 15 mL/kg, which usually raises factor levels enough to correct a deficiency. Doses of 1 to 5 mL/kg are too small to be effective. Doses of 30 mL/kg or more risk circulatory overload and are not a standard starting dose.
  22. The correct answer is C (Acceptable, because frozen plasma is held at minus 18 C or colder): Frozen plasma is stored at minus 18 C or colder, so minus 22 C is within the requirement. A lower temperature is used by some laboratories for longer expiry but is not mandatory for acceptance. Storage at 1 to 6 C applies only after thaw, and a warming period would damage the product.
  23. The correct answer is B (Add a water filled tube opposite the odd one, because uneven loads strain the rotor): A centrifuge rotor must carry balanced loads, so an odd tube needs a water filled balance tube of similar volume opposite it. Grouping tubes on one side or spinning an uneven load strains the bearings and can damage the rotor. The lid must be closed during the spin to contain aerosols and protect staff.

Practice Test 11 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Warm the sample to 37 C and rerun, because clumps falsely lower the red count): Cold agglutinin clumps are counted as one large cell by the analyzer, which lowers the RBC count, raises the MCV and distorts the MCH and MCHC. Warming the sample to 37 C disperses the clumps so a repeat measurement is reliable. Cooling or diluting with cold saline would keep the clumps in place.
  2. The correct answer is B (Washed red cells, because washing removes residual plasma proteins, including IgA, that trigger the reaction): Anaphylaxis from anti-IgA is triggered by the small amount of donor IgA in the plasma around the red cells. Saline washing removes most of that plasma, so washed cells are the right selection from this inventory. Leukoreduction and irradiation act on white cells rather than plasma proteins, and ABO compatibility does not influence IgA.
  3. The correct answer is D (Yes, report it as a nonconformance, because near misses reveal weak points before harm occurs): Near misses are nonconformances even when caught early, because they show where the system can fail and allow trends to be found. Harm is not required for reporting. The laboratory that detects the event reports it to quality staff, rather than relying on the ward.
  4. The correct answer is D (Reject it and request recollection, because a birth date mismatch leaves patient identity unconfirmed): Every identifier on the tube must agree with the requisition, so a differing birth date means the specimen cannot be trusted as belonging to that patient. Labels are not changed in the laboratory, and testing before identity is settled risks reporting a wrong type.
  5. The correct answer is B (Out of tolerance, because an 8 second shortfall is about 13 percent of the setting): A 52 second spin is 8 seconds short of 60, which is 13.3 percent of the setting and well beyond the 5 percent limit. Short spins leave cells poorly packed and the saline wash incomplete, which can affect later antiglobulin reactions. The washer must be removed from use, repaired and rechecked before patient testing resumes.
  6. The correct answer is C (Partial blockage of the aspiration probes for those positions; clean them and repeat the check): Weak check cell reactions confined to two positions point to a problem with those positions rather than the saline, the reagent or the spin settings, which would affect every tube. A partly blocked aspiration probe leaves excess saline that dilutes antihuman globulin. The probes should be cleaned or replaced and the check repeated before the washer is released.
  7. The correct answer is A (Run a DAT and review recent transfusions, because pink plasma may reflect in vivo hemolysis): Pink plasma in a recently transfused patient whose earlier plasma was clear could signal a delayed hemolytic reaction rather than a collection fault. A DAT and review of the transfusion history help separate the two. Rejecting or diluting would risk missing a real reaction.
  8. The correct answer is D (Hold both results and recollect under witness, because one tube may be from another patient): Two specimens with the same patient identifiers but different ABO groups mean at least one tube came from another person. Neither group can be trusted, so results are held, the discrepancy is investigated with the supervisor and a new specimen is collected with positive patient identification. Choosing the first or the most recent result would risk an ABO incompatible transfusion.
  9. The correct answer is C (Absorbent wrapped tube in the sealed bag, then both inside the rigid outer box): Triple packaging means a leakproof primary container, a sealed secondary container with absorbent material that can hold the whole volume, and a rigid outer package. Placing the layers on the outside or leaving out the absorbent material removes the protection if the tube leaks. The layers must nest in order.
  10. The correct answer is C (ABO unresolved with discrepancy details, and group O red cells for transfusion until resolved): A group A patient should have anti-B in the plasma, so the absent reverse reaction is a discrepancy, often from weak antibodies in older patients. The record should state that ABO is unresolved, describe the findings, and direct use of group O red cells until the group is settled. Assigning a group from the forward reaction alone, or guessing group O or AB, risks an incompatible transfusion.
  11. The correct answer is D (The blood supplier, whose medical staff can review the donor and quarantine related components): TRALI is linked to antibodies in the donor's plasma, so the supplier's medical staff must review the donor and quarantine related components. Hospitals do not hold donor contact details for investigation, and neither the pharmacy nor the college manages donor review.
  12. The correct answer is A (A recipient of a unit from a first-degree relative, because shared HLA haplotypes allow engraftment): Donors who are first-degree relatives often share an HLA haplotype with the recipient, so the recipient does not recognize the donor lymphocytes as foreign and they can engraft. Irradiation prevents this. Anemia, past febrile reactions and orthopedic surgery do not by themselves create that risk, and irradiation does not prevent cytokine-mediated fever.
  13. The correct answer is A (Test with antigen-negative reagent cells, because a nonreactive result helps identify the antibody): A serum that reacts 2+ with every panel cell yet leaves the autocontrol nonreactive suggests an antibody to a high-prevalence antigen. Testing with rare antigen-negative cells narrows the specificity because a nonreactive cell lacks the target antigen. Autoadsorption is for autoantibodies, and enzyme treatment would not clarify a high-prevalence antibody.
  14. The correct answer is D (It stops donor lymphocytes from proliferating, preventing transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease): Gamma or X-ray irradiation damages the DNA of donor T lymphocytes so they cannot multiply in an immunocompromised recipient, which prevents transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease. Removing white cells is the role of leukoreduction, and irradiation does not sterilize the unit or change red cell antigens.
  15. The correct answer is A (Group A Rh positive, because forward and reverse results agree and D is present): Anti-A agglutinated the cells and anti-B did not, so the forward group is A, and anti-D agglutination makes the patient Rh positive. The reverse group confirms it, because a group A patient has anti-B (B cells agglutinated) and no anti-A (A1 cells negative). Forward and reverse agree, so the group can be reported.
  16. The correct answer is C (Issue group O Rh-positive platelets, notify the physician, and arrange Rh immune globulin per policy): ABO identical platelets are preferred for best increments, and an Rh-negative woman of childbearing potential should receive Rh-negative platelets when available. When only Rh-positive units exist, treatment should not be delayed. The physician is told of the anti-D risk, and immune globulin covers the small red cell content.
  17. The correct answer is D (ABO-compatible Rh negative red cells, because the policy treats her as Rh negative for transfusion): The policy stated in the stem treats a weak D positive female of childbearing potential as Rh negative when she receives red cells, so ABO-compatible Rh negative units are chosen to avoid anti-D formation. Rh positive units would risk anti-D that could harm later pregnancies. Waiting for DNA testing is not required by the policy and would delay a planned procedure.
  18. The correct answer is B (It welds tubing with heat so the system stays closed and keeps the longer expiry): A sterile connecting device cuts and welds tubing with heat, so components can be joined without opening the closed system. Because no air enters, the product can keep the longer expiry assigned to closed-system preparation. It does not sterilize contents, filter bacteria or mix the units.
  19. The correct answer is B (Corrective action, because it eliminates the cause of a nonconformance that has already occurred): Corrective action removes the cause of a nonconformance that has been detected so that it does not happen again. Preventive action deals with potential problems, containment limits immediate harm, and verification only checks that the action worked.
  20. The correct answer is A (Repeat with a correct suspension, because too few cells can cause false negatives): A suspension that is too dilute gives too few cells to form visible agglutinates, which can produce a falsely negative result for a weak antibody. The test must be repeated with a correctly standardized suspension. Reporting the card as negative, adding plasma or extending incubation does not repair the test.
  21. The correct answer is C (Acknowledge the concern, explain the expected timing, and offer an update when work is complete): Professional communication stays calm, acknowledges the clinician's worry and gives honest timing. Escalating or hanging up damages teamwork and may delay care. An unrealistic promise creates a second problem when it is not met.
  22. The correct answer is B (Pause the transfusion, give an antihistamine, and resume if symptoms settle and the physician agrees): For mild urticaria with normal vital signs, the transfusion is paused, an antihistamine is given, and the unit may be resumed if symptoms resolve and the physician approves under local policy. Continuing without pausing ignores the protocol, whereas anaphylaxis treatment and a full hemolysis workup are reserved for more severe or atypical findings.
  23. The correct answer is A (Invalid and must be repeated, because the negative check cells show anti-human globulin was neutralized): Check cells must agglutinate in every negative antiglobulin tube, since they prove active anti-human globulin remained after washing. When they do not react, inadequate washing or a missed reagent addition may have caused a false negative. The test is repeated rather than reported.
  24. The correct answer is A (Correct the form, document the error and correction, and repeat the unit identity check): The error is clerical, so the fix is to correct the record transparently and then repeat the identity check between tag and form before issue. The unit itself is not harmed by a mistake on a form. Leaving an incorrect form in place invites a mismatch later.
  25. The correct answer is B (20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation, so 26 C is above the range): Platelets are stored at 20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation to keep them viable, so 26 C is a true excursion above range. Refrigeration would damage platelets, frozen storage applies to plasma, and a bench incubator temperature is for serology reactions, not storage.
  26. The correct answer is D (Restrict access to authorized staff and dispose of paper records by secure shredding): Safeguards include role-based access and secure destruction of paper that carries identifiers. Shared logins remove the audit trail that shows who viewed a record. Recycling bins and open trays expose names and results to anyone passing by.
  27. The correct answer is D (Issue the bag, because aggregates that disperse on kneading do not indicate a quality problem): Small aggregates that break up fully with gentle kneading and leave a swirling pattern are usually acceptable under policies that permit dispersible aggregates. Clumps that do not disperse would be a reason to hold the unit. Refrigeration damages platelets and does not reverse aggregation.
  28. The correct answer is A (Patient identification, unit number, time of onset, symptoms, and the most recent vital signs): The laboratory needs to link the report to the right patient and component and to understand the timing and severity, so identification, unit number, onset time, symptoms and vital signs are required. An opinion about the cause is not a substitute for observations. Expiry date, ordering physician and bed number do not describe the reaction.
  29. The correct answer is C (Workers need ready access to data sheets on every shift, so locked storage is unacceptable): Under WHMIS 2015 the employer must make current safety data sheets readily available to workers who may be exposed, on every shift. A locked office closed at night defeats that purpose, since an exposure may happen when no manager is present. Suppliers provide the sheets but the workplace must still hold and share them, and colour printing is not a requirement.
  30. The correct answer is A (Report it through the proper channel, because falsified logs endanger stored blood): Records are the proof that blood was stored within limits, so falsified entries hide risk and mislead the quality system. Professionals report such practice through the defined route. Covering for a colleague or gossiping does not correct the problem, and waiting for failure delays prevention.
  31. The correct answer is C (Reopen the investigation to find the real root cause, because retraining did not stop recurrence): A corrective action is only effective if the problem stops. An unchanged rate means the real cause, here probably hand-written labels and inconsistent forms, was not addressed. The record is reopened and a system fix is chosen rather than repeating the same training.
  32. The correct answer is D (Keep it in quarantine and report the discrepancy, because release needs a label matching results): Release requires that the label, the confirmed ABO and Rh result and the completed donor tests all agree. A group O label on a group A unit could lead to an incompatible transfusion, so the unit stays in quarantine until the discrepancy is investigated and corrected through the quality system rather than by hand relabelling.
  33. The correct answer is C (Existing hemolysis can mask hemolysis caused by an antibody, hiding a true positive): A hemolytic antibody such as anti-Jka may lyse screening cells, and that lysis counts as a positive reaction. When the plasma is already pink the lysis cannot be seen, so a true positive may be missed. Free hemoglobin does not neutralize antigens and colour alone does not show an antibody, so a fresh sample is preferred.
  34. The correct answer is B (Patient cells or plasma may enter the vial and cause false reactions later): Touching a reagent dropper to a patient sample can transfer cells, plasma or antibody into the vial, contaminating every later test run with it. The risk is false positive or negative results, not a change in titre or temperature. Droppers are held clear of the tube contents to prevent this.
  35. The correct answer is B (Units 2 and 4, since they lack the E antigen and the antiglobulin crossmatch is nonreactive): The patient needs units negative for the antigen of the known antibody and compatible at the antiglobulin phase. Unit 5 is E-positive and must be excluded even though it was nonreactive, because the antibody may be weak. Unit 3 is E-negative but incompatible, suggesting another antibody.
  36. The correct answer is D (Soluble antigen binds the antibody, so reactions disappear if the specificity is correct): Soluble Lewis substance combines with Lewis antibody in the serum and ties it up, so the antibody can no longer agglutinate cells carrying the antigen. Loss of reactivity in the treated aliquot, compared with the untreated aliquot, supports the specificity. The substance does not change complement, antibody class or the antigens on the panel cells.
  37. The correct answer is B (0.15 mL of packed cells, because 3 percent of 5 mL equals 0.15 mL): A 3 percent suspension means 3 mL of cells per 100 mL, so 5 mL needs 0.03 x 5 = 0.15 mL of packed cells, with saline added to reach 5 mL. The other volumes come from applying the percentage to the wrong total or moving the decimal point incorrectly.
  38. The correct answer is B (Ask the patient to state name and birth date, then compare both with the band): An active reply from the patient, checked against the band, confirms identity because the patient cannot simply agree with a wrong name. Reading the name aloud invites a nod from a drowsy or confused patient. A bed card and a relative are not reliable sources because both can be wrong or out of date.
  39. The correct answer is D (3.08 x10^11 platelets, so the unit meets the minimum content requirement): Content equals concentration times volume: 1100 x10^9/L multiplied by 0.280 L gives 308 x10^9, which is 3.08 x10^11. This is above the 3.0 x10^11 minimum. Forgetting to convert millilitres to litres shifts the answer by a factor of ten or one thousand.
  40. The correct answer is A (Weak agglutination may be hard to see, so a true antibody could be missed): With too few cells, the agglutinates formed are small and sparse, so a weak reaction may look like a smooth negative button. The concentration range of 2 to 5 percent gives enough cells to read reactions confidently. A light suspension does not cause hemolysis or neutralize antiglobulin reagent.
  41. The correct answer is D (Intramuscular epinephrine, with the line kept open with saline for fluid and drug access): Stridor with hypotension and hives is anaphylaxis, which needs immediate intramuscular epinephrine while the transfusion remains stopped and the line is kept open for fluids. Antihistamines alone are too slow, acetaminophen treats fever rather than airway compromise, and restarting the unit would give more allergen.
  42. The correct answer is D (The new lot is two doubling dilutions weaker, which exceeds policy, so it fails): The final positive dilution fell from 1:64 to 1:16, which is two doubling steps (64 to 32 to 16), so the new lot is weaker. Policy permits only one doubling dilution, so the comparison fails. A higher dilution number at the end point means more potent reagent, which is why the new lot is not stronger.
  43. The correct answer is A (Drug-induced antibody coating is likely, because eluates often react only when drug is added): A positive DAT with a negative plasma screen and a negative eluate is a recognized pattern for drug-induced immune hemolysis, because the antibody may need the drug present to react and the drug may be bound to the cells. A valid negative eluate with a negative last wash is a real result, not a failed procedure. Further testing with drug-treated cells would be referred to a reference laboratory.
  44. The correct answer is A (Tell the physician work-up is pending, record who was told and when, and hold units): A new positive screen means the earlier crossmatch cannot be relied on until the antibody is identified. The physician must be told promptly that work-up is pending, and the notification must be recorded with the name and time. Holding the allocated units prevents release of blood that may be incompatible, while cancelling surgery is not a laboratory decision.
  45. The correct answer is B (A positive autocontrol suggests antibody already coating the patient cells, which the test confirms): When the autocontrol reacts together with every screening cell, the patient own red cells are probably coated with antibody, as in an autoimmune process. A DAT detects this coating, and an eluate or adsorption may follow. Identifying a specific alloantibody comes later because autoantibody must first be dealt with.
  46. The correct answer is C (Record temperatures, times and unit numbers, quarantine the units, and open a nonconformance for review): A temperature excursion needs a complete record of the readings, the times and the affected units, with the units held in quarantine until a qualified person decides disposition. A nonconformance is opened so the cause can be investigated. Returning units to stock or discarding them without reporting skips that review.
  47. The correct answer is A (Likely passive anti-D from recent immune globulin, with the dose date noted and titre followed): Recent Rh immune globulin produces passive anti-D, typically at a low titre, which fits a titre of 2 eight days after the dose. The report should state the likely passive origin, note the dose date and follow later titres, since an active response would rise over time. Calling it confirmed immunization or an error ignores the documented injection.
  48. The correct answer is C (Report the high titre and thermal range promptly, because reactivity near cooled temperatures can cause hemolysis): A high titre antibody that still reacts at 30 C could bind red cells and activate complement when the patient is cooled during bypass. The surgical team needs the titre and thermal range so they can plan warming strategies. Reporting it as insignificant ignores the planned cooling.
  49. The correct answer is C (Anti-A 0, anti-B 0, anti-D 4+, A1 cells 3+, B cells 3+, expected for group O): A group O person has no A or B antigen, so the forward tubes are negative with anti-A and anti-B, and the plasma contains both anti-A and anti-B, so A1 and B cells are agglutinated. Rh positive is shown by a strong anti-D reaction. Reaction sets that leave out the reverse reactions fit other groups or very young infants.
  50. The correct answer is A (The transplant date and donor ABO group, because the current group may reflect donor cells): After allogeneic transplant the patient's red cell and plasma antibodies can shift toward the donor's ABO group, so the laboratory needs the transplant date and donor group to interpret mixed or changed results. Location and old record numbers do not explain a changed ABO result. Pre-transplant antibody results alone leave out the donor information.
  51. The correct answer is C (About 10 microsieverts per hour, because the dose rate falls to one quarter): For a small source the dose rate varies with the inverse square of distance, so doubling the distance cuts the rate to one quarter, giving 10 microsieverts per hour. Halving would apply only to a simple linear relationship, and an eighth would need a cube law. Distance is one of the three protective factors along with time and shielding.
  52. The correct answer is D (Dated records of units found, quarantined or returned, and who completed each step): An auditor needs traceable evidence of what was done, when, to which units and by whom. The notice alone shows what was asked but not what was done. An inventory total and a general statement do not show unit level action or notification of patient care teams.
  53. The correct answer is A (Wrong blood in tube, because a different patient's blood can still give a consistent group): A true change from group B to group A does not occur in a patient without transplantation, and a subgroup of B never types as A. Acquired B affects group A patients, and a cold autoantibody would disturb the reverse group. A sample from the wrong patient is the most likely cause and must be ruled out.
  54. The correct answer is C (Perform the testing, flag the refusal on the record, and issue nothing without new consent): Consent to testing is separate from consent to transfusion. The laboratory can complete the type and screen while recording the refusal so no product is prepared or released unless the patient later agrees in a documented way. Crossmatching units or leaving the refusal off the record would work against the patient's decision.
  55. The correct answer is A (About 5 to 10 minutes, since no incubation or antiglobulin phase is required): An immediate-spin crossmatch mixes patient serum with donor cells, centrifuges briefly and reads for agglutination, which takes only a few minutes. The 45 minute figure belongs to a tube antiglobulin procedure. Reading still needs a technologist, so the step cannot be completed in seconds.
  56. The correct answer is D (Report the result and retain the sample, because a negative DAT shows no bound antibody): A negative cord DAT means the infant cells are not coated by maternal antibody, so no further serology is needed beyond keeping the sample for repeat testing if jaundice develops. An eluate is reserved for a positive DAT. Immune globulin is given to Rh negative mothers, not infants.
  57. The correct answer is B (Test it with positive and negative cells and document the results before use): A new lot must be shown to react correctly with known antigen-positive cells and to give no reaction with antigen-negative cells, and the results must be recorded. The supplier certificate does not replace the laboratory's own verification. A patient sample cannot serve as a control because its true result is not yet known.
  58. The correct answer is B (Remove it until repaired and verified, because 33 C is out of range): An incubator outside its acceptable range, usually 36 to 38 C, cannot reliably detect IgG antibodies and must be taken out of service until corrected and verified. Extra time or a dial change without verification does not restore validated conditions. Tests incubated at 33 C would be unreliable.
  59. The correct answer is A (Reverse grouping confirms the forward result, because anti-A and anti-B must match the missing antigens): The unit's plasma antibodies must match the antigens its red cells lack, so reverse grouping is an independent check on the forward result and catches subgroup, reagent or labelling problems. It does not determine Rh type, and red cell antigens remain detectable in stored units.
  60. The correct answer is C (Contact the clinician, confirm the patient's current wishes, and hold issue until consent is documented): A refusal flag stays in force until the patient's decision is documented as changed. Because the order is routine, there is time to contact the clinician and confirm current wishes. Issuing on the basis of an old flag, deleting it or cancelling the order without discussion all ignore the patient's right to decide.
  61. The correct answer is C (Work up the antibody and follow hemoglobin, because early coating may precede overt hemolysis): A new mixed field positive DAT soon after transfusion can reflect antibody-coated donor cells in the circulation alongside the patient's own cells, an early sign of a delayed reaction. The laboratory should identify any antibody from the serum and an eluate and follow the hemoglobin, rather than ignoring it or declaring an acute reaction. Giving unmatched units would add risk.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (HIV, hepatitis B, hepatitis C, HTLV and syphilis screening, with nucleic acid testing for viruses): Donations are screened for HIV, hepatitis B and C, HTLV and syphilis, and nucleic acid testing is used for key viruses to shorten the window period. ABO and Rh grouping are blood group tests, not infection screening. Hospitals do not repeat donor infectious disease screening.
  2. The correct answer is C (Reject the sample as mislabelled and request a new one promptly, while explaining the urgency): A sample with an unreliable label cannot be used for pretransfusion testing, whatever the physician says. The technologist rejects it, asks for a fresh sample and offers emergency release options if the patient cannot wait. Relabelling by the laboratory would defeat the purpose of bedside identification.
  3. The correct answer is A (A maternal EDTA sample drawn after delivery, because fetal cells enter her blood mostly then): Fetal cells enter the maternal circulation mostly around delivery, so an anticoagulated maternal sample drawn soon after birth detects the bleed. Cord blood tests the infant, not the mother's circulating fetal cells. A sample drawn weeks later or at 28 weeks would not measure the delivery bleed.
  4. The correct answer is D (An audit trail, which protects data integrity by showing who changed a result and when): An audit trail keeps a permanent, time stamped history of changes, so the original result, the edit and the person responsible can always be reviewed. This protects data integrity and supports investigations. Backups, access lists and reagent lot logs are useful controls but do not record who edited a result.
  5. The correct answer is D (A titre of 16, the reciprocal of the highest dilution that still shows 1+ agglutination): The titre is the reciprocal of the highest dilution that still gives a 1+ reaction, which here is 1:16. The first negative tube and the number of tubes are not the endpoint, and counting tubes confuses dilution steps with reaction strength.
  6. The correct answer is B (Group O red cells, because they lack antigens for both sets of antibodies): The recipient plasma carries anti-A and the donor line will make anti-B, so red cells must be free of both antigens until the donor line takes over. Group O red cells meet this need. Group A cells would meet the recipient anti-A and group B cells would meet donor derived anti-B, while group AB cells would meet both.
Case CHB10

A 78-year-old woman in a long-term care home is booked for elective surgery and needs a group and screen. She received two units of red cells five weeks ago. Her left arm has lymphedema after a mastectomy, and a saline infusion is running into a forearm vein of her right arm. Her veins are fragile, and the collector has only one chance at collection before the surgery booking.

  1. The correct answer is B (Pause the infusion, then draw below its site on the right, because that avoids dilution): The right arm is the only usable limb because lymphedema in the left arm raises the risk of swelling and infection. Stopping the infusion briefly and drawing below its site prevents dilution of the specimen with saline. Drawing from the cannula or above the infusion contaminates the sample and can give falsely weak reactions.
  2. The correct answer is A (Three days, because recent transfusion can stimulate new antibodies within weeks): Patients transfused or pregnant within the last three months can form new red cell antibodies quickly, so the specimen is valid for only three days, with the day of draw counted as day zero. Longer limits would risk missing a newly formed antibody. Stable patients with no recent exposure are the only ones for whom a longer local limit may apply.
  3. The correct answer is B (Reject the tube and request recollection, because two identifiers are required on the label): A pretransfusion specimen must carry two unique patient identifiers that match the requisition, because a mislabelled tube is a leading cause of ABO incompatible transfusion. A witnessed draw reported by phone does not replace a complete label, and a matching historical group does not prove the sample came from this patient. Recollection is quick compared with the risk of a wrong blood in tube error.
  4. The correct answer is A (Indicator cells spread across the well surface as a diffuse layer instead of a button): In solid phase testing, a negative result lets the indicator cells settle into a tight button at the bottom center. When patient antibody has attached to the coated well, the anti-IgG indicator cells bind it and spread as a layer across the well surface. A button therefore means no antibody was detected, and lysis or an edge ring is not the accepted positive pattern.
  5. The correct answer is C (Hemoglobin F resists acid elution, so fetal cells stay pink while adult hemoglobin washes out): Fetal hemoglobin is resistant to elution in acid, so fetal cells keep their hemoglobin and stain pink. Adult hemoglobin is eluted and leaves ghost cells. The stain depends on hemoglobin type, not on any Rh antigen.
  6. The correct answer is A (Issue emergency group O red cells, Rh negative if available, while the sample is tested immediately): Emergency release uses group O red cells with physician acknowledgement, and Rh negative units are preferred for females of childbearing potential, while testing proceeds without delay. Group AB is the universal plasma donor, not the red cell donor, and guessing the group is unsafe.
  7. The correct answer is D (Treat the higher reading as real, quarantine the units and investigate which device is accurate): When two devices disagree, the safe approach is to treat the worse reading as real until calibration checks show otherwise, and to hold the units for review. Choosing the lower value or averaging has no basis and could release unsafe units. Discarding everything is not warranted before the investigation.
  8. The correct answer is D (It avoids plasma related allergic reactions, infection risk and ABO matching while giving volume): When no clotting factor replacement is needed, five percent albumin is the usual fluid because it keeps oncotic pressure without the allergic and infectious risks of plasma and without ABO matching. It does not contain clotting factors, platelets or antibody inhibitors. Fibrinogen and other factors fall after each exchange, so sessions are spaced apart.
  9. The correct answer is B (Group O red cells, because donor lymphocyte anti-A cannot attack cells lacking A antigen): Donor lymphocytes make anti-A against group A cells, and the recipient already has anti-B, so only red cells with neither A nor B antigen are safe. Group O red cells meet that need and are used until the lymphocyte phase has passed. Group A cells would be attacked by donor anti-A and group B cells by recipient anti-B.
  10. The correct answer is D (Record it as a nonconformance, review the affected results, and correct the distribution process): Using a superseded procedure is a nonconformance even when controls pass, because the process was not followed as approved. The affected results are reviewed, and the root cause, a failure of distribution, is corrected. Blaming one person or changing dates does not fix the system.
  11. The correct answer is B (The positive screen, the expected 60 minute delay, and the options for uncrossmatched red cells): The obstetrician needs the finding, the realistic time frame and the available alternatives so that a clinical decision can be made about waiting or using uncrossmatched blood. Withholding the estimate or the finding limits those choices. Promising units within the hour would be unsupported before the workup is finished.
  12. The correct answer is A (1400 on Tuesday, because 24 hours after identification is the same time one day later): The stated limit runs from the time the reaction was identified, so 24 hours from 1400 on Monday is 1400 on Tuesday. The clock does not wait for the investigation to finish. Shift change or the end of the calendar day are not the limits in the policy described.
  13. The correct answer is A (Excess antigen can mask weak antibody reactions and give false negative or weaker results): A heavy suspension carries too much antigen for the amount of antibody in the tube, so antibody is spread thinly and weak agglutination can be missed. This is the antigen excess problem and it can produce false negative results. Density alone does not cause hemolysis or mixed field reactions.
  14. The correct answer is B (Drug dependent antibody forms immune complexes with the drug that activate complement on red cells): In the immune complex mechanism a drug dependent antibody binds the drug and the complex activates complement on red cells, giving intravascular hemolysis and a DAT positive for C3d only. A warm IgG pattern would give an IgG positive DAT and slower splenic removal. A cold IgM mechanism does not fit a drug started days earlier.
  15. The correct answer is B (Poor response to random units but good response to HLA-selected units supports HLA antibody-mediated refractoriness): Transfusions 1, 2 and 4 gave one-hour increments of 2 x10^9/L each, a corrected count increment near 1, while the HLA-selected unit raised the count by 28 x10^9/L, a corrected count increment of about 16. A good response to HLA-selected platelets in an afebrile patient with a normal spleen points to HLA antibodies destroying random donor platelets. The later fall at 24 hours is normal consumption, and Rh D is not carried on platelets.
  16. The correct answer is A (Speak up respectfully and ask for the check, because patient safety outranks hierarchy): A culture of safety expects any team member to raise a concern at the time, in a respectful way, so the sample can be checked before testing. Delaying feedback lets a possible misidentification go forward. Silent correction or private notes do not fix the process or inform the team.
  17. The correct answer is B (Anti-Kpa, because the Kpa antigen occurs in only about two percent of donors): Kpa is a low-prevalence antigen found in roughly two percent of donors, so screening cells seldom carry it and anti-Kpa is usually found by crossmatch. The k (Cellano) and Vel antigens are high-prevalence, found on nearly all red cells, while Jka is present on about three quarters of donors. Antibodies to high-prevalence antigens give reactions with almost every cell.
  18. The correct answer is B (Do not return it to inventory, and handle it as a discard under policy): The unit was out of controlled storage for longer than the 30 minutes the policy permits, so it cannot be returned to inventory or issued to another patient. Close to the limit is not within the limit, and recooling does not reset the time out of storage. The unit is handled according to the discard procedure.
  19. The correct answer is D (Quarantine the units, document the deviation, and notify the supervisor, who involves the medical director): A temperature indicator showing the limit was exceeded means the units are no longer proven safe, so they are quarantined and the event is documented and escalated. Decisions about use belong to the medical director. A sealed bag does not protect against warming, and cooling again does not restore proof of quality.
  20. The correct answer is D (Group A or AB plasma, because it lacks antibodies against the recipient's remaining A cells): The recipient still has some group A red cells, so plasma must not contain anti-A, which means group A or AB. Group O and group B plasma both contain anti-A. Plasma compatibility remains important until all recipient red cells have been replaced.
  21. The correct answer is A (Dosage-showing antibodies react more strongly with double-dose antigen, so weak antibodies are detected): Many antibodies in the Rh, Duffy, Kidd and MNS systems show dosage, meaning they react more strongly with red cells carrying a double dose of the antigen. Screening cells with homozygous expression therefore detect weak antibodies that heterozygous cells could miss. Homozygosity does not change antigen number overall and does not make low-prevalence antibodies detectable.
  22. The correct answer is B (Wear protection, contain the bag and request a new sample, because the specimen is compromised): A cracked, leaking specimen is an exposure risk and an unreliable sample, so the technologist protects herself, contains the bag and asks for a recollection. Decanting blood by hand adds exposure and breaks the specimen identification chain. Silently discarding it delays the patient and hides the problem, and hepatitis C is transmitted by blood contact, so the risk is not small.
  23.
