The correct answer is B (The drug induces production of true red cell autoantibodies that resemble warm autoimmune antibodies): Methyldopa can induce production of true red cell autoantibodies that look like warm autoimmune antibodies, with a positive DAT and serum and eluate that react with all cells. Many patients have no anemia. Hapten, immune complex and non-specific protein mechanisms give different serologic patterns.
  24. The correct answer is C (Add saline and re-read, because stacked cells disperse while true agglutinates stay together): Rouleaux are stacked cells caused by elevated protein, and a saline replacement technique disperses them while true agglutination remains. Antiglobulin reagent and heating do not separate the two reliably. This distinction prevents a false incompatible crossmatch from delaying issue.
  25. The correct answer is D (Red cells at 1 to 6 C and platelets at 20 to 24 C): Red cells are stored in a refrigerator at 1 to 6 C to slow metabolism and bacterial growth, while platelets must be kept at 20 to 24 C with continuous agitation to preserve function. Storing platelets in the cold causes them to lose viability and clear rapidly after transfusion. Reversing or mixing the ranges would spoil the products.
Case CHB11

A 68-year-old woman needs red cells for anemia. A tube-method antibody screen with polyspecific AHG was negative at immediate spin and 37 C. In the AHG phase, the technologist left about five drops of saline in each tube after the last wash, then added AHG. All three screening cell tubes were negative, and check cells added to every tube gave no agglutination. The test was repeated after thorough washing and full decanting: only screening cell II gave 2+ at AHG.

  1. The correct answer is D (Incomplete washing left serum globulin that neutralized the AHG so check cells could not agglutinate): AHG is neutralized when unbound serum globulin remains in the tube because washing or decanting was incomplete. The check cells then find no free AHG to link them, so they stay unagglutinated. Check cell reactivity in negative tubes is the test of washing and reagent addition, and the antigen strength of the screening cells does not affect it.
  2. The correct answer is A (Reject them as invalid and repeat the antiglobulin phase with thorough washing and complete decanting): Check cells that fail to react show that the negative antiglobulin results are not interpretable. The results must be treated as invalid and the test repeated with thorough washing and full decanting. Reporting negative, relying on the autocontrol or rereading after a delay does not fix a neutralized reagent.
  3. The correct answer is A (The 2+ reaction already proved active AHG was present, so check cells add no information): Check cells are added only to tubes that are negative at the AHG phase. A reaction in a positive tube already shows that active AHG was added and was not neutralized, so check cells would add no information. They are added after reading and not at 37 C, and a 2+ result is a real positive result.
  4. The correct answer is B (Treat the Rh typing as invalid and investigate the cause before reporting a D type): A reacting control means the cells agglutinate without anti-D specificity, for example because they are antibody-coated or carry a cold-reactive autoantibody. The anti-D result cannot be believed, and subtracting reaction strengths has no valid basis. The technologist should investigate, for example by washing the cells and performing a DAT, and use an alternate method before reporting.
  5. The correct answer is A (Investigate the cause and document corrective action, even with a passing overall score): Any unacceptable response needs investigation and documented corrective action, because a failed identification could affect patients even when the total score passes. Asking for a regrade without evidence of a program error is not appropriate. Stopping all antibody work is excessive and not required by the program.
  6. The correct answer is B (Search issue records for each listed unit number to identify the recipients of its components): The supplier knows which components came from each donation, but only the hospital holds the issue records that tie a unit number to a patient. Searching those records is the first step toward notifying the responsible physician. Quarantining inventory of one ABO group or contacting the donor does not trace past recipients.
  7. The correct answer is A (Invalid, because the positive control suggests IgG-coated cells, so investigate with a DAT): A positive Rh control means the cells agglutinate without anti-D, which usually means they are already coated with IgG. The weak D result cannot be interpreted and a DAT should be performed. Check cells only confirm that antiglobulin reagent was active, and they do not validate a reactive control tube.
  8. The correct answer is D (Unit number, component type, recipient identifiers, issue date and time, and issuing technologist): Traceability from the hospital side depends on linking the unit identifier and component to the recipient, with the time and person responsible for issue. Clinical details help care but do not identify the unit, and donor health information is held by the blood supplier, not by the hospital transfusion service.
  9. The correct answer is C (Phone the transfusion medicine physician and follow the protocol, because guessing risks patient harm): The director is available and the protocol exists, so the right action is to consult while following the protocol. Group O plasma contains anti-A and anti-B and is not the universal plasma; group AB or a protocol-defined alternative applies. Delaying or relying on memory risks delay or harm.
  10. The correct answer is D (Rh reactivity persists, and the lost reactions are consistent with anti-Fya being present): Ficin and papain destroy Duffy, MNS and some other antigens but enhance Rh, Kidd and Lewis reactions, so loss of reactions on enzyme-treated cells fits anti-Fya and persistence of Rh reactivity fits an Rh antibody. The two antibodies are therefore separated rather than excluded. Enzyme sensitivity does not reduce the clinical significance of anti-Fya, which can cause hemolytic reactions.
  11. The correct answer is C (Red cells and platelets, because cellular components carry viable lymphocytes that can cause graft disease): Irradiation prevents donor lymphocytes from proliferating, so it applies to cellular components such as red cells and platelets. Frozen plasma contains no viable lymphocytes, so irradiating it adds nothing. The purpose is to prevent transfusion-associated graft versus host disease, not to reduce infectious risk.
  12. The correct answer is B (Prepare an eluate from the coated cells, because bound IgG may be an autoantibody or alloantibody): A positive DAT with a negative screen and no recent transfusion suggests IgG bound to the cells, either as an autoantibody or an alloantibody on circulating cells. An eluate removes the bound IgG and tests it against reagent cells to see whether it has specificity. A negative screen does not explain the positive DAT, and enzyme cells do not replace elution.
  13. The correct answer is A (Hold the related records beyond the scheduled date until the investigation is formally closed): An open lookback means the hospital must be able to trace those units to recipients, so routine destruction is suspended for the affected records until the investigation is closed. Copying names or keeping a summary loses unit and issue details needed to match donations to patients. Policy dates do not override an active public health investigation.
  14. The correct answer is A (Fewer febrile nonhemolytic reactions and less HLA alloimmunization in repeatedly transfused recipients): Removing most donor white cells before storage lowers cytokine build up and exposure to HLA antigens, so febrile nonhemolytic reactions and HLA alloimmunization become less frequent. It also lowers the risk of transmitting cell-associated CMV. It does not stop donor lymphocytes from causing graft-versus-host disease, which requires irradiation, and it does not change red cell expiry.
  15. The correct answer is C (Keep it in the dry shipper until transplant, because warming damages cryopreserved cells): Cryopreserved cord blood must stay at or below minus 150 C in vapour phase nitrogen or a validated dry shipper until the transplant team asks for it. A plasma freezer is far too warm, and refrigeration or early thawing shortens cell viability. Thawing happens only on the transplant program's instruction.
  16. The correct answer is C (Mixed field, because agglutinated and unagglutinated cells coexist from two red cell populations): Two red cell populations are present because group O cells from the transfusion lack the A antigen and stay free, while the patient's own group A cells agglutinate. This mixture of clumped and free cells is a mixed field reaction. It should be recorded as mixed field and not graded as a weak positive or called negative.
  17. The correct answer is D (No clinically significant antibody was detected, so an immediate spin crossmatch may proceed): A negative screen at every phase with working check cells means no clinically significant antibody was detected against antigens on the screening cells. Reactive check cells simply prove that the antiglobulin reagent works, and they do not make the screen positive. Rare-antigen antibodies are not detected by screening, which is why policy, not the screen, decides whether an immediate spin crossmatch is acceptable.
  18. The correct answer is D (Compare tube identifiers with the requisition, and confirm collection date, time and collector details): Acceptance depends on matching the identifiers on the tube against the request and confirming the collection details before testing starts. Testing cannot establish identity, visual quality does not replace identification, and a porter has no way of verifying the patient.
  19. The correct answer is A (1.5 hours remain, so the units are within validated time and acceptable): From 08:15 to 14:45 is 6.5 hours, so 8 minus 6.5 leaves 1.5 hours of validated time, and the units arrived inside the limit. Treating elapsed time as remaining time or adding the two values reverses the logic. Rejecting the units would discard platelets that met the validated shipping condition.
  20. The correct answer is A (Move the plasma to a working freezer, quarantine it and record the excursion for review): The power failure is the cause, so the units must be protected immediately by moving them to a working freezer at the required temperature and holding them in quarantine. The excursion record and chart trace go to the supervisor or medical director, who decides whether the plasma is still suitable. Discarding, waiting, or thawing the units without that review is premature.
  21. The correct answer is D (No, a new sample is needed because the Wednesday specimen is past day three): Wednesday is day 0, so the following Tuesday is day 6, which is past the end of day 3. Pregnancy within three months places her under the short limit, so a fresh specimen is required. A prenatal screen does not stay valid for the whole pregnancy, and the policy does not demand a same day draw.
  22. The correct answer is B (Run positive and negative control cells today, because reactivity must be confirmed daily): Blood grouping antisera are checked with known antigen-positive and antigen-negative red cells on each day of use, which confirms reactivity and specificity before patient results are reported. An incoming check from last week does not show that the vial still reacts today. A negative cell alone cannot show that the reagent can still agglutinate its antigen.
  23. The correct answer is A (Verify the specimen label and patient identity against the requisition and the earlier specimen): A new sample can resolve a discrepancy only if it comes from the correct patient, so identity and labelling must be verified first. Drawing from the opposite arm is the accepted practice and does not invalidate testing. Skipping checks could repeat a wrong blood in tube error.
  24. The correct answer is A (Lewis antibodies are mostly IgM, and neonatal red cells carry very little Lewis antigen): Lewis antibodies are mostly IgM and do not cross the placenta. Newborn red cells carry little Lewis antigen because it develops after birth, so even transferred antibody would find few targets. Lewis hemolytic disease is therefore rarely seen.
  25. The correct answer is D (Issue the pools, since ABO matching is not required for adult cryoprecipitate, and record the group): Cryoprecipitate has a small plasma volume, so ABO compatibility is usually not required for adults, although identical units are preferred when available. Delaying fibrinogen replacement in a bleeding patient causes more harm than the minor risk. Recording the group keeps the issue traceable.
  26. The correct answer is C (Adjust the bath and confirm a thermometer reading of 55 to 57 C before use): A bath reading below the acceptable range means the temperature needed to release antibody from the red cells has not been reached, so elution results would be unreliable. The bath must be adjusted and verified in range before use. Extending the time does not make up for the lower temperature, and a 37 C block cannot perform a 56 C elution.
  27. The correct answer is B (Dilution can weaken antibody reactions, so a clinically significant antibody could be missed): Infusion fluid dilutes the patient's plasma, so antibody concentration falls and weak antibodies may give negative or weaker reactions. Fluids do not change ABO antigens or raise antibody binding. The error is dangerous because a missed antibody could lead to an incompatible transfusion.
  28. The correct answer is A (Anti-E, because every E positive cell reacts and every E negative cell is nonreactive): Only the three E positive cells react, and all five E negative cells are nonreactive, so anti-E fits and meets the three and three pattern. Anti-c is excluded because cells 4, 5 and 7 carry c and do not react, and anti-D is excluded because cell 6 is D negative yet reactive. Anti-Jkb fails because cell 5 carries Jkb and is nonreactive.
  29. The correct answer is C (At 14:30 or later, because the policy requires ten full minutes of contact): Disinfection depends on the disinfectant staying wet on the surface for the full stated contact time, which here ends at 14:30. Wiping earlier removes the chemical before it has inactivated organisms. Doubling the time is not required by the policy, and paper towels only absorb the blood, they do not disinfect it.
  30. The correct answer is D (Two vials, because 40 mL divided by 30 rounds to 1 and one vial is added): The bleed is 0.8 times 50, which equals 40 mL. Dividing by 30 mL per vial gives 1.33, which rounds down to 1 under the stated policy, and one extra vial is added for a total of 2. Stopping at one vial omits the added safety vial, and rounding up or skipping the division gives the wrong total.

Practice Test 13 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Quarantine them and investigate, because the transport temperature limit was exceeded): A reading above the required transport range means the unit's storage conditions are not proven, so the units are quarantined until the laboratory investigates and a decision is made under its policy. Accepting them ignores the nonconformance. Cooling them again does not repair a documented excursion, and automatic discard skips the assessment the quality system requires.
  2. The correct answer is B (Anti-Fya plus anti-Jka, because enzyme treatment removed one reactivity and enhanced the other): Loss of reactivity with Fy(a+) cells after ficin shows an antibody to a destroyed antigen, and stronger reactions with Jk(a+) cells show an antibody to an enhanced antigen. Together with two cells lacking both antigens that stay nonreactive, this fits a mixture of anti-Fya and anti-Jka. An autoantibody would react with all cells.
  3. The correct answer is D (Quarantine the units pending medical director review, because the storage limit was exceeded): Frozen plasma must stay at or below minus 18 C, so a five-hour excursion to minus 14 C is a deviation from storage requirements even though the units look solid. The units are held aside until the medical director reviews the data. Relabelling or refreezing invents a status the units do not have, and a simple return to temperature does not erase the deviation.
  4. The correct answer is D (The infant is weak D positive, so the mother needs a fetal bleed screen for RhIG): A valid weak D reaction at the antiglobulin phase with a negative control means the infant is D positive. The mother is therefore at risk of sensitization and needs a fetal bleed screen to determine the RhIG dose. A spin-only result would miss this, and the reaction was in the infant's cells, not the mother's.
  5. The correct answer is C (Fourteen units, because the needed rise of 0.7 g/L divided by 0.05 g/L is 14): The needed rise is 1.5 minus 0.8, or 0.7 g/L. Dividing 0.7 by 0.05 g/L per unit gives 14 units. Using the target alone gives 30 and using the starting value alone gives 16, and both ignore that only the shortfall must be replaced. A fixed pool of ten is only a common starting dose.
  6. The correct answer is D (It prevents sensitization, and a negative screen confirms she is not yet sensitized): Antenatal Rh immune globulin is given to Rh negative pregnant women who are not sensitized to prevent anti-D formation from small fetomaternal hemorrhages. A negative screen shows she has not yet formed anti-D, so prophylaxis is still useful. If anti-D were already present, the globulin would not help and a titre would be followed instead.
  7. The correct answer is A (Log the change in the revision history, issue a new version, obtain approval, replace copies): A change to a working instruction is a document revision: it is recorded, given a new version number, approved and distributed so that every controlled copy matches. Hand corrections, file-only updates or verbal notices leave different versions in use.
  8. The correct answer is C (Repeat with a fresh 0.8 percent suspension, because excess cells distort the gel pattern): A thick suspension overloads the gel, so cells smear through it and mimic weak reactions. A result like this is unreliable and should not be graded or reported. Repeating the test with a correctly prepared 0.8 percent suspension restores the intended balance, while adding saline in the card or reading only part of the gel does not correct the error.
  9. The correct answer is C (IgG already on the patient cells reacts with antiglobulin reagent, so the results are false positive): Antiglobulin-phase typing relies on agglutination with anti-IgG, but cells coated with IgG in vivo agglutinate whatever reagent is added. The reactive reagent control confirms the false positive. Results are not valid until IgG is removed or another method is used.
  10. The correct answer is D (A titre of 32, because 1:32 is the last dilution giving a 1+ AHG reaction): The titre is read as the reciprocal of the highest dilution that still gives the defined endpoint, here a 1+ reaction at 1:32, so the titre is 32. The 1:64 tube is negative and cannot define the endpoint. Reporting 16 or 8 would stop at stronger reactions than the laboratory's definition allows.
  11. The correct answer is B (Group B or AB plasma, since neither contains anti-B that attacks recipient red cells): Plasma compatibility is the reverse of red cell compatibility. Group B and group AB plasma lack anti-B, so they will not coat the recipient's B antigen. Group A and group O plasma both contain anti-B and would react with the patient's cells.
  12. The correct answer is C (Notify the clinical team and obtain a new carefully collected sample to compare plasma colour): Pink plasma with fever, dark urine and a falling hemoglobin may be intravascular hemolysis, so the team is told at once and a clean specimen is drawn for comparison. Treating it as a collection artefact risks missing a transfusion reaction. Issuing more red cells without investigation adds risk.
  13. The correct answer is A (A cold autoantibody, likely insignificant, because it is negative at 37 C and AHG): Reactivity at immediate spin and room temperature that disappears at 37 C and the antiglobulin phase is typical of a cold reacting IgM autoantibody, as seen after mycoplasma infection. Such antibodies are usually not clinically significant. A warm autoantibody or alloantibodies would react at 37 C or AHG, and a delayed hemolytic reaction needs prior transfusion, which this patient did not have.
  14. The correct answer is D (Calibration of spin time and speed, because the optimum varies with each centrifuge): Calibration finds the spin time and speed that give the tightest cell button with easy resuspension for that particular centrifuge. The result differs between instruments, so the manual's default may not suit it. Reagent reactivity, staff proficiency and cleaning are separate quality activities.
  15. The correct answer is A (Report the status as unresolved and document a search of ward and patient chart records): With no ward record and only a recollection, the status cannot be proven either way, so it is reported as unresolved while a documented search continues. The nurse's memory is not a record, and an issue without a return does not prove administration. Reporting a guess could mislead the supplier's investigation.
  16. The correct answer is C (Heparin, because it can inhibit the polymerase reaction used in DNA-based HLA typing): Heparin carries over into DNA extracts and inhibits the polymerase used for amplification, so most laboratories ask for EDTA or ACD whole blood instead. EDTA and ACD are both accepted anticoagulants for molecular HLA testing. The other anticoagulants named do not carry this inhibition problem.
  17. The correct answer is B (Pause and contact the physician or supervisor, because the documented refusal needs clarifying): A signed refusal conflicts with a transfusion order, and the technologist must not guess which governs. Contacting the physician or supervisor allows consent to be clarified before any unit is prepared or issued. Cancelling alone or preparing units in secret both bypass the proper decision makers.
  18. The correct answer is A (High extracellular potassium in older irradiated cells, because this could harm a small infant): Stored red cells leak potassium, and irradiation increases the leak, so large volume transfusion to a neonate can cause hyperkalemia. Fresher units, or washing the cells before use, reduce this risk. Red cell antigen expression and lymphocyte inactivation are not the concern with unit age.
  19. The correct answer is A (An A2 subgroup with anti-A1, because A2 cells lack the A1 antigen on reagent cells): A minority of group A people are A2, and some of them make anti-A1, which agglutinates reagent A1 cells in the reverse group while the B cells still react. The negative autocontrol rules out a cold autoantibody, and the absence of anti-B reaction in the forward group rules out group AB. The forward reaction is strong, so a disease-weakened antigen is not the best fit.
  20. The correct answer is B (Proceed with emergency issue per policy and document the urgency, because no known refusal exists): The emergency exception covers an unconscious patient whose life is at risk when no refusal is known. Waiting for a guardian or lawyer would add delay that the exception exists to prevent. The laboratory documents the urgency and the policy it relied on.
  21. The correct answer is B (Group O red cells, because the recipient's anti-A would destroy group A red cells): In a major mismatch the recipient's plasma still holds anti-A, and anti-B for a group O patient, so group O red cells are the only fully compatible group until the recipient antibodies disappear. Group A, B and AB cells all carry antigens those antibodies can attack.
Case CHB12

A 19-year-old patient with sickle cell disease is admitted with acute chest syndrome and a hemoglobin of 68 g/L. Her record lists anti-E and anti-K from an earlier transfusion. Her extended phenotype is C+ c0 E0 e+ K0, and the physician orders two red cell units for transfusion this evening.

  1. The correct answer is C (Units matched for C, E and K, and negative for the antigens of her known antibodies): Patients with sickle cell disease form antibodies frequently, mainly to Rh and Kell antigens, so prophylactic matching is recommended. Units must also be negative for the antigens of her existing anti-E and anti-K. Matching only ABO and D leaves her exposed to new antibodies.
  2. The correct answer is C (Type the unit for E and K with antisera, using positive and negative control cells): A unit cannot be assumed antigen-negative without typing. Testing with antisera and controls confirms E and K negative status for this patient's known antibodies. A hemoglobin S result or a titration says nothing about Rh or Kell antigens.
  3. The correct answer is D (A delayed hemolytic reaction from antibody rebound, so identify the antibody and notify the physician): Hemoglobin falling a week after transfusion with a new antibody and a positive IgG DAT points to a delayed hemolytic reaction. The antibody is identified and future units are selected antigen-negative. Acute ABO reactions occur within minutes to hours, not days.
  4. The correct answer is C (Quarantine the units and start an excursion review, because readings exceeded the 6 C limit): The later readings rise above 6 C, so the units must be held and the time and temperature exposure evaluated under policy. The average of the readings is about 5.3 C and hides the excursion, so it cannot be used to justify issue. Automatic discard is also wrong because the review decides the disposition of each unit.
  5. The correct answer is B (Complement components coat the cells, while IgG is not detectable on the red cell surface): The anti-C3d reaction is positive while anti-IgG is negative, so complement alone is on the cells. The polyspecific reagent contains both anti-IgG and anti-C3d, so its 2+ reaction comes from the complement component. The saline control is negative, so the result is not caused by spontaneous agglutination.
  6. The correct answer is C (Wash the cord cells repeatedly with saline to remove Wharton jelly and repeat the typing): Wharton jelly from the umbilical cord coats cord cells and makes them clump with every reagent, including saline. Washing the cells several times with saline removes it, and the typing is then repeated. Reporting group AB or testing maternal serum does not fix the contamination, and reagent volume is not the cause.
  7. The correct answer is A (Not excluded; culture the bag and inform the supplier, because stains miss low counts): A Gram stain needs high numbers of organisms to be positive, so a negative stain does not exclude a contaminated unit. Culture of the bag and notification of the supplier are needed to link the organism to the donation. A repeat stain is no more sensitive, and donor screening cannot rule out contamination that arises after collection.
  8. The correct answer is B (Make a fresh eluate and retest on time, because the delay exceeds the kit limit): Manufacturer instructions set the time window in which an eluate remains valid, and a validated procedure must be followed as written. A negative last wash shows washing was adequate but says nothing about eluate stability after a delay. Making a fresh eluate is the corrective action, whereas adding buffer or freezing are not validated steps.
  9. The correct answer is A (Record the excursion, tell the supervisor, and test the tubes with the deviation noted): The excursion must be documented and reported so the quality system can assess the risk. The retained tubes may still give useful information, so the best course is to test them and flag the storage deviation in the report. Discarding all samples or refusing the request throws away evidence, and warming the tubes does not repair any damage.
  10. The correct answer is C (Centrifuge and test it right away, because urgent work should not wait for batches): Urgent specimens are processed on receipt rather than waiting for the next scheduled batch, since the delay could prolong the wait for compatible blood. Reagent saving and batch convenience do not outweigh patient risk, and urgent work does not need written confirmation first.
  11. The correct answer is C (Maternal IgG crosses the placenta and can react with donor red cells given to the infant): Antibodies in the infant's plasma in the first months are mostly maternal IgG that crossed the placenta, so testing maternal plasma helps predict reactions with donor cells. A matching mother-infant group is not required. Direct maternal donation is not routine and does not explain the testing.
  12. The correct answer is B (Wharton jelly contamination, because it causes nonspecific clumping that washing the cells removes): Wharton jelly is a mucopolysaccharide that sticks red cells together and gives clumping in tests and controls alike. It disperses with repeated saline washing, which distinguishes it from antibody coating. Reagent problems would not produce a cloudy gelatinous wash.
  13. The correct answer is B (Investigate the ABO discrepancy before any issue, because a mislabelled sample is possible): A current group that differs from the historical record is an ABO discrepancy. It may reflect a wrong blood in tube, a record error or a transplant, so it must be investigated before issue. Overriding the block, deleting the old record or relying on a bedside check hides a possibly serious identification error.
  14. The correct answer is C (Test the stored pretransfusion sample for ABO and Rh, since current cells are mixed): Massive transfusion of group O cells dilutes the patient's own cells and produces a mixed field that no longer reflects the patient's genotype. The admission sample predates transfusion and shows the true group. Washing the sample in the tube cannot separate donor from recipient cells.
  15. The correct answer is D (Hold results and investigate, because consecutive points beyond 2 SD violate the 2 2s rejection rule): Two consecutive results beyond the same 2 SD limit break the 2 2s rule, which is a rejection rule indicating a systematic shift. Patient results from the affected run are held while reagent, equipment and technique are investigated. Repeating until a control passes or discarding the chart hides the shift, and a warning limit alone is not the rule that applies here.
  16. The correct answer is D (It shows whether stocking and ordering match real demand, so avoidable losses fall): Wastage is a measure of units lost to expiry, damage or storage failure, and it reflects how well inventory levels fit actual use. Reaction rates, crossmatch ratios and supplier delivery times are separate indicators with their own definitions. Monitoring wastage lets the service adjust par levels and rotation to cut avoidable cost and loss of a scarce donor resource.
  17. The correct answer is C (Units must stay in quarantine, because they could be issued before donor testing is complete): Until all required donor tests are complete and acceptable, units must be kept physically separate in quarantine so they cannot be labelled or issued by mistake. Sorting, freezing or room temperature storage address other needs and would either damage the unit or fail to prevent premature release.
  18. The correct answer is A (Classify discards by reason, because expiry from excess stock and alarm excursions need different fixes): Different causes of wastage call for different fixes, so sorting the discards by reason comes before any other step. Excess stock leads to outdating and calls for par level review, whereas unanswered alarms suggest handling and monitoring failures. Acting on one assumed cause before the data are sorted risks fixing the wrong problem.
  19. The correct answer is B (It limits run to run variation, so a two dilution rise reflects real change): Titration has a built-in variation of about one dilution from run to run, caused by pipetting, cell suspension and reading differences. Testing the old and new samples together removes much of that variation, so a rise of two or more dilutions can be accepted as a true change in antibody concentration. It does not identify the antibody or share a dilution series.
  20. The correct answer is D (Cancel the screen, noting the mother is D positive, because anti-D immunization cannot occur): A D positive mother has the D antigen and cannot form anti-D from D positive fetal cells, so no screen or immune globulin is indicated. Documenting the reason for cancellation keeps the record clear. The weak D and DAT steps do not change this, since her strong D result is already established.
  21. The correct answer is B (The authorized reviewers named in the document control procedure, such as the medical director): Revisions are approved by the people authorized in the document control procedure, often a medical director and quality manager, before the document is released. Experienced staff, users or suppliers can give input but do not provide the required approval.
  22. The correct answer is B (Evacuate everyone and call for help, because nitrogen displacing oxygen can cause sudden collapse): An oxygen alarm in a room with liquid nitrogen means the atmosphere may no longer support breathing, and collapse can come without warning. Everyone leaves at once and help is called, and rescuers do not enter without proper equipment. Sealing a venting dewar can cause overpressure, and fanning vapour or ignoring the alarm leaves people exposed.
  23. The correct answer is A (Mix serum, untreated cells and dissolved drug, and compare with a tube lacking the drug): For an immune complex type drug dependent antibody, the drug must be present in the test, so serum is mixed with untreated cells and dissolved drug and compared with a tube without drug. Reaction only in the drug tube confirms drug dependence. Washing drug coated cells suits the hapten type, and untreated cells alone cannot show a drug dependent antibody.
  24. The correct answer is B (The system blocks issue of an expired test unit and displays a warning message): A working expiry control must actively prevent issue of an expired unit and tell the user why. A system that allows issue and only records it, or only highlights the unit, does not prevent the error, and a block limited to same-day expiry leaves other expired units unprotected.
  25. The correct answer is C (Clerical check, visual check of post-reaction plasma for hemolysis, and repeat ABO and DAT): The first laboratory steps for any suspected reaction are a clerical check, a look at the plasma for hemolysis, and a repeat ABO and DAT on the post-reaction sample. They find the dangerous causes quickly. A full panel on stored units, only an antibody screen or tests outside the laboratory do not match the required order of work.
  26. The correct answer is B (Section 4, first aid measures, which lists steps for each route of exposure): Section 4 of a WHMIS 2015 safety data sheet gives first aid measures for eye, skin, inhalation and ingestion exposure. Handling and storage covers safe use and incompatible materials, and physical properties gives data such as pH and flash point. Disposal considerations deal with waste, not with treating an exposed person.
  27. The correct answer is C (Continue group O red cells, because anti-A persists and donor engraftment is incomplete): Persistent anti-A in the reverse typing shows that recipient antibody can still destroy group A cells, and the mixed field shows donor engraftment is not complete. Group O cells stay safe for both. The low reticulocyte count fits red cell aplasia, so the transplant physician should be told.
  28. The correct answer is D (Take it out of service for repair, because speed is over 7 percent under target): The deviation is 3,400 minus 3,150, divided by 3,400, which is about 7.4 percent and exceeds the 5 percent limit written in the procedure. A centrifuge outside tolerance gives poor sedimentation and unreliable reactions, so it must be removed from service until repaired and rechecked. Compensating with a longer spin or waiting to retest would let patient results depend on an unvalidated instrument.
  29. The correct answer is D (Read the order back, get confirmation, and record the caller's name with date and time): A verbal order is safe only when the details are read back and confirmed by the caller. Recording who called and when creates a traceable record. Repeating only the name, skipping the read back or leaving the form unsigned lets errors in product, dose or patient go unnoticed.
  30. The correct answer is A (An unauthorized access to personal health information, because the lookup had no care purpose): Under PHIPA and similar provincial laws, a custodian's agents may view records only for authorized purposes such as providing care. Looking up a neighbour with no care role is unauthorized access even if no harm followed, and sharing credentials adds a second breach. Antibody results linked to a person are personal health information.
  31. The correct answer is B (Place a calibrated thermometer in a well containing fluid and read it once stable): The thermometer must sit in the same kind of environment as the tubes, so it is placed in a well with a heat-conducting fluid such as water or oil and read when the value is stable. The setpoint only shows what was programmed, not what the wells reach, and surface or touch readings do not represent well temperature.
  32. The correct answer is A (They remove most white cells, reducing febrile reactions, HLA alloimmunization and cytomegalovirus transmission risk): Leukoreduction filters retain most donor white cells, which lowers febrile non-hemolytic reactions, HLA alloimmunization and transmission of cell-associated cytomegalovirus. They do not remove all lymphocytes, so irradiation is still needed to prevent graft-versus-host disease. They do not change red cell antigens or remove plasma proteins.
  33. The correct answer is C (Four vials, from a 90 mL bleed giving three vials plus one extra): The bleed is 1.8 x 50 = 90 mL of whole blood. Dividing by 30 gives 3.0 vials, and the policy adds one extra vial for a total of four. Reporting three omits the safety vial, and the other values come from using the wrong multiplier.
  34. The correct answer is B (Repeat the test, record both readings and the final interpretation, and issue after supervisor agreement): A disputed weak reaction may be an early sign of incompatibility, so it must be resolved by repeat testing and senior review before issue, and the disagreement and decision documented. Choosing the more favourable reading for speed, or averaging into a vague trace, hides the question. Time pressure in a stable patient does not justify skipping resolution.
  35. The correct answer is D (Recollect after an adequate line discard, because heparin prevents clotting and fibrin mimics agglutination): Heparin inhibits normal clotting, so fibrin can form late and look like agglutination or trap cells. Removing strands or waiting does not make the serum reliable. A new sample with the discard volume the policy requires is the safe solution.
  36. The correct answer is C (Contact the transfusion medicine physician or on-call supervisor and document authorization before any override): The block protects against delayed hemolysis, so only a person with authority and clinical context can override it, and that authorization must be recorded. Another person on call can be reached quickly in most settings. Deleting the flag or letting the nurse sign removes the safeguard rather than satisfying it.
  37. The correct answer is A (Perform an identification panel, because antiglobulin reactions suggest a clinically significant IgG alloantibody): Two of three screening cells react 2+ only at the antiglobulin phase while the autocontrol is nonreactive, which is the typical pattern of an IgG alloantibody. Identification must be completed so antigen-negative, antiglobulin-compatible red cells can be selected. Reaction strength does not decide significance, and a cold autoantibody would react at immediate spin instead.
  38. The correct answer is A (Perform an alloantibody workup and advise the physician to review the methyldopa, because it causes reactivity): A drug history and an alloantibody workup are needed because an autoantibody can hide an alloantibody in a patient who may be transfused. The physician decides about stopping methyldopa, which usually lets the reactivity fade over weeks. Refusing units or issuing untested group O units is not appropriate, and washing cells does not remove a bound autoantibody.
  39. The correct answer is D (Infants this age lack anti-A and anti-B, so reverse grouping cannot confirm the forward group): Anti-A and anti-B are not made in measurable amounts until about 3 to 6 months of age, so an infant's reverse group is usually negative and cannot confirm the forward group. The adult group O serum control reacted 4+ with both reagent cell sets, which shows the reagent cells are working. The mother's group does not decide the infant's group, and the Rh control was negative so the D result is valid.
  40. The correct answer is A (Rh positive, because donor red cells with weak D can immunize Rh negative recipients): Donor Rh typing must include a test for weak D, and a positive antiglobulin reaction with a negative control means D antigen is present. Weak D donor red cells can still immunize Rh negative recipients, so the unit is labelled Rh positive. A negative immediate spin only shows the antigen is weak, not absent.
  41. The correct answer is D (SBAR, because it presents problem, history, assessment and request in a predictable order): SBAR stands for situation, background, assessment and recommendation, which keeps a call brief and complete. A long chronological account buries the key point. A name alone leaves the physician without the problem, and a note left on a desk cannot support an urgent decision.
  42. The correct answer is B (Unit 2 only, because it lacks the Jka antigen and gave a compatible crossmatch): Unit 1 is Jka positive and reacts at the antiglobulin phase, and unit 3 is group B, so the patient's anti-B gives an immediate-spin reaction. Unit 4 is Jka positive, so its nonreactive crossmatch cannot be trusted, because a weak reaction with a heterozygous donor cell may be missed. Only unit 2 is antigen negative and compatible.
  43. The correct answer is D (Anti-D labelling counts D-positive fetal cells specifically, so maternal hemoglobin F cells are not scored): In a mother with raised hemoglobin F, her own F cells resist acid and are falsely counted as fetal on a Kleihauer-Betke slide. Flow cytometry with anti-D labelling identifies the D-positive fetal cells directly in a D-negative mother, so maternal F cells are not scored. It does not use acid elution or measure free hemoglobin.
  44. The correct answer is D (Hemolysis already present can hide hemolysis caused by an antibody, so a new sample is needed): Free hemoglobin colours the plasma and can hide or mimic hemolysis caused by a complement-binding antibody during testing. Reactions can be misread and a true hemolytic reaction may go unnoticed. A new sample is the safest approach, and the pink colour does not prevent agglutination or indicate lipemia.

Practice Test 14 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Keep all components in quarantine, because none may be released before the result is resolved): A reactive screening result means the unit cannot be released, so all components stay segregated in quarantine until the result is resolved and the unit is discarded or otherwise handled according to policy. Red cells, plasma and platelets from one donation carry the same infectious risk, and urgent need does not allow release.
  2. The correct answer is D (A warm IgG autoantibody directed against high-prevalence antigens on the patient's own red cells): A positive DAT, pan-reactivity including the autocontrol, and an eluate reacting with all cells indicate a warm autoantibody, most often IgG, directed at a high-prevalence antigen such as an Rh protein. Cold IgM antibodies react at immediate spin rather than the antiglobulin phase. Alloantibodies would not react with the patient's own red cells, and drug-dependent antibodies need the drug present.
  3. The correct answer is D (Sublimating carbon dioxide displaces air and can cause asphyxiation in poorly ventilated spaces): Dry ice is solid carbon dioxide that sublimates directly to gas, which builds up in small unventilated rooms, displaces oxygen and raises carbon dioxide levels. Headache and dizziness are early signs, so the room should be ventilated and the technologist should leave. Dry ice does not produce carbon monoxide, a liquid or radiation.
  4. The correct answer is B (A pH between 6.5 and 7.5 and a clear solution free of visible contamination): Saline for washing should be near neutral, with a pH of about 6.5 to 7.5, and clear and free of contamination. Acidic or alkaline saline can affect antibody binding and cell integrity, and contaminating protein or bacteria can neutralize antihuman globulin. Hypertonic or cloudy saline is unsuitable.
  5. The correct answer is C (Contact the ordering physician about the documented refusal and withhold plasma until it is resolved): The documented refusal must be reviewed with the physician before any plasma is prepared. Platelets and cryoprecipitate are suspended in or derived from plasma, so substituting them does not avoid the issue the patient raised. An order does not cancel a patient's recorded decision.
  6. The correct answer is D (Competency must be reassessed and documented periodically, so the file is incomplete without current records): Accreditation standards expect competence to be reassessed at defined intervals and documented, not assumed from tenure. An orientation checklist from years ago shows only that initial training occurred. Experience and laboratory proficiency testing support competence but do not replace the individual record.
  7. The correct answer is D (Within 72 hours of delivery, because early dosing blocks the maternal immune response to fetal D): Postpartum Rh immune globulin is given within 72 hours of delivery to suppress the immune response to fetal D-positive cells. A dose given later may still be offered if the window is missed, but protection is best within this time. Waiting for the six week visit allows sensitization to begin, and discharge timing is not the rule.
  8. The correct answer is C (Report it as below the critical level, since policy sets 16 as the urgent trigger): The policy defines the critical level, and a titre of 8 falls under it, so it is reported as below critical with the repeat schedule noted. It is still a real result that guides monitoring, so it must be reported and not withheld. A titre under the critical level does not make the antibody insignificant, it only means the urgent trigger was not met.
  9. The correct answer is D (Passive anti-D from the injection, because the sample was drawn after the dose): Rh immune globulin contains IgG anti-D, which can persist in the mother and react weakly at the antiglobulin phase. A sample drawn two days after the dose commonly shows this passive antibody. Recording the injection date at collection lets the laboratory avoid mistaking it for sensitization.
  10. The correct answer is B (Withdraw the rule out and test a Jk(a+b0) cell, because heterozygous cells can miss weak anti-Jka): Kidd antibodies show strong dosage, so a nonreactive heterozygous cell cannot rule out anti-Jka. Only a nonreactive cell homozygous for Jka, for example Jk(a+b0), can exclude it. After a recent transfusion with a falling hemoglobin, missing anti-Jka could allow a delayed hemolytic reaction to continue.
  11. The correct answer is D (Newborn antibody production is not yet developed, so reverse grouping is not expected to react): Infants make their own anti-A and anti-B only after several months, so the reverse group is not used in the early months of life and is not a true discrepancy. Maternal IgM does not cross the placenta in useful amounts. The neonate's cells are not changed by the mother's group.
  12. The correct answer is B (Tube 5, the purple cap EDTA tube, because it holds anticoagulated whole blood for DAT): EDTA keeps blood from clotting and preserves red cells for ABO and DAT testing, and it is the purple cap tube numbered 5 in the chart. Citrate is meant for coagulation tests and is diluted by its liquid additive. Heparin and fluoride tubes are not the tubes chosen for routine serology.
  13. The correct answer is B (It removes coating IgG so antigen sites on the cells can bind free autoantibody): Patient red cells already carry IgG, which occupies the antigen sites that would otherwise take up free autoantibody from the serum. ZZAP, a mixture of a reducing agent and a protease, strips that IgG so the cells can adsorb the serum autoantibody. It does not act on the serum, add complement or lyse the cells for the adsorption.
  14. The correct answer is C (Remove the airtight seal so carbon dioxide can vent, because trapped gas builds pressure): Dry ice sublimates into carbon dioxide gas, which expands and can burst an airtight container, so the packaging must let the gas escape. More dry ice produces more gas, and extra tape or bags make the sealed space worse. The package must also carry the required dry ice marking and net weight, which are separate steps.
  15. The correct answer is A (Reflect on practice, set goals and record learning, because regulators expect documented development): Continuing competence programs ask registrants to reflect, plan learning and document it, so the college can see that skills stay current. Holding an entry credential is only the start. Orientation covers the first year, and reacting to errors does not substitute for planned learning.
  16. The correct answer is D (About 3.4 mL, because the weekly limit is 6.4 mL and 3.0 mL was drawn): Total blood volume is 2.5 kg x 85 mL/kg = 212.5 mL, and 3 percent of that is about 6.4 mL per week. Subtracting the 3.0 mL already drawn leaves about 3.4 mL. The weekly limit is cumulative, so earlier draws must be counted.
  17. The correct answer is D (Dolichos biflorus lectin, which agglutinates A1 red cells and leaves A2 red cells unreacted): Dolichos biflorus lectin is diluted so that it agglutinates A1 red cells and not A2 red cells, which is how A subgroups are separated. Anti-A,B detects both A1 and A2 because both carry A antigen. The other reagents are used for different antigens and do not separate A1 from A2 in this way.
  18. The correct answer is B (Accept the leukoreduced unit as CMV-safe if local policy treats leukoreduction as equivalent): CMV-safe means leukoreduced or CMV-seronegative, and many Canadian programs accept leukoreduced components as equivalent. The flag applies to cellular components of any type. Leukoreduction does not destroy platelet function.
  19. The correct answer is D (Wear gloves and goggles, soak up the spill with pads, then dispose as directed): A small corrosive spill is contained and absorbed while wearing gloves and eye protection, and the used absorbent is discarded according to the laboratory chemical waste procedure. Ammonia mixed with bleach releases toxic chloramine gas, so it must not be added. Washing it into a drain or leaving it to dry spreads the hazard and leaves a slip risk.
  20. The correct answer is C (Transfusion-associated dyspnea, because respiratory distress occurs without meeting criteria for TRALI, TACO or allergic reaction): When respiratory distress follows transfusion but does not meet the definitions of TRALI or TACO and no allergic or other cause is found, it is classified as transfusion-associated dyspnea. Here oxygenation, chest imaging, blood pressure and BNP are all unremarkable, which argues against both lung injury and overload. Timing alone does not establish donor antibody injury or volume overload.
  21. The correct answer is D (Quarantine the plasma, move it to a working freezer, and open a deviation record): The log rises above minus 18 C from about hour 16, so the plasma has left its storage range and must be held while its exposure is evaluated. Moving it protects the remaining units and the deviation record starts the investigation of why the alarm failed. Relabelling or leaving the units in place ignores the excursion.
  22. The correct answer is D (K-positive and E-negative cells, because a reaction can then be attributed to anti-K alone): Cells that carry the suspected antigen K but lack E can react only with anti-K, because anti-E has no antigen to bind. A reaction therefore proves anti-K and no reaction rules it out, provided the cell shows adequate K expression. Cells positive for both antigens cannot separate the two, and cells lacking K cannot test for anti-K.
  23. The correct answer is B (Issue ABO compatible units once the system verifies both results, because electronic crossmatch criteria are met): Electronic crossmatch is allowed when a validated system confirms two ABO and Rh determinations, the antibody screen is negative and there is no history of significant antibodies. The computer then checks donor and recipient ABO compatibility. A serologic crossmatch, a cold screen or a third determination adds no safety for a patient who meets these criteria.
  24. The correct answer is B (An EDTA whole blood tube, because it preserves cells for DNA extraction later): Chimerism testing extracts DNA from nucleated cells, so an EDTA whole blood tube is the usual choice. Serum has almost no intact nucleated cells, and heparin inhibits the polymerase reaction. Grey-cap fluoride tubes are intended for glucose, not DNA work.
  25. The correct answer is C (Repeat the DAT with anti-IgG and anti-C3d separately, because each reagent identifies a different protein): Monospecific anti-IgG and anti-C3d tell the technologist which protein is bound, which narrows the cause of hemolysis. The polyspecific reagent contains both, so adding more of it cannot separate them. An eluate is done after the DAT type is known, and the laboratory cannot name a diagnosis from polyspecific testing alone.
  26. The correct answer is D (Repeat it in a verified block, because incubation below 36 C may miss IgG): The log shows the block fell below 36 C by 12:00, so the screen was incubated outside the acceptable range and a negative result cannot be trusted for IgG antibodies. The test must be repeated in a verified block after the cause is corrected. Earlier readings within limits do not validate a later incubation.
  27. The correct answer is B (Antigen excess weakened the first reaction, so report the result from the standardized suspension): A heavy suspension carries antigen in excess of the antibody available, which weakens visible agglutination. The standardized 2 to 5 percent suspension is the validated method, so its 3+ result is the one to report. Mixed field means two cell populations in the same tube, not different readings from different suspension strengths.
  28. The correct answer is C (Do not issue it, because it expired at 23:59, and remove it from inventory): A component is acceptable only until the expiry date and time on its label, and this unit passed that point ten minutes before the request. A computer record that still shows the unit in inventory does not change the label. Irradiation does not extend expiry beyond the original date, and urgency does not allow issue of an expired component.
  29. The correct answer is D (Unit 3, because the forward and reverse groupings do not agree with each other): Unit 3 shows no reaction with anti-A or anti-B, which indicates group O, yet its plasma fails to agglutinate A1 cells, which group O plasma should do. This forward and reverse disagreement is an ABO discrepancy and the unit is held until resolved. The other three units show matching forward and reverse patterns.
  30. The correct answer is D (Report her own omission, withdraw the result, and complete full testing before any issue): Omitting reverse grouping is a procedural nonconformance regardless of whether the result happens to match the history. Honest, prompt reporting lets quality staff assess the cause and ensures the result is recalled and completed. Hiding the omission or relying on the history defeats the purpose of the check.
  31. The correct answer is A (Only the Fya positive, S negative cell reacts, while the other cell stays nonreactive): Selected cells separate two candidate antibodies, with one cell carrying only the antigen for the first and the other carrying only the antigen for the second. Reaction with the Fya positive, S negative cell alone points to anti-Fya. Reaction with both cells would mean both antibodies, or another antibody, remain possible.
  32. The correct answer is A (Repeat the reverse grouping on the retained specimen and enter a clearly documented new result): When the original observation cannot be reconstructed, the only reliable fix is to repeat the test on the retained specimen and document it. Choosing an entry by order or averaging readings has no scientific basis. Releasing an unverified result with a note passes the risk to the physician.
  33. The correct answer is B (April 14, because 28 days reach March 31 and 14 more days follow): Counting the collection date as day 0, March 3 plus 28 days is March 31, and 14 more days give April 14. Counting the collection day as day 1 shortens the dating by a day, while using 30 days for March or starting from March 1 shifts the date in other directions. The expiry must be entered exactly as the policy defines it.
  34. The correct answer is B (Report the unit as unaccounted for and start a search, because its location is unknown): A unit whose location cannot be shown breaks the chain from donor to recipient, so it must be reported and investigated at once in case it was transfused, stored incorrectly or lost. Altering records, assuming expiry or replacing it quietly hides the gap and removes the traceability the records exist to give.
  35. The correct answer is A (Request a formal record merge, keeping transfusion history and antibody findings with the patient): A formal merge links the emergency transfusion and any antibody findings to the true patient, so later staff see the full history and an antibody is not missed. Leaving two records risks an unsafe future crossmatch based on incomplete history. Hand copying and discarding the original destroys the audit trail, and continuing to use a temporary number is unsafe.
  36. The correct answer is D (Enzymes destroy some clinically significant antigens, so antibodies to them could be missed): Ficin and papain destroy Fya, Fyb, M, N and some S antigens, so an antibody to one of them would not react with the treated panel and could go unnoticed. Untreated cells are needed to detect those antibodies. Enzyme methods are used in addition to, not instead of, standard testing.
  37. The correct answer is A (Contain the problem, investigate the root cause, apply corrective action, then verify that it worked): The standard sequence limits harm first, finds the root cause, applies a corrective action that addresses that cause, and then checks that the action prevented recurrence. Acting before the cause is known, or closing the record before verification, leaves the underlying problem in place.
  38. The correct answer is D (Fetal D-positive cells are present in her circulation, so a quantitative test is needed): Anti-D binds D-positive fetal cells in a D-negative mother, and after washing, indicator cells attach to the bound antibody and form rosettes. A positive screen is qualitative, so the bleed must then be quantified. Maternal anti-D in plasma is removed by washing, and weak D expression would make all maternal cells react, not a few.
  39. The correct answer is D (Respect the documented refusal and follow the policy, because capable adults may decline treatment): A capable adult has the right to refuse treatment, including blood, even when the refusal could be fatal. The laboratory follows the hospital's policy for patients who decline blood and clarifies orders with the surgeon. Family members cannot override a documented refusal from a capable patient.
  40. The correct answer is B (Hold the request and contact the ward to obtain the missing identifier before starting testing): Two identifiers are required so the request can be matched safely to the patient and sample. The laboratory should not fill in or assume identifiers itself, and testing should not begin on an incomplete request. Contacting the ward lets the missing identifier be supplied and documented without rewriting the whole order.
  41. The correct answer is A (Pink plasma suggests intravascular hemolysis and supports a reaction, so the physician is told): Free hemoglobin makes the post-reaction plasma pink against a straw-coloured pre-transfusion plasma, and a clean draw with prompt spinning makes artifact unlikely. This visual check is one of the first tests in a reaction workup. Lipemia looks milky, not pink, and the finding should be reported rather than set aside.
  42. The correct answer is B (Anti-Fya and anti-M, because ficin destroys the Duffy and M antigens on the membrane): Ficin and other proteolytic enzymes destroy Duffy (Fya and Fyb), M, N, S and s antigens, so antibodies to them stop reacting with treated cells. Rh, Kidd, Lewis and P1 antigens are enhanced or unchanged, so antibodies to them react the same or more strongly. The treated panel therefore helps separate Duffy and MNS antibodies from Rh and Kidd antibodies.
  43. The correct answer is D (A cold autoantibody, usually IgM, that is clinically insignificant without 37 C reactivity): Reactivity limited to immediate spin with a positive autocontrol points to a cold autoantibody, most often IgM anti-I. IgM antibodies that do not react at 37 C seldom shorten red cell survival, so they are usually clinically insignificant. Warm autoantibodies and IgG alloantibodies would react at the antiglobulin phase instead.
  44. The correct answer is D (Free hemoglobin can hide true antibody-mediated hemolysis, because the supernatant is already red): Free hemoglobin turns the supernatant red, so the red colour of a hemolytic antibody reaction cannot be told apart from the specimen colour. Hemolysis alone does not prove disease and does not damage reagent cells. Many laboratories therefore compare against a fresh sample before interpreting the reactions.
  45. The correct answer is A (The 4-1s rule, which signals a systematic shift, so investigate probe calibration): Four consecutive points on the same side of the mean and each beyond 1 SD break the 4-1s rule, which points to a systematic shift such as a drifting probe. The shift should be investigated before it moves outside the platelet limits. The 1-3s and R-4s rules need a single wide excursion, and the 10x rule needs ten points.
  46. The correct answer is D (Use a pink or purple EDTA tube, because EDTA prevents clotting and preserves red cells): EDTA chelates calcium, which prevents clotting and inhibits complement activation, so red cells and plasma stay suitable for grouping and screening. Heparin, citrate and fluoride tubes are intended for other tests and can dilute the sample or change reactions. Laboratory policy may also allow serum, but the EDTA tube is the one stated here.
  47. The correct answer is C (Antibodies to HLA class I antigens, which are present on platelets and clear transfused cells): Platelets express HLA class I antigens, and antibodies made after pregnancy or earlier transfusion destroy HLA-incompatible donor platelets within hours. HLA class II antigens are largely absent from resting platelets, and Rh D and Kell antigens are not carried on platelets in a way that causes refractoriness. This is why ABO-identical, fresh units can still fail when HLA antibodies are present.
  48. The correct answer is B (Screen invalid, repeat with fresh reagents, because the failed positive control cannot support a negative): The positive control contains known D positive cells and must form rosettes. None formed, so the reagents or procedure failed. A negative patient tube means nothing when the positive control fails, so the run is invalid and must be repeated before any result is released.
  49. The correct answer is B (Flag the historical anti-Jka and issue Jk(a-) red cells, because levels may fall undetectable): Kidd antibodies are well known for falling below the detection limit while the patient can still mount a rapid, hemolytic anamnestic response. The historical antibody must therefore stay on the record and antigen negative red cells be selected. A negative screen alone does not show the antibody is gone, and plasma products are not affected.
  50. The correct answer is C (Frequent blood draws can cause anemia, because a neonate's total blood volume is very small): A neonate's total blood volume is only a few hundred millilitres, so repeated or oversized draws can lead to iatrogenic anemia and a need for transfusion. Collecting the minimum volume protects the infant. Cost, clotting speed and antibody concentration are not the reason.
  51. The correct answer is D (At least 25 Gy to the central plane, with no part below 15 Gy): The standard dose for irradiating cellular blood components is at least 25 Gy to the central plane of the container, with no part receiving less than 15 Gy. This dose inactivates donor T lymphocytes so they cannot cause graft-versus-host disease. Lower doses may leave lymphocytes viable, and surface dose alone does not describe the dose inside the bag.
  52. The correct answer is D (Slow the return rate and give oral calcium, because citrate is lowering ionized calcium): Tingling and numbness around the mouth are typical signs of citrate toxicity from lowered ionized calcium. Slowing the return rate and giving oral calcium corrects it. A faster return worsens the citrate load, and fluids alone or a flat position do not address the cause.
  53. The correct answer is B (The sample is probably diluted by infusion fluid, so recollect from the other arm): A large unexplained fall in hemoglobin in a stable, non-bleeding patient, with a sample taken beside an infusion, suggests dilution by the fluid. Recollecting from the opposite arm settles the result. Clear plasma argues against hemolysis, and a fixed anticoagulant concentration does not explain such a drop.
  54. The correct answer is C (28 days from today, because the irradiation limit comes sooner than the original expiry): The policy gives the sooner of two dates: 28 days after irradiation or the original expiry. Twenty-eight days from today falls before the original expiry of 30 days, so it becomes the label date. Adding the periods together or halving them are not part of the standard, and the original expiry does not govern when it is later.
  55. The correct answer is A (That compatibility testing was not completed before issue, with the issuing technologist identified): Anyone handling the unit must see that it was released before compatibility testing was complete, and who issued it, so the clinical team and laboratory know retrospective testing is outstanding. A statement of universal compatibility is not a substitute. The final group is unknown at issue time, and donor personal details are not placed on a tag.
  56. The correct answer is D (Notify the physician now of the antibody and delay, then document any emergency release): The physician must know that a clinically significant antibody is documented and that antigen-negative units are not immediately available, so the clinical risk can be weighed. Any emergency release then proceeds under signed policy with documentation. Silent release, silent waiting or cancelling the order all leave the decision-maker uninformed.
  57. The correct answer is D (An alloantibody to a high-prevalence antigen, which the patient's own red cells lack): A serum that reacts at the same strength with every panel cell while the autocontrol is nonreactive points to an alloantibody to an antigen found on nearly all red cells, which the patient's own cells lack. Autoantibody would also react with the autocontrol, and a low-prevalence antibody would leave most cells nonreactive. Contaminated reagent cells would not explain a negative autocontrol.
  58. The correct answer is A (Gently dislodge the button by tilting the tube and watch for clumps against a light): Hard shaking breaks fragile agglutinates and can turn a weak true reaction into an apparent negative. Gently dislodging the button and tilting the tube lets the technologist see whether the cells lift off as clumps or as a smooth suspension. A light source behind the tube makes small clumps easier to see.
  59. The correct answer is C (Intravascular hemolysis may have occurred after transfusion started, so the reaction workup should continue): A clear pretransfusion sample followed by a pink post-reaction sample drawn with care points to hemolysis in the patient during the transfusion. The visual check of plasma colour is a standard first step in a reaction workup. Icterus makes plasma yellow or amber, not pink.
  60. The correct answer is B (Allow it once validated and documented, because it still meets the Canadian standard): A laboratory may adopt practices stricter than the Canadian standard, provided they do not conflict with it and are validated, documented and approved through document control. AABB standards do not replace the Canadian standard, and Health Canada does not approve each hospital procedure change.

Practice Test 15 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (A documented nonconformance report with root cause investigation, corrective action and effectiveness follow-up review): Accreditors expect a traceable written record showing what went wrong, why, what was changed and whether the change worked. Verbal notes or entries limited to one person's file do not show system corrective action. The program does not track internal corrections between surveys.
  2. The correct answer is A (Acceptance of fractions varies by individual, so the clinician must record the patient's own decision): Individual patients decide differently about plasma derived fractions, so no blanket rule applies. The clinician discusses the course and documents the personal decision before the laboratory issues anything. Assuming that all or none are accepted would override the patient's own course.
  3. The correct answer is A (A score of 43, from adding the values for all seven tubes): Each reaction is converted to its value and the values are added: 10 plus 10 plus 8 plus 8 plus 5 plus 2 plus 0 equals 43. A score gives more information than the titre alone because it reflects reaction strength across all tubes. Dropping tubes or changing a value, as in the other totals, gives a different number.
  4. The correct answer is C (Group O Rh negative red cells, because her childbearing potential makes anti-D formation a lasting risk): Group O red cells lack A and B antigens, so they are compatible with every ABO plasma. A woman of childbearing potential should receive Rh negative red cells whenever possible, because anti-D could cause hemolytic disease of the fetus and newborn in later pregnancies. Waiting for the group delays treatment while she is hemorrhaging, and AB red cells carry A and B antigens that could meet her plasma antibodies.
  5. The correct answer is C (Section 8, exposure controls and personal protection, which lists recommended gloves, eyewear and ventilation): Section 8 of the safety data sheet covers exposure controls and personal protection, including glove type, eye protection, ventilation and exposure limits. First aid measures describe what to do after an exposure, and transport information addresses shipping. Toxicological information describes health effects, not the protective equipment to wear.
  6. The correct answer is B (Repeat the two failed tubes after refilling saline, because their negative results are invalid): Failed check cells make a negative antiglobulin tube invalid, so the two failed tubes are repeated after the reservoir is refilled and the washer checked. The six tubes with reacting check cells remain valid. Reporting the failed tubes as negative or adding more check cells does not correct the inadequate wash.
  7. The correct answer is A (Citrate binds ionized calcium, so slowing the procedure and giving calcium is appropriate): Citrate in the anticoagulant and in plasma binds ionized calcium, and low calcium causes tingling around the mouth and fingers plus cramps. The usual response is to slow the inlet rate and give calcium as ordered. Allergic, hemolytic and volume problems look different, with hives, red urine or breathlessness.
  8. The correct answer is B (The product affected, quantity available, expected duration, and how to request urgent needs): A shortage notice works when it states what is affected, how much is available, how long the problem is expected to last and who to call for urgent needs. Clinicians then set their own priorities. A vague notice, a patient list or a technologist's ranking of patients does not give wards what they need.
  9. The correct answer is B (Complete the training and assessment, because experience cannot replace validation on new equipment): New equipment has its own procedure, limits and failure modes, so every user needs training and documented competency whatever their seniority. Principles of agglutination are shared, but gel handling, interpretation and troubleshooting are not. Exemptions based on seniority weaken the quality system.
  10. The correct answer is C (Check records for clerical error, inspect post-reaction plasma for hemolysis, then repeat ABO and DAT): The initial laboratory response is a clerical check, a visual check of post-reaction plasma for hemolysis, and a repeat ABO group and direct antiglobulin test, because these detect the most dangerous causes quickly. An eluate is reserved for a positive DAT and comes later. Discarding the bag or waiting for cultures delays recognition of a serious reaction.
  11. The correct answer is A (Negative screen with no antibody history, because ABO errors are the remaining risk): When the screen is negative and there is no history of significant antibodies, the main remaining incompatibility is ABO, which an immediate spin or electronic check detects. A negative DAT does not exclude plasma antibodies. Age and group O selection do not remove the need to detect clinically significant antibodies.
  12. The correct answer is C (Fewer than 5 x10^6 leukocytes per unit, which lowers febrile reactions and HLA alloimmunization risk): The Canadian specification for a leukoreduced red cell component is fewer than 5 x10^6 residual leukocytes per unit. This reduction lowers febrile nonhemolytic reactions, HLA alloimmunization and cytomegalovirus transmission. A count near 5 x10^9 describes an unmodified unit, and leukoreduction does not replace irradiation for preventing graft versus host disease.
  13. The correct answer is D (Filtration leaves some viable lymphocytes, because irradiation is needed to stop them from dividing): Leukoreduction lowers the number of white cells but does not remove them all, so viable lymphocytes remain and can engraft in a susceptible patient. Irradiation inactivates their ability to divide, which is why it remains required. Timing of filtration and washing do not reliably prevent graft-versus-host disease.
  14. The correct answer is B (A structured verbal report with written notes, because it limits lost details): A structured verbal report plus written notes gives the incoming technologist the stage of each investigation, reserved units and timing. A brief summary omits detail that is not stored in the information system. Reconstructing events from a bench invites assumptions, and unfinished work must be transferred, not left with the previous person.
  15. The correct answer is C (Discard the set and use another, because cloudiness suggests contamination or a defect): A collection bag must be inspected for expiry, leaks, tubing damage and abnormal appearance of the anticoagulant, because cloudiness or particles point to contamination or a manufacturing fault. A set with an abnormal solution is discarded and reported according to the supplier and quality procedure. Mixing, filtering or using it anyway would put a donor and a recipient at risk.
  16. The correct answer is B (It lowers alloimmunization and febrile reactions in a patient who receives repeated transfusions for life): Lifelong transfusion exposes the patient to many donor antigens, and phenotype matching prevents antibody formation. Leukoreduction lowers febrile reactions and HLA sensitization. Neither measure changes the iron in the red cells, and iron overload is managed with chelation.
  17. The correct answer is A (Ask for acceptable photo identification, because positive donor identification must be confirmed at every visit): Donor identity must be positively confirmed against acceptable identification at each visit, because donor records, screening results and the unit itself are linked to that identity. Familiarity, a signature or a health card alone does not meet a policy that requires photo identification. Without confirmed identity, results from the donation could be attached to the wrong person.
  18. The correct answer is C (Hold the sample, request a new identified sample, and investigate wrong blood in tube): A current type that conflicts with a reliable history and has no medical explanation suggests the blood in the tube came from someone else. Retesting the same tube repeats the same error, so a fresh sample collected with proper identification is required before any type is accepted.
  19. The correct answer is D (Group AB plasma, because it holds neither anti-A nor anti-B to attack recipient red cells): Plasma antibodies are what matter in plasma transfusion. Group AB plasma has no anti-A or anti-B, so it is safe for any recipient. Group O plasma holds both antibodies, and group A or B plasma holds one, each of which could react with an unknown recipient.
  20. The correct answer is D (It lets a unit be traced from donor to recipient and back during a recall): Linking the donation number to the recipient and the issuing staff member makes it possible to identify every recipient of a suspect donation and the donor of any implicated unit. This traceability supports reaction investigations, look back and recalls. Billing, consent and supplier performance are separate processes with their own records.
  21. The correct answer is C (Perform a saline replacement technique, then repeat the reverse grouping to confirm resolution): Saline replacement removes the excess protein that causes cells to stack and leaves true antibody-mediated agglutination intact. Adding more serum increases the protein and the stacking. Heat does not reliably dissolve rouleaux, and reverse grouping cannot be skipped in an adult.
  22.
