The correct answer is D (K is highly immunogenic, and anti-K can cause severe fetal anemia by suppressing red cell production): After D, K is one of the most immunogenic red cell antigens, and anti-K is an IgG that crosses the placenta. It can cause severe fetal anemia by suppressing erythropoiesis, so avoiding sensitization is important. K negative blood is actually plentiful, and anti-K is not a typical naturally occurring IgM.
  23. The correct answer is D (Perform a DAT and alert the physician, because delayed hemolysis is a possibility): A negative screen can occur in a delayed hemolytic reaction because the antibody level may be below detection before the anamnestic response. Pink plasma, falling hemoglobin and dark urine after recent transfusion suggest hemolysis, so a direct antiglobulin test and physician notification come first. Issuing more units or blaming collection delays recognition of a dangerous reaction.
  24. The correct answer is C (It reduces the zeta potential, allowing cells to approach closer so agglutination occurs): Albumin lowers the zeta potential, the repelling charge cloud around red cells, so cells can come close enough for IgG to bridge them. It does not digest cell proteins, which is the action of enzymes, or lower ionic strength, which is the role of LISS.
  25. The correct answer is C (Anti-I, because adult I positive cells react far more strongly than cord cells): Anti-I reacts most strongly with adult cells, which carry abundant I antigen, and weakly with cord cells, which carry mostly i antigen. The autologous cells react because the patient's own cells are I positive, and the reactions disappear at 37 C as expected for a cold autoantibody. Anti-i would show the opposite strength pattern.
  26. The correct answer is A (Five units reached patients, so five separate recipients must be traced by the hospital): Five units reached patients: four were transfused directly, and the fifth was restocked after return and later transfused. The discarded unit was never given. Each of the five recipients must be traced, and a restocked unit still counts once it is transfused.
  27. The correct answer is D (Peak temperature, time outside 1 to 6 C, units affected, actions taken and staff involved): Disposition depends on how high the temperature rose, how long units were outside range and which units were exposed, so these facts must be written down along with the actions taken. An alarm time alone is not enough to judge the units. Service history and supplier names do not describe the excursion itself.
  28. The correct answer is B (Sealed safety cups opened inside a biological safety cabinet, because aerosols are then contained): Aerosols from a tube that breaks during centrifugation stay suspended for minutes, so sealed cups should be opened inside a biological safety cabinet where the air is filtered and contained. A surgical mask and a face shield guard the face from splashes but neither contains the aerosol nor protects other people in the room. Extra gloves only protect the hands.
  29. The correct answer is C (Check the spin timer against a stopwatch, because time can drift while speed stays correct): Speed and time are separate settings, so a good tachometer reading does not prove that the spin time is right. A timer that runs short leaves cells under-packed and can weaken or lose reactions. Reagent quality control already passed, and changing temperature or lengthening spins without verification would only hide the fault.
  30. The correct answer is C (Remove the thawer from service, quarantine the remaining units, recall the issued unit, and investigate): The independent thermometer shows the water is well above the validated limit, so the display cannot be trusted and plasma thawed since the fault began may have been overheated and lost factor activity. The device must come out of service, remaining units must be held, the clinical area must be told about the issued unit, and the fault investigated before use resumes. Continuing use or resetting the display hides the problem.
  31. The correct answer is D (Group B or O red cells, because reverse grouping shows anti-A in the patient plasma): The patient red cells react with anti-B and anti-D, and the plasma agglutinates A1 cells, so the patient is group B Rh-positive with anti-A. Group B and group O red cells lack the A antigen. Group A and group AB cells would be attacked by the patient's anti-A.
  32. The correct answer is C (Collect it in a labelled chemical waste container for hazardous waste pickup): Sodium azide can react with copper or lead plumbing to form shock sensitive metal azides, so policy routes it to the chemical waste program. Dilution and neutralizing it with bleach do not make sink disposal acceptable, and bleach adds its own hazards. The biohazard bin is meant for infectious material, not for chemical reagent waste.
  33. The correct answer is A (The trauma number matching the wristband and request, with date, time and collector initials): Unidentified patients receive a unique trauma number that appears on the wristband, request and tube so every item matches. A guessed name or a bed number is not a unique, stable identifier, and an unlabelled tube cannot be accepted for testing. The date, time and collector details complete the traceable record.
  34. The correct answer is A (Hold the unit, tell the supervisor and physician, and repeat testing with antibody investigation): A missed positive screen means the patient may have a clinically significant antibody, so the next unit is held until investigation is done, with the supervisor and physician informed because earlier transfusions may also need review. Issuing on the strength of earlier uneventful units is unsafe. The specimen is still valid, so a new draw is not needed just to repeat the test.
  35. The correct answer is B (Identify the antibody from serum and eluate, then crossmatch antigen-negative units by antiglobulin technique): A positive DAT with a new screen reaction after recent transfusion points to an alloantibody coating donor cells, so the antibody is identified from the serum and from an eluate. Units lacking the corresponding antigen are then crossmatched by the antiglobulin method. ABO matching alone, waiting, or assuming an autoantibody would risk further destruction.
  36. The correct answer is D (22 units, because 12 units of expected use plus the 10 unit safety stock equals 22): Expected use during the delivery wait is 6 units per day for 2 days, which is 12 units. Adding the safety stock of 10 units gives a par level of 22. Omitting the delivery time, doubling for weekends or counting safety stock as part of daily use each produce a different, incorrect figure.
  37. The correct answer is A (Collect a new sample, because the mismatch suggests this tube came from another patient): The current tube types as group A while the record on file is group O, and both patterns are internally consistent, which points to a different patient's blood rather than a reagent or incubation problem. Repeating tests on the same tube cannot correct a mislabelled specimen. A new, correctly collected sample is needed before any units are issued.
  38. The correct answer is C (Issue a corrected report showing original and revised results, and notify the physician): A released result that is wrong must be corrected openly so the original, the revision, the time and the person responsible are traceable. The physician must be told because an Rh type can guide product selection. Silent deletion or overwriting destroys the audit trail.
  39. The correct answer is C (Disclosure to the patient is expected, led by the responsible clinician with laboratory support): Canadian disclosure guidance expects patients to be told about events that reach them, even without harm. The responsible clinician leads the conversation and the laboratory supplies accurate facts. Leaving it to lawyers or to one technologist does not meet that expectation.
  40. The correct answer is D (Delay washing until shortly before 19:00 so the four hour expiry covers the transfusion): The four hour expiry starts when washing ends, and the unit is held at room temperature, so washing should be timed to finish shortly before the patient is ready. Washing early would leave the platelets expired by 19:00, and refrigeration would damage them. Substituting regular platelets ignores the physician's request for a patient with repeated severe reactions.
  41. The correct answer is C (Cells lack Duffy antigens and resist Plasmodium vivax, because Duffy glycoprotein is the parasite receptor): The Duffy glycoprotein serves as the receptor used by Plasmodium vivax, so cells lacking it resist invasion. Patients with this phenotype can still form anti-Fya or anti-Fyb if exposed to Duffy positive cells. The protective effect is specific to vivax, not falciparum.
  42. The correct answer is B (Speed and timer verification with calibrated devices at the interval the manufacturer specifies): Gel card centrifuges run fixed manufacturer programs, so the laboratory verifies that speed and timer perform as specified, using calibrated devices at the stated interval. Technologists must not change the speed to fit one result. Comparing reaction scores or cleaning the rotor does not show that speed and time are correct.
  43. The correct answer is D (444 mL, because net blood weight 468 g is divided by density): Blood weight is the gross weight minus the empty bag set, which gives 468 g. Dividing by 1.053 g/mL gives about 444 mL. Multiplying, using the gross weight or treating one gram as one millilitre produce volumes that do not represent what was collected.
  44. The correct answer is D (Two inactive H genes leave no H substance, so A and B transferases cannot add sugars): In a Bombay person both H genes are inactive, so H substance is not made on the red cells. The A and B transferases need H substance as the sugar to build on, so even a person who inherits an A or B gene shows no A or B antigen and types as group O. True group O people make normal H substance. The O gene itself produces no active transferase, not an enzyme that changes H.
  45. The correct answer is A (Confirm the number in the provider's registered record, then send with a cover sheet): Faxing to an unverified number is a common cause of misdirected results. The recipient and number must match the registered provider or an agreed contact, and a confidentiality cover sheet limits harm if the fax still goes astray. Speaking a result aloud is also a disclosure, and trimming the report does not remove the patient identifiers.
  46. The correct answer is C (Collect cord blood in EDTA after cleaning the cord, because maternal blood can cause errors): Cord blood is usually drawn into EDTA from a cleaned cord so that the infant red cells can be typed and tested by the direct antiglobulin test. Wharton's jelly and maternal blood can produce false agglutination or mixed results. Maternal blood does not reveal the newborn's red cell antigens.
  47. The correct answer is B (The unit's donation number and component code, with the time the transfusion started and stopped): The supplier needs to find the exact donation and component, so the donation number and component code are essential, along with the transfusion timing to link the reaction to the unit. Contact numbers, equipment brands and billing numbers do not identify the product.
  48. The correct answer is C (Saline replacement, because removing the plasma proteins disperses rouleaux while true agglutination remains): Rouleaux are stacks of red cells caused by raised plasma proteins, as in multiple myeloma. Replacing the plasma with saline removes the proteins, so rouleaux disperse while true agglutination remains. Cold incubation, extra plasma or warming does not reliably remove the protein effect.
  49. The correct answer is B (The target stock level set from usage and delivery time, so orders refill to it): A par level is the target quantity of a product to hold, calculated from typical use and how long a delivery takes. Orders are placed to bring stock back up to it, which is more responsive than a fixed monthly order. Capacity, cost and the previous month's issues may inform the number but do not define it.
  50. The correct answer is B (3+, because several large clumps with a clear background are not a single solid clump): Several large clumps with a clear background is a 3+ reaction. A 4+ reaction is one solid agglutinate with a clear background, while 2+ shows medium clumps and 1+ shows small clumps in a cloudy background. Using the same grading definitions across staff keeps results consistent.
  51. The correct answer is A (Keep the ratio, because excess serum raises ionic strength and reduces the LISS benefit): The LISS method depends on a low ionic strength, which the validated serum to additive to cell ratio preserves. Extra serum raises the salt content of the mixture and weakens the enhancement, so reactions may get weaker rather than stronger. Procedure ratios should be followed exactly.
  52. The correct answer is C (Remove the pipette from service and arrange adjustment, because the delivered volume is outside the limit): Delivered volume is 43.2 uL (0.0432 g equals 43.2 mg), which is 6.8 uL below target and well outside plus or minus 2 uL. The pipette is withdrawn and adjusted by a qualified person, then rechecked. Compensating at the bench or waiting for the annual service leaves inaccurate volumes in patient tests.
  53. The correct answer is B (Label it Rh positive, because the weak D test shows D antigen is present): Donor red cells carrying weak D antigen can immunize D negative recipients, so a donor unit that reacts at the antiglobulin phase of the weak D test is labelled Rh positive. The negative immediate spin result does not rule out weak D, and a valid control means the positive reaction is not a false result.
Case CHB13

A 31-year-old woman of South Asian descent is admitted for elective surgery. Her red cells show no agglutination with anti-A or anti-B but agglutinate 4+ with anti-D. In the reverse group, A1 cells, B cells and group O reagent cells all agglutinate 4+. The antibody screen is positive for all three screening cells at 37 C, and the autocontrol is negative. Anti-H lectin does not agglutinate her red cells.

  1. The correct answer is D (Bombay phenotype, because her cells lack H antigen and her serum holds anti-A, anti-B and anti-H): Her cells carry no A, B or H antigen, so they type as group O on forward grouping, yet the O reagent cells react in her serum because she makes anti-H in addition to anti-A and anti-B. The negative lectin test confirms that H antigen is absent. Ordinary group O people have abundant H antigen and make no anti-H.
  2. The correct answer is C (Red cells from a Bombay donor, because her anti-H reacts with H antigen on other cells): Anti-H made by a Bombay patient reacts with the H antigen found on red cells of every other phenotype, and it is active at 37 C, so only blood from another Bombay (Oh) donor is safe. Group O cells carry the most H antigen of any common type. Group A and AB cells carry A or B antigen that her anti-A and anti-B would also attack.
  3. The correct answer is A (Test the sister's cells with anti-H lectin and crossmatch, because only H-negative cells are compatible): A Bombay type is inherited as a recessive trait, so a sister has only some chance of sharing it, and a routine forward group would call a Bombay donor group O just as it did for the patient. Only anti-H lectin testing and a compatible crossmatch show whether the sister's cells lack H antigen. Ordinary group O cells would be destroyed by the patient's anti-H.
  4. The correct answer is A (Sixteen more days, because 56 minus 40 leaves 16 days before the interval ends): The minimum interval is 56 days and 40 have passed, so 16 days remain. A good hemoglobin does not shorten an interval that exists to protect iron stores, and adding the numbers or counting only whole weeks gives wrong totals.
  5. The correct answer is B (Quarantine the unit and tell the supervisor, because bag and segment identity cannot be confirmed): The segments are used for compatibility testing, so they must be proven to come from the same donation as the bag. Any mismatch means the identity of the unit is uncertain, and the unit must be quarantined and investigated with the supplier. Relabelling or issuing with a note would risk transfusing a unit that was not properly tested.
  6. The correct answer is C (Reject the sample and request a recollection, because identity cannot be verified for dosing): A sample with incomplete labelling cannot be accepted because results and the immune globulin dose could be linked to the wrong patient. Handwriting identifiers after the draw or repeating tests on the same tube does not restore the link between patient and sample. A properly labelled sample must be collected again.
  7. The correct answer is D (Treat the history as recent and apply the three day limit to the specimen): When the transfusion or pregnancy history cannot be confirmed, the safer course is to assume it was recent and use the short three day limit. Assuming a negative history could let an older sample hide a newly formed antibody. Waiting for records delays care, and another hospital's date does not describe this specimen.
  8. The correct answer is C (Phone the critical titre to the physician and document the call so Doppler can begin): A titre at the laboratory's critical level is a result needing prompt communication so the obstetrician can arrange middle cerebral artery Doppler studies. Delaying for confirmation or routine reporting risks missing fetal anemia, and a fetal bleed screen is for Rh immune globulin dosing in D-negative mothers, not for tracking an existing antibody.
  9. The correct answer is C (Confirm the transplant history, recollect to exclude a sample error, then document the changed group): A group change is a discrepancy until proven otherwise, so the laboratory first confirms the transplant history and recollects to exclude a mislabelled sample. Only then is the new group documented with a transplant alert. Overwriting immediately risks an error, and keeping group A would be unsafe for a recipient now producing group O cells.

Practice Test 16 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Wash well and use anti-IgG, because polyspecific AHG may give false positives with PEG): PEG can cause nonspecific protein aggregation, so the cells must be washed thoroughly and anti-IgG reagent is used rather than polyspecific AHG to avoid false positives. Skipping the wash leaves free proteins to neutralize or confuse the antiglobulin step. Heating to 56 C would damage reagents and is not part of a PEG procedure.
  2. The correct answer is D (The reagent or technique is faulty, so the run is invalid until it is resolved): Group A cells carry no B antigen, so anti-B reagent should give no agglutination. A positive negative control means the reagent is contaminated or deteriorated, or the technique is wrong, so patient grouping cannot be trusted until the cause is corrected and the control repeated.
  3. The correct answer is D (To identify trends and system weaknesses, because shared reports allow changes that prevent later harm): Hemovigilance is a learning system: collecting reactions and near misses lets the transfusion service and the supplier see patterns and fix process weaknesses before more patients are harmed. It is not designed to assign blame, and non-fatal events and near misses provide the largest share of the useful information.
  4. The correct answer is B (Report the incident to the privacy officer, because possible exposure must be assessed and documented): A possible exposure of personal health information is handled through the privacy officer, who decides whether notification of the patient is required. Staying silent removes the chance to assess harm and meet legal duties. Contacting the patient before the organization has a plan can create further problems.
  5. The correct answer is B (Notify the supervisor and prepare the sample for referral, because the procedure requires it): Escalation procedures name who must be told about unusual results and how referral is arranged. Waiting for a batch delays care for a pregnant patient with a possible clinically significant antibody. Reporting an unsupported specificity or hiding the finding behind repeats bypasses the required review.
  6. The correct answer is C (Report the injury and go to occupational health now, because prophylaxis works best within hours): Exposure management is time sensitive, since any HIV prophylaxis is most effective within hours, so the injury is reported and assessed immediately. Source patient results can guide later decisions but treatment is not delayed to wait for them. Waiting for symptoms is unsafe because bloodborne infections are often silent at first, and an unreported injury also loses follow up and compensation rights.
  7. The correct answer is D (Hemolysis is a positive reaction, so investigate the patient's plasma for a complement-binding antibody): Hemolysis of reagent cells in a reverse grouping tube is a positive reaction. It shows that an antibody and complement lysed the reagent cells, even though no clumps are visible. Because the original plasma was not hemolyzed, the pink supernatant comes from the test, so the finding should be investigated and not ignored.
  8. The correct answer is A (The donation number and component type of each unit, because they identify the donor product): The supplier traces a product through its donation number and component type, so these identifiers are needed on the report. Patient contact details, bed location and staff signatures do not help trace the product to its donor, and patient identifiers should not be sent when they are not needed.
  9. The correct answer is C (Solid phase adherence, where positive reactions spread red cells as a layer over the well): In solid phase testing, antigen or antibody is fixed to the well surface. After incubation and washing, red cells that have bound spread across the well as a layer, while unbound cells form a compact button in the centre. Gel testing uses a sieving column and tube testing uses a spun button, so neither matches this description.
  10. The correct answer is B (Notify the physician promptly, because the titre rose three dilutions and exceeds policy): A titre moving from 8 to 64 is a rise of three doubling dilutions, which exceeds the two dilution change accepted as significant and also crosses the laboratory critical titre of 32. Because the samples were already tested together, repeating them delays notification. The physician needs the comparison to plan fetal monitoring.
  11. The correct answer is C (Decline the unit, because group B red cells are incompatible with group A plasma): Group A plasma contains anti-B, so group B red cells would be attacked and could cause an acute hemolytic reaction. Directed units must meet the same ABO and Rh compatibility rules as any other unit, and washing or relabelling cannot change the antigens on the red cells.
  12. The correct answer is D (Administration to the patient is documented, with start time, patient identifiers and the unit number): A unit is counted as transfused only when administration is documented with the patient identifiers, the unit number and the start time. Issue, a compatible crossmatch or silence from the ward shows the unit moved or was tested, not that it was given.
  13. The correct answer is D (Stop the issue and hold the unit, then investigate the mismatch with a supervisor): A group A unit is not compatible with a group O recipient's plasma, so the mismatch must stop the issue and be investigated before any entry is made. Changing the label or the record would hide an error, and the compatibility record cannot override the label on the unit.
  14. The correct answer is B (Perform a saline replacement technique, which disperses the rouleaux clumps while true agglutination stays intact): In saline replacement, serum is removed after centrifugation and replaced with saline. Rouleaux, which is stacking of red cells caused by high plasma protein, disperses in saline while true agglutination remains. Albumin and extra serum add protein and make rouleaux worse, and warming does not reliably separate the two.
  15. The correct answer is B (Do not issue RhIG, because she is already immunized and the antibody is active): A titre of 1:64 with no history of RhIG indicates active immunization, not passive antibody. RhIG only prevents the primary immune response and cannot reverse sensitization, so a dose would be of no benefit. The infant should be monitored for hemolytic disease instead, and dose calculation is not needed.
  16. The correct answer is A (Report the error and recall the unit, because patient safety needs early correction): An error found after issue but before transfusion can still be prevented from reaching the patient if the unit is recalled and rechecked. Silent correction hides the problem and may leave an incompatible unit in use. Visual inspection of a bag cannot show serologic compatibility.
  17. The correct answer is B (The enzyme treatment failed, so the panel is invalid until fresh treated cells are prepared): Fya is destroyed by ficin, so the control with anti-Fya should lose most or all reactivity with treated Fy(a+) cells. A 3+ reaction identical to the untreated cells shows the enzyme step did not work. The panel results cannot be trusted until a fresh batch of treated cells passes the control.
  18. The correct answer is B (Titre rose from 16 to 32, one dilution, which is not a significant change): The earlier sample gives 1+ at 1:16, so the titre is 16, and the current sample gives 1+ at 1:32, so the titre is 32. A single dilution change is within normal test variation and is not a significant rise, which requires two or more dilutions. The negative control rows show the test was valid.
  19. The correct answer is A (An anamnestic antibody response to a donor antigen, causing extravascular destruction of transfused red cells): In an immunized patient, an antibody too low to detect before transfusion can rise rapidly within days of re-exposure to the antigen on donor cells, coating them and clearing them in the spleen and liver. ABO reactions occur within minutes, passive plasma antibodies are minor, and contamination reactions present with fever and shock.
  20. The correct answer is D (Decline to share the flag and refer the caller to the treating team or the patient): Health information is released only to people the law and the patient's consent allow. A caller on the phone cannot be verified as an authorized person, so the technologist should not disclose anything and should point the caller to the care team. Family relationship alone does not give a right to the chart.
  21. The correct answer is C (Keep platelets at 20 to 24 C with gentle agitation, because cold exposure damages them): Platelets are stored at 20 to 24 C with continuous gentle agitation because cold causes them to change shape and be cleared quickly after transfusion. Refrigerating or icing them damages function even though red cells need the cold. Freezing is used for plasma, not for platelet concentrates.
  22. The correct answer is C (Febrile non-hemolytic reaction, a diagnosis of exclusion often tied to cytokines from donor white cells): A temperature rise of at least 1 C to 38 C or more with chills, without hypotension, hemolysis or other organ signs, fits a febrile non-hemolytic reaction once more serious causes are excluded. Allergic reactions bring hives and itch, lung injury brings hypoxemia, and delayed hemolysis appears days later.
  23. The correct answer is D (Perform a saline replacement, because rouleaux disperse when saline is added and true agglutination remains): Rouleaux are stacks of red cells held together by high serum protein and look like agglutination in tube tests. Replacing the serum with saline after spinning disperses the stacks but leaves true antibody-coated clumps intact. Warming, washing the reagent cells or adding albumin does not separate protein-driven stacking and may strengthen it.
  24. The correct answer is B (They lack A and B antigens but carry H substance, the precursor for both): Group O red cells have no A or B antigen but carry H substance, the base sugar that A and B transferases modify in other groups. The O gene produces no active transferase, so H stays unchanged. Group O is not named for a separate O antigen.
  25. The correct answer is B (Add IgG check cells to a negative test, because they must agglutinate if washing worked): IgG sensitized check cells show whether the antiglobulin reagent was still active in a negative test. If washing left free IgG behind, the reagent is neutralized and the check cells fail to agglutinate. Measuring volume or time does not prove proteins were removed, and a weak anti-D control tests reagent sensitivity rather than washing.
  26. The correct answer is D (They confirm that active AHG was added and not neutralized, so the negative result is valid): Check cells are IgG-sensitized group O cells added to negative AHG tubes after reading. If AHG is active and was not neutralized by unbound globulin, it will agglutinate the check cells. A negative screen is therefore valid only when the check cells react. They do not prove the patient has no antibody, and they do not detect complement on the patient's own cells.
  27. The correct answer is C (13:15 on the same day, and the pool is acceptable at issue): Open-system pooling starts the expiry clock at the time of pooling, so 09:15 plus four hours gives 13:15. At 12:40 the pool is still within that window. Counting from issue or from the original thaw time would give the wrong label time.
  28. The correct answer is B (14:30 on Wednesday, which is 24 hours after the recorded time of thawing): The policy counts 24 hours from the moment of thawing, so the expiry is the same clock time the next day: 14:30 on Wednesday. Rounding to a calendar day boundary or a shift change changes the allowed storage time. Two days applies only to policies that assign a longer thawed plasma expiry, which this one does not.
  29. The correct answer is A (Fetal hemoglobin resists acid elution while adult hemoglobin is removed from maternal cells): Hemoglobin F is resistant to elution at low pH, so fetal cells keep their hemoglobin and stain dark pink. Adult hemoglobin A is washed out of maternal cells, leaving pale ghost cells. The stain is not directed at D antigen or cell size, and maternal hemoglobin does not mask fetal cells.
  30. The correct answer is A (To detect ABO incompatibility, because IgM antibodies in patient plasma agglutinate donor cells quickly): The immediate spin crossmatch is a final check for ABO incompatibility, because IgM anti-A and anti-B agglutinate incompatible cells during centrifugation without incubation. It does not detect most IgG antibodies, which need 37 C incubation and the antiglobulin phase. Donor plasma is not the test serum, and Rh type is confirmed by separate testing.
  31. The correct answer is D (Trace the remaining units through issue and transfusion records and report the count): The plateau at 4 units shows the remaining units are not in inventory, so the search should move to issue, transfer and transfusion records. A recall is not complete until every named unit has a documented fate, and the supplier must be told about any unresolved units. Writing the units off as expired without evidence would falsify the record.
  32. The correct answer is B (Unit one is compatible, while unit two is incompatible and needs investigation): A crossmatch is compatible only when no phase shows agglutination or hemolysis. Unit one shows none, so it is compatible. A 2+ antiglobulin reaction on unit two with a negative antibody screen and autocontrol suggests an antibody to a low prevalence antigen on that unit, so it is incompatible and must be investigated.
  33. The correct answer is D (Group A, because prewarmed reverse grouping agrees with the forward grouping and clears cold reactivity): Prewarming removes the cold autoantibody reactivity, so the reverse grouping now matches the forward grouping: anti-B only, as expected for group A plasma. The room temperature reactions were caused by the cold autoantibody and not by a true ABO discrepancy. A repeat that resolves the problem allows the group to be reported.
  34. The correct answer is A (Flag the record and issue none of the refused components, since the clinician manages consent): The treating clinician is responsible for obtaining and documenting consent or refusal. The laboratory supports the patient's decision by flagging the record so refused components are not issued. Testing for other purposes can continue, and the laboratory does not run the consent process.
  35. The correct answer is C (Place it directly into the sharps container, because setting it down invites later injury): A used sharp should go straight into a puncture resistant, closable sharps container at the point of use. Putting it on a tray leaves an exposed contaminated sharp that someone can touch or knock. Recapping and wrapping a small lancet in paper towel do not make it safe for ordinary waste, and both create handling injuries.
  36. The correct answer is C (Pregnancy lowers plasma Lewis substance, and red cells only adsorb the antigens from plasma): Lewis antigens are made in plasma and adsorbed onto red cells, so a fall in plasma Lewis substance during pregnancy lowers the antigen on the cells. The change is temporary and the woman does not necessarily make an antibody. A negative screen argues against antibody blocking.
  37. The correct answer is A (12 percent, because 90 divided by 750 equals 0.12 of the thawed units): Wastage is discarded units divided by units thawed, so 90 divided by 750 equals 0.12, or 12 percent. Using a larger denominator lowers the rate, and using the transfused fraction reports the wrong quantity. A decimal slip understates a serious wastage problem by a factor of ten.
  38. The correct answer is C (Keep the flag and provide antigen-negative units, because antibody levels can fall below detection): Kidd antibodies are well known for falling below detectable levels and then returning rapidly after re-exposure, causing delayed hemolytic reactions. Flags for clinically significant antibodies therefore remain on the record. A negative screen or a signature does not make the history safe to discard.
  39. The correct answer is C (A positive DAT or ABO discrepancy on the post-reaction sample showing incompatible donor red cells): Immune destruction of incompatible donor cells shows up as a positive DAT or a mixed field or discrepant ABO type on the post-reaction sample, supported by pink plasma. Fever height and white cell counts overlap between reaction types, and a bacterial culture addresses contamination rather than hemolysis.
  40. The correct answer is B (Do not return it to inventory, because 36 minutes exceeds the 30 minute limit): The unit was out of controlled storage for 36 minutes, which is longer than the 30 minutes allowed by the stated policy, so its temperature cannot be assured and it cannot go back into inventory. The unit is handled as a nonconforming product under the quality procedure. Visual inspection or warming does not demonstrate that the unit stayed within range.
  41. The correct answer is B (It is a detected deviation, and recording it allows causes and trends to be reviewed): Any departure from the written procedure is a deviation, even when it is caught and contained. Recording it allows the laboratory to review causes and look for repeated events. A label note, a reaction report or a supplier record would not capture the process failure.
  42. The correct answer is A (Remove it and use the controlled current copy, because uncontrolled copies may be outdated): Only controlled copies carrying a version, effective date and approval are used for testing. An uncontrolled sheet cannot be verified as current, so it is removed and the controlled version is followed. Adding a date or initials does not make a copy controlled.
  43. The correct answer is C (Close and replace the container now, because overfilling puts hands near exposed needle tips): Sharps containers must be closed and replaced at the fill line, since crowding the opening puts fingers within reach of exposed points. Pushing contents down or handling loose needles are common causes of injury. Delaying replacement keeps the hazard in a busy work area.
  44. The correct answer is D (Add IgG coated control cells and recentrifuge, because agglutination proves the reagent remained active): Check cells coated with IgG are added to a negative DAT to show that the antiglobulin reagent still works after the washes. If agglutination appears, the negative result is valid. Adding patient cells or warming the tube does not test the reagent, and a negative result is not reported before that check.
  45. The correct answer is C (At least 25 Gy to the central plane, with no part receiving below 15 Gy): The accepted standard is a minimum of 25 Gy delivered to the central plane of the container, with no part of the component below 15 Gy. Lower doses may not inactivate donor lymphocytes, and higher doses are not required. Leukoreduction lowers lymphocyte numbers but is not a substitute for irradiation.
  46. The correct answer is D (Mixed field from transfused group O cells, so check history and use group O): Group O red cells given a short time ago circulate with the patient own group A cells, so anti-A clumps only the patient cells and leaves the transfused cells free, which gives mixed field. The finding fits the history and does not change the true group. Until the true group is confirmed from a pretransfusion sample or two independent specimens, group O red cells remain the safe selection.
  47. The correct answer is A (Proceed and record the icteric appearance, because bilirubin does not interfere with agglutination reactions): Bilirubin colours the plasma but does not block antigen sites or change agglutination, so clear reactions can be read and reported with a comment. A specific bilirubin cut-off for rejection does not exist in serology. Yellow colour says nothing about immunoglobulin binding.
  48. The correct answer is C (Wrong patient identification at the draw, because HLA type does not change in a person): A person's HLA type is inherited and stays the same unless a transplant has occurred, so a mismatch points to a mislabelled or wrong-patient sample. Relying on a room number is not a valid identification check. Platelet transfusion and heparin do not change the recipient's inherited type.
  49. The correct answer is C (Clerical check of sample and unit identifiers, because a mix-up explains many incompatibilities): Clerical and labelling errors, such as a wrong sample or a wrongly labelled unit, are common and quickly checked, so they come before further serology. Enzyme panels and titration are later investigation steps. A hospital laboratory can investigate an incompatible crossmatch rather than simply returning the unit.
  50. The correct answer is D (Say results can be shared only with the patient's consent and suggest he ask her): An emergency contact is someone to call in an emergency, not an authorized recipient of test results. Without the patient's consent the technologist should decline to discuss any part of the result and let the patient decide. Proving the husband's identity does not give him a right to her information, and a general description is still a disclosure.
  51. The correct answer is C (It gives a continuous record of temperature, showing excursions that happened between manual readings): A chart recorder draws a continuous trace, so staff can see how the temperature behaved overnight or over a weekend and whether it left the 1 to 6 C range between manual checks. It does not control cooling or sound the alarm, which are separate systems. It is also not adjusted to match the thermometer, since agreement between the two is checked as part of verification.
  52. The correct answer is B (Circulating donor cells could adsorb an alloantibody, which would remove it and hide it): A patient transfused in the last three months may have donor red cells still circulating. If an alloantibody were present, those donor cells in the autoadsorption mixture could take it up, so the underlying alloantibody could be removed along with the autoantibody and missed. Allogeneic adsorption with selected reagent cells of known phenotype avoids this risk.
  53. The correct answer is C (Discard and record why, because 43 minutes out of storage exceeds the 30 minute limit): Time out of storage is calculated from issue at 14:05 to return at 14:48, which is 43 minutes and beyond the 30 minute limit in the policy. A cooler temperature within range does not offset the time criterion, since both conditions must be met. Approximate or physician extended limits are not allowed by the stated policy, so the unit is discarded and the reason recorded.
  54. The correct answer is C (Use alcohol-based hand rub after removing gloves, since gloves can leak or tear unnoticed): Hand hygiene is performed after glove removal because gloves can have small defects and hands are contaminated during removal. An alcohol-based rub is effective when hands are not visibly soiled, so the distant sink is not needed. Clean looking hands can still carry organisms, and a coat or a new pair of gloves does not replace cleaning.
  55. The correct answer is B (Wash the wound with soap and water, then report it promptly, because follow-up depends on timing): Immediate washing removes contaminating material, and prompt reporting lets occupational health assess the exposure and decide on prophylaxis while it is still effective. Bleach damages tissue and does not draw anything out. Delaying the report or waiting for symptoms wastes the time window in which follow-up works best.
  56. The correct answer is A (Amend the comment to a significant rise, then notify the physician directly): A difference of more than one doubling dilution is considered significant because ordinary titration variation is about one tube. A rise from 8 to 64 is three dilutions, which suggests increasing antibody and fetal risk, so the comment is wrong and the physician must be told promptly. Deleting the earlier result would remove the evidence needed to follow the trend.
  57. The correct answer is B (Report the reaction as mixed field with a transfusion history comment, and refer for resolution): A mixed field pattern after recent transfusion usually reflects two cell populations, the patient's own and donor cells, so neither a simple positive nor negative is accurate. The report should describe the mixed field, note the transfusion and send the case for resolution under laboratory policy. Calling it contamination without evidence loses the clinical clue.
  58. The correct answer is C (Group AB plasma, because it has no anti-A or anti-B to react): Recipient group A red cells need plasma without anti-A, and donor group B red cells need plasma without anti-B. Only group AB plasma lacks both antibodies. Group A plasma contains anti-B, group B plasma contains anti-A, and group O plasma contains both, so each would attack one of the two red cell populations.
  59. The correct answer is D (Cross out the time with a single line, write the correct time and initial it): A single line keeps the original entry visible, and the collector's initials show who made the change and when, so the record stays traceable. Correction fluid hides the original information. Only the collector who witnessed the draw can correct what the label says, and the laboratory does not hold that knowledge.
  60. The correct answer is D (Give it as soon as possible within 72 hours, because primary immunization develops over days): Postpartum prophylaxis is intended to be given within 72 hours of delivery, before the mother mounts an active response to fetal D positive cells. Waiting for a positive screen would mean sensitization has already begun, and discharge date is not the governing factor.
